We first characterise short-term (5-100 token) attention degradation, finding a universal exponential-then-plateau pattern whose rate is inversely correlated with depth, with distinct layer-wise entropy signatures per architecture. Function token anchoring proves architecture-dependent: OPT-1.3B (absolute positional encoding) shows distance-dependent preposition specificity, GPT-2 shows uniform non-specific dependence, and LLaMA (RoPE) shows reversal at long distances. Strategic comma insertion at clause boundaries causally reduces prediction degradation in the 40-80 token range, with the benefit tied to syntactic boundary alignment rather than token density. We then test the mechanism causally: Relay-Aware Attention (RAA), which biases attention logits toward function token positions, verifiably increases attention mass by 16-24% yet yields null effects on GPT-2 and LLaMA-1B, preliminary harm on LLaMA-3B, and a mixed effect on OPT-1.3B that nets to approximately zero. Multi-fact retrieval probes further show that degradation rate does not predict retrieval accuracy across models. We conclude that mean attention degradation is largely descriptive rather than prescriptive: function tokens contribute through what their hidden states compute, not through the attention they receive -- with implications for interpretability methodology and attention-score-based inference optimisations such as KV-cache eviction.

</details>


### [53] [Autonomous disproofs of the sum-product conjecture over $\mathbb R$ with GPT-5.5 Pro](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20525)
*Yichen Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An AI agent using GPT-5.5 Pro autonomously disproved the Erdős–Szemerédi sum-product conjecture over ℝ in 7 out of 8 trials through a three-stage prompting pipeline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inspired by OpenAI's recent AI-assisted disproof of the Erdős unit distance conjecture, the researchers aimed to demonstrate autonomous AI capabilities in mathematical proof generation for another major conjecture.

Method: Used a simple agent built on GPT-5.5 Pro with a problem-agnostic, three-stage prompting pipeline: (1) proof-plan proposal, (2) proof construction, and (3) review stage.

Result: The agent autonomously generated correct proofs that the sum-product conjecture is false over ℝ in 7 of 8 independent trials, with diverse proof strategies. In the remaining trial, it identified an unresolved gap in its argument. The system used an average of 132.4k reasoning tokens per trial.

Conclusion: This work provides a reproducible, data-contamination-free case study demonstrating AI's ability to autonomously generate mathematical proofs for significant conjectures, potentially enabling new mathematical discoveries.

Abstract: OpenAI's recent disproof of the Erdős unit distance conjecture marked a milestone for AI in mathematics. It also inspired another breakthrough: a human disproof of the Erdős--Szemerédi sum-product conjecture over $\mathbb R$. In this paper, we present a simple agent built on GPT-5.5 Pro. Using a problem-agnostic, three-stage prompting pipeline -- proof-plan proposal, proof construction, and review -- the agent autonomously generated correct proofs that the sum-product conjecture is false over $\mathbb R$ in 7 of 8 independent trials; in the remaining trial, it identified an unresolved gap in its argument. The seven proofs are diverse: some are close to existing unit-based constructions, while others avoid units by using $L^p$-type regions of algebraic integers. The system used an average of 132.4k reasoning tokens per trial. We release the code, intermediate outputs, and generated proofs, providing a reproducible, data-contamination-free case study in autonomous proof generation.

</details>


### [54] [ConfidenceBench: Evaluating Confidence Calibration in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20526)
*Matthew ffrench-Constant,Daniel Yang,Xinmeng Huang,Sanyam Kapoor*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ConfidenceBench is a calibration benchmark for evaluating verbalized confidence estimates in LLMs using Brier scores, showing that calibration and accuracy are distinct dimensions of reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly deployed in high-stakes settings where incorrect but fluent answers can be costly, so models must know when they're likely to be wrong. Current evaluations focus on accuracy but miss calibration - how well a model's stated confidence matches its actual accuracy.

Method: Created ConfidenceBench with 200 private multiple-choice questions across four categories (spatial reasoning, high-precision mathematics, word lookup, unknowable questions). Evaluates 15 frontier LLMs using Brier score (proper scoring rule for probability reporting). Confidence is elicited via prompting without needing model logits, making it applicable to both closed and open-source systems.

Result: Claude Opus 4.6 and Gemini 3.1 Pro Preview achieve lowest Brier scores (0.103), substantially outperforming calibrated-random baseline (0.1875). Gemini 3.1 Flash-Lite scores 0.367 (severe miscalibration). Accuracy and calibration diverge: most accurate models aren't best-calibrated, some perform worse than baseline despite reasonable accuracy.

Conclusion: Verbalized confidence calibration is a distinct and practically important axis of LLM reliability that complements standard accuracy-based evaluation. Calibration matters in real-world deployments where knowing when a model is uncertain is crucial.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in settings where fluent but incorrect answers can be costly. In these settings, accuracy alone is insufficient: models must also know when they are likely to be wrong. We present ConfidenceBench, a calibration benchmark that evaluates verbalized confidence estimates in 15 frontier LLMs using the Brier score, a proper scoring rule that incentivises truthful probability reporting. Confidence is elicited via prompting, requiring no access to model logits and making the framework applicable to both closed-source and open-source systems. The benchmark comprises 200 private multiple-choice questions across four categories: spatial reasoning, high-precision mathematics, word lookup, and unknowable questions. Across three independent runs, Claude Opus 4.6 and Gemini 3.1 Pro Preview achieve the lowest Brier scores, both reported as 0.103. Both substantially outperform the calibrated-random baseline of 0.1875, while Gemini 3.1 Flash-Lite scores 0.367, indicating severe miscalibration. Accuracy and calibration diverge substantially across model families: the most accurate model is not the best-calibrated, and several models perform worse than the calibrated-random Brier baseline despite reasonable accuracy. These results show that verbalized confidence calibration is a distinct and practically important axis of LLM reliability, complementary to standard accuracy-based evaluation.

</details>


### [55] [Evaluating and Guarding Citation Faithfulness in Agentic Scientific Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20527)
*Taewan Goo,Junsik Kim,Kyulhee Han,GwonYul Jo,Jong-Soo Kim,Tae-Hyung Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Existing LLM citation verification methods are unreliable with rates varying 3-18% depending on verifier strictness. Authors propose a gold-anchored evaluation protocol and conformal guard to provide measurable, bounded reliability guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agentic LLM systems check citation support but don't audit the reliability of those checks themselves. The authors show these verification methods are unreliable, with unsupported-citation rates varying widely (3-18%) depending only on verifier strictness, making cross-paper comparisons invalid.

Method: 1) Gold-anchored evaluation protocol that validates verifiers, measures re-attribution, and calibrates against human gold standards. 2) A deployable guard using split-conformal prediction to place distribution-free, finite-sample bounds on truly unsupported citations that slip past chosen flagging rules.

Result: The protocol achieves recall of 0.94 on supported citations (held out). The guard provides concrete statistical bounds on unsupported citations, with identified condition (calibration-negative difficulty) governing transfer to deployment. Validated across four open 27-35B models and three agentic pipelines on public benchmarks.

Conclusion: Existing citation verification in LLM systems is unreliable, necessitating standardized evaluation protocols. The proposed gold-anchored protocol and conformal guard provide measurable, bounded reliability with statistical guarantees, enabling trustworthy cross-system comparison and deployment assurance.

Abstract: Agentic LLM systems such as OpenScholar and PaperQA2 read the scientific literature and return cited answers, and both they and their benchmarks already check whether those citations hold, with a fixed attribution model or human graders. Neither audits the reliability of that check itself. We show it is not reliable, and that this matters. On identical agent outputs the measured unsupported-citation rate ranges from about 3% to about 18% depending only on the verifier's strictness, and although verifiers agree on which citations are supported, they disagree on which to flag (negative-specific agreement 0.27 to 0.30), so no single flag set is trustworthy and cross-paper comparison is invalid without a named verifier and protocol.
  We present a gold-anchored evaluation protocol and a deployable guard that make this behavior measurable and bounded. The protocol validates the verifier, measures re-attribution, and calibrates a guarantee against human gold rather than another model's verdict; the verifier is a swappable instrument chosen on cost (recall 0.94 on the supported class, held out), and re-attribution is a commodity step where a deterministic BM25 matches the best open generator. The guard adds a split-conformal layer placing a distribution-free, finite-sample bound on truly unsupported citations that slip past a chosen flagging rule, a guarantee on catch rate rather than conclusion correctness. The bound holds on held-out gold, and we identify and quantify the condition governing its transfer to deployment, calibration-negative difficulty, with a concrete recalibration recipe, left untested by prior conformal-factuality work.
  Validated across four open 27-35B models and three agentic pipelines on public benchmarks (SciFact, QASA, PubMedQA), with confidence intervals on every headline number, the protocol and guard ship as an open single-GPU kit.

</details>


### [56] [PromptPack: Scaling LLM Annotation Agents for Online Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20528)
*Sebastian Koralewski,Merwan Barlier,Yulia Stolin,Blaž Škrlj*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PromptPack enables cost-effective LLM-based feature extraction for online recommendations by batching multiple ad creatives together with shared system prompts, reducing costs by 89% while maintaining performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Online recommendation platforms need LLMs to extract structured features from ad creatives, but per-creative prompting is prohibitively expensive to scale. The current single-call approach wastes 94% of billed input tokens on redundant system instructions sent with every request.

Method: PromptPack uses in-context batching with three key components: 1) a shared system prompt across multiple creatives, 2) a strict XML structural envelope for consistency, and 3) an output correction layer to ensure deterministic, pipeline-ready feature extraction across multiple creatives simultaneously.

Result: At batch size 20, PromptPack cuts LLM costs by 89% and accelerates throughput by 2.5x while fully preserving AUC performance. The system also introduces and validates Volume-Weighted Absolute Lift (VWAL), a novel metric for measuring signal quality of generated features.

Conclusion: PromptPack successfully addresses the scalability and cost challenges of LLM-based feature extraction for recommendation systems, enabling practical deployment of high-quality structured feature extraction from ad creatives at scale while maintaining or improving performance metrics.

Abstract: Online recommendation platforms increasingly use Large Language Models (LLMs) to extract structured features from ad creatives. While deploying a single-call LLM annotation agent yields significant Click-Through Rate (CTR) improvements in our live production environment, per-creative prompting is prohibitively expensive to scale. The redundant system instructions sent in every request account for 94% of billed input tokens. To break this cost bottleneck, we introduce PromptPack, a scalable, high-throughput LLM annotation agent. PromptPack achieves this scale via in-context batching, combining a shared system prompt, a strict XML structural envelope, and an output correction layer to ensure deterministic, pipeline-ready feature extraction across multiple creatives simultaneously. We evaluate PromptPack via an offline retrieval benchmark using a downstream logistic-regression ranker. To deeply profile the agent's behavior, we measure AUC and introduce Volume-Weighted Absolute Lift (VWAL), a novel metric capturing the signal quality of the generated features. Compared to our live, unbatched production baseline, PromptPack at batch size 20 cuts our LLM costs by 89% and accelerates throughput by 2.5x while fully preserving AUC.

</details>


### [57] [DynamicMCPBench: A Trace-Grounded, Effect-Scored Benchmark for LLM Agents over Live MCP Servers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20531)
*Jerzy Kamiński,Ilya Galyukshev,Artem Kuznetsov,Sergey Chuprin,Kirill Redko,Aidar Shumbalov,Anna Kalyuzhnaya*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DynamicMCPBench is a reusable framework for evaluating LLM agents on live, stateful MCP servers by generating realistic goals, recording successful trajectories, creating path-agnostic effect checkpoints, and scoring agents based on reproducing effects rather than final answers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM agent benchmarks are fragile when applied to live, stateful data because they either score only the final answer or use fixed "ground-truth" tool lists, making them unsuitable for evaluating agents deployed on dynamic Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers.

Method: The framework: 1) works with any set of MCP servers and models, 2) generates realistic goals, 3) pursues each goal live to record successful trajectories, 4) distills trajectories into path-agnostic effect checkpoints, and 5) scores agents based on whether they reproduce those effects (pass^3 scoring requiring 3 successful attempts).

Result: Large-scale evaluation of 24 models over 121 servers and 750 tasks across 15 categories shows: strongest agents solve only ~50% of tasks, 31% of tasks unsolved by any model, and accuracy collapses from 39% on shortest tool chains to 13% on longest chains. Human validation confirms scoring reliability (chance-corrected agreement 0.76).

Conclusion: DynamicMCPBench transforms benchmark construction into a reproducible process for practitioners while revealing fundamental limitations of current agents, particularly their inability to handle long, multi-step agentic tasks effectively.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly deployed over Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers, yet the benchmarks used to evaluate them score the final answer or a fixed "ground-truth" list of tools, both of which are fragile once the underlying data is live and stateful. We present DynamicMCPBench, a reusable framework rather than a fixed dataset. A practitioner can run it on their own MCP servers to test models on their own tasks, or let it collect servers automatically to measure a model's general ability to solve agentic tasks. Given the servers and any set of models, it generates realistic goals, pursues each one live to record a successful trajectory, distills that trajectory into path-agnostic effect checkpoints, and scores an agent on whether it reproduces those effects, never on the final answer. To show what the framework reveals, we run it at scale: 24 models over 121 servers and 750 tasks spread evenly over 15 task categories (50 each), where each category targets a distinct tool-use challenge of the generated questions. Each task is scored by pass^3: it counts as solved only if all three independent attempts succeed. Even the strongest agents solve only about half of the tasks, 31% of tasks are solved by no model at all, and accuracy collapses as the required tool chain grows longer (from 39% on the shortest chains to 13% on the longest). A human validation study confirms the automatic scoring is reliable (chance-corrected agreement of 0.76). DynamicMCPBench thus turns benchmark construction into something practitioners can rerun on their own servers and models, while exposing a consistent inability of current agents to handle long, multi-step agentic tasks.

</details>


### [58] [StrideDiffusion: Accelerating Diffusion Models for Time-series Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20545)
*Du Yin,Estrid He,Julián Jerónimo Bañuelos,Yang Yang,Feng Hu,Yuchen Luo,Hao Xue,Stephan Sigg,Flora Salim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: StrideDiffusion is a training-free spectral-aware sampler for time-series diffusion models that adaptively selects denoising strides based on band-level activity, achieving up to 18.9x speedup with comparable or improved quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion models require many sequential denoising steps at inference time, limiting practical use. Existing fast samplers use fixed/generic timestep schedules that overlook the unique property of time-series diffusion where different spectral bands evolve at different rates during reverse diffusion.

Method: StrideDiffusion monitors relative band energy, log-power drift, and phase velocity at each step to identify whether high-frequency dynamics remain active. It adaptively selects stride size - fine steps when rapidly varying bands are active, larger jumps when only coarse components remain. Bandwise stability analysis shows inactive frequency bands change linearly with jump size under deterministic affine reverse updates.

Result: Across six unconditional time-series generation benchmarks, StrideDiffusion uses only 14-66 function evaluations instead of 500/1000 denoising steps, achieving up to 18.9x wall-clock speedup while preserving or improving generation quality. On conditional imputation and forecasting, it delivers 5-14x average acceleration with comparable predictive accuracy.

Conclusion: Spectral evolution provides a practical and principled signal for fast time-series diffusion sampling, enabling significant acceleration without compromising quality across various generation tasks.

Abstract: Diffusion models have become competitive generators for time series, but their practical use is limited by the large number of sequential denoising steps required at inference time. Existing fast samplers typically use fixed or generic timestep schedules, overlooking a distinctive property of time-series diffusion: different spectral bands evolve at different rates during the reverse process. We introduce StrideDiffusion, a training-free spectral-aware sampler that adaptively selects the denoising stride from band-level activity. At each step, StrideDiffusion monitors relative band energy, log-power drift, and phase velocity to identify whether high- frequency dynamics remain active or whether the trajectory is dominated by stable low-frequency structure. It then takes fine steps when rapidly varying bands are active and larger jumps once only coarse components remain. A bandwise stability analysis shows that inactive frequency bands change only linearly with the jump size under deterministic affine reverse updates, providing a local justification for spectral activity as a step-size indicator. Across six unconditional time-series generation benchmarks, StrideDiffusion uses only 14-66 function evaluations instead of 500/1000 denoising steps, achieving up to 18.9x wall-clock speedup while preserving or improving generation quality. On conditional imputation and forecasting, it further delivers 5-14x average acceleration with comparable predictive accuracy. These results show that spectral evolution provides a practical and principled signal for fast time-series diffusion sampling. Our code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/stridediff-ts.

</details>


### [59] [CMI-Mem: Toward Generalizable Long-Term Memory Management via CMI-Augmented Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20553)
*Yubo Wang,Qiuyu Zhao,Zenghui Sun,Shichao Dong,Jinsong Lan,Xiaoyong Zhu,Haoyang Li,Bo Zheng,Lei Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CMI-Mem is a reinforcement learning-based memory manager model for agents that uses a hybrid reward combining downstream QA correctness and intrinsic Conditional Mutual Information, reducing dependence on synthetic queries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memory manager models rely heavily on LLM-judged synthetic QA pairs, making memory valuation dependent on sampled queries and downstream readers, which is a limitation.

Method: Proposed CMI-Mem, a lightweight RL-based memory manager with hybrid reward that combines downstream QA correctness and intrinsic Conditional Mutual Information (CMI), where CMI evaluates information contributed by new inputs relative to current memory without conditioning on sampled QA queries.

Result: Developed CMI-Mem-4B model with code available on GitHub and model checkpoint on ModelScope, demonstrating a novel approach that complements rather than replaces QA grounding.

Conclusion: CMI-Mem addresses limitations of existing methods by introducing CMI-based evaluation that reduces dependency on synthetic QA pairs while maintaining grounding through hybrid rewards.

Abstract: Memory Manager models are pivotal in agent systems. Existing methods rely predominantly on LLM-judged synthetic question-answer (QA) pairs, making memory valuation dependent on sampled queries and the downstream reader. To address this limitation, we propose \textbf{CMI-Mem}, a reinforcement learning(RL)-based lightweight memory manager model with a hybrid reward that combines downstream QA correctness and intrinsic Conditional Mutual Information (CMI). CMI evaluates the information contributed by new conversational inputs relative to the current memory state without conditioning on a sampled QA query, thereby complementing rather than replacing QA grounding. Our codes are available at: https://github.com/Wyb0627/CMIMem , and the CMI-Mem-4B model checkpoint is available at: https://www.modelscope.cn/models/wyb0627/CMIMem-4B

</details>


### [60] [AI-Driven Multi-Hop Relay Selection for Smart Urban NR-V2X Networks via Learning-to-Optimize Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20554)
*Giambattista Amati,Federica Mangiatordi,Simone Angelini,Emiliano Pallotti,Pierpaolo Salvo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A Graph Neural Network-based Learning-to-Optimize framework achieves near-optimal relay selection for NR-V2X communications with dramatically reduced computational time compared to traditional MILP approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Smart mobility in dense urban environments requires reliable, low-latency NR-V2X communications, but practical challenges like limited RSU density, frequent non-line-of-sight conditions, and highly dynamic vehicular topologies prevent stable single-hop connectivity for many CAVs. While multi-hop relay-assisted communication can extend coverage, real-time relay selection under practical constraints remains challenging.

Method: Proposes a Learning-to-Optimize (L2O) framework using Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) for real-time NR-V2X relay selection. Models vehicular communication states as attributed graphs with CAVs and RSUs as nodes, and radio links enriched with propagation-aware features. Uses an edge-aware Graph Isomorphism Network (GINE) trained with supervision from an offline MILP oracle to approximate optimal decisions with near-constant inference latency.

Result: Experiments on large-scale urban datasets generated by an integrated SUMO-GEMV2 simulation pipeline show the approach achieves connectivity comparable to the MILP oracle while reducing execution time by orders of magnitude. The framework maintains near-constant inference latency regardless of network density.

Conclusion: The proposed GNN-based L2O framework enables cost-effective enhancement of urban V2X connectivity by leveraging existing vehicular assets and supporting scalable, real-time NR-V2X operation in smart city environments, overcoming the computational limitations of traditional optimization methods.

Abstract: Reliable and low-latency NR-V2X communications are essential for smart mobility in dense urban environments. However, limited Road-Side Unit (RSU) density, frequent non-line-of-sight conditions, and highly dynamic vehicular topologies often prevent many Connected and Automated Vehicles (CAVs) from maintaining stable single-hop connectivity. Although multi-hop relay-assisted communication can extend infrastructure coverage, selecting relay links in real time under practical flow, capacity, and connectivity constraints remains challenging.
  Mixed-Integer Linear Programming (MILP) yields optimal multi-hop relay decisions, but its computational complexity scales sharply with network density, limiting real-time applicability. To address this, we propose a Learning-to-Optimise (L2O) framework based on Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) for real-time NR-V2X relay selection. Vehicular communication states are modeled as attributed graphs, where CAVs and RSUs are nodes and candidate radio links are enriched with propagation-aware features. An offline MILP oracle provides optimal supervision, while an edge-aware Graph Isomorphism Network (GINE) approximates oracle decisions with near-constant inference latency. Experiments on large-scale urban datasets generated by an integrated SUMO--GEMV2 simulation pipeline show that the proposed approach achieves connectivity comparable to that of the MILP oracle while reducing execution time by orders of magnitude. The framework enables cost-effective enhancement of urban V2X connectivity by leveraging existing vehicular assets and supporting scalable, real-time NR-V2X operation in smart city environments.

</details>


### [61] [KeySI: An Interaction Framework for Tuning Text Embeddings Based on Human Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20556)
*Yan Zhu,Y. Chen,Rebecca Faust*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: KeySI is an interaction framework that enables feature-level feedback through keyword-based concept specification for adapting embedding models, reducing the need for manual document inspection and labeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pre-trained language models often struggle to capture domain-specific semantics and require large labeled datasets and technical expertise for adaptation. Existing visual interaction methods operate on document-level feedback, forcing users to open and assess individual documents.

Method: KeySI uses keyword-based concept specification where users organize extracted keywords into groups representing concepts. This feature-level feedback is translated into document-level supervision for model tuning. The framework includes keyword curation, visualization via dimensionality reduction, interactive keyword group specification, and iterative refinement through system feedback.

Result: KeySI was evaluated through user studies, usage scenarios, and quantitative experiments, demonstrating its effectiveness in capturing user intent and improving embedding alignment while reducing manual document inspection requirements.

Conclusion: KeySI provides an effective framework for adapting embedding models through keyword-based feedback, lowering barriers to domain-specific adaptation by reducing the need for manual document labeling and technical expertise.

Abstract: In large-scale text analysis tasks, pre-trained language models are often used to embed text corpora for downstream analysis. However, such models may struggle to capture domain-specific semantics and adapting them typically requires large amounts of labeled data and technical expertise to implement training pipelines. Recent approaches have demonstrated how visual interactions in document projections can capture human feedback as training signals for model tuning. However, these methods operate on document-level feedback, which requires users to open and assess individual documents in order to provide effective feedback. In this paper, we propose KeySI, an interaction framework that enables feature-level feedback through keyword-based concept specification. Users specify feedback by organizing extracted keywords into groups representing concepts, which KeySI translates into document-level supervision for subsequent tuning. By operating on keywords as the primary interaction medium, KeySI reduces the need for manual document inspection and labeling and lowers the barrier to adapting embedding models. We present a prototype implementation that, given a corpus, curates representative keywords, visualizes keywords and document embeddings via dimensionality reduction, allows interactive specification of keyword groups, and supports iterative refinement through system feedback. We evaluate KeySI through a user study, usage scenarios, and quantitative experiments demonstrating its effectiveness in capturing user intent and improving embedding alignment.

</details>


### [62] [WaveformQA: Benchmarking LLM Temporal Reasoning on Digital Waveforms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20638)
*Yichuan Liu,Daniel Cummings,Nick Vadlamudi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WaveformQA is a new benchmark for evaluating LLMs' temporal reasoning capabilities over digital waveforms, showing current models struggle with complex temporal questions despite reasonable performance on simple queries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs have shown strong code generation and reasoning abilities, but their capacity for temporal reasoning over digital waveforms (critical for hardware design verification) remains unexplored. Existing benchmarks focus on HDL code generation and use waveforms only as supplementary context, leaving a gap in evaluating temporal reasoning capabilities.

Method: Created WaveformQA, an open-source QA benchmark with 360 programmatically generated questions across 8 difficulty categories, including multi-signal correlation and event ordering. Waveforms are generated from open-source design implementations for reproducibility. Evaluated frontier LLMs and compared event-time JSON representation vs. standard VCD format.

Result: Frontier LLMs achieve reasonable accuracy on simple queries but performance degrades on complex temporal and multi-step questions due to context window limitations and reasoning difficulties. Event-time JSON representation improves LLM reasoning accuracy compared to the standard VCD format.

Conclusion: WaveformQA addresses a critical gap in evaluating LLM temporal reasoning over digital waveforms, revealing current limitations while providing an extensible open-source framework for future research in hardware verification and temporal reasoning.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong capabilities in code generation and reasoning, yet their ability to perform temporal reasoning over digital waveform data remains largely unexplored. Although reasoning over digital waveforms is a critical bottleneck in design verification, existing benchmarks primarily evaluate hardware description language (HDL) code generation and use waveforms only as supplementary context. This paper presents WaveformQA, an open-source question-answering benchmark for evaluating LLM temporal reasoning over digital waveforms. The benchmark comprises 360 questions with programmatically generated ground truths across eight categories of varying difficulty, including questions targeting multi-signal correlation and event ordering. Waveforms are generated from open-source design implementations, ensuring reproducibility and grounding the benchmark in real hardware behavior. Evaluation of frontier LLMs reveals that while models achieve reasonable accuracy on simple queries, performance degrades due to context window limitations and reasoning difficulties on complex temporal and multi-step questions. In addition, we show that an event-time JSON representation of waveforms improves LLM reasoning accuracy versus the standardized value change dump (VCD) format. The open-source framework supports extending to new question categories and importing new waveform sources, enabling researchers to rapidly prototype temporal reasoning experiments.

</details>


### [63] [NVIDIA-labs OO Agents: Native Python Object-Oriented Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20709)
*Paul Furgale,Severin Klingler,James Nolan,Matt Staats,Gaia Di Lorenzo,Elisa Martinez Abad,Christian Schüller,Razvan Dinu,Alessio Devoto,Pascal Berard,Gal Kaplun,Elad Sarafian,Riccardo Roveri,Leon Derczynski,Ricardo Silveira Cabral*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NOOA is a Python framework where agents are Python objects with methods as actions, fields as state, docstrings as prompts, and type annotations as contracts - allowing both developers and AI models to interact with agents using the same familiar programming interface.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional agent development is fragmented across multiple components (prompt templates, tool schemas, callback code, workflow graphs), making development complex and lacking the reliability and maintainability of standard software engineering practices.

Method: Create a model-agnostic Python framework where agents are Python objects. Methods represent actions, fields represent state, docstrings serve as prompts, and type annotations act as contracts. Methods with "..." bodies are completed by LLM-driven agent loops at runtime, while normal methods remain deterministic Python code.

Result: The paper presents three contributions: (1) Agent-as-Python-object programming model with Pythonic APIs; (2) Six novel model-facing ideas combined for the first time; (3) Demonstration that current models effectively use this interface on benchmarks like SWE-bench Verified, Terminal-Bench 2.0, and ARC-AGI-3.

Conclusion: NOOA successfully bridges the gap between AI agent development and traditional software engineering by providing a unified Pythonic interface that allows agents to be tested, traced, refactored, and improved like standard software, while enabling current models to effectively use this interface for complex agentic and reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Traditional agent development is split across prompt templates, tool schemas, callback code, and workflow graphs. We present NVIDIA Object-Oriented Agents (NOOA), a model-agnostic Python framework for building reliable AI agents. NOOA takes a simpler approach: an agent is a Python object. Its methods are the actions the model can take, fields are its state, docstrings are its prompts, and its type annotations are contracts. A method whose code body consists of "..." is completed at runtime by an LLM-driven agent loop, while methods with normal bodies remain standard deterministic Python. This gives developers and agents the same interface, so agent behavior can be tested, traced, refactored, and improved just like other software.
  This paper makes three contributions. (1) We present the agent-as-a-Python-object programming model and the design principles behind it. Where Python has existing abstractions, we adopt them directly. Agent-specific capabilities--context, events, state rendering, long-term memory, and validated LLM loops--are exposed through simple Pythonic APIs, so both developers and agents share one familiar programming model. (2) We identify six model-facing ideas that NOOA is, to our knowledge, the first to combine on a single surface: typed input/output, pass-by-reference over live objects, code as action, programmable loop engineering, explicit object state, and model-callable harness APIs for context and events. We find the community already converging on several of these ideas--often as experimental or partial features--and present the comparison to encourage further adoption. (3) We demonstrate that current models use this interface effectively, both in targeted capability tests and on agentic and reasoning benchmarks such as SWE-bench Verified and Terminal-Bench 2.0 and ARC-AGI-3.

</details>


### [64] [ArbiGraph: Arbitrarily Scalable Verifiable Task Graphs for Evaluating Context Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20764)
*Pavel Golikov,Evgenii Opryshko,Gennady Pekhimenko,Mark C. Jeffrey*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ARBIGRAPH is a benchmark generator that creates complex, composable task graphs to evaluate how well tool-assisted language agents manage context across extended reasoning workflows, revealing significant performance degradation on dependent tasks not visible in single-task evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations of tool-assisted language agents focus on single, isolated tasks, which doesn't reveal how well these agents can retain, update, compose, and discard task-relevant context across extended reasoning workflows with dependencies between tasks.

Method: ARBIGRAPH generates controllable task graphs where each task is a natural-language problem with executable Python solver, composed through typed intermediate states (scalar and list values). The system allows variation in graph length, dependency structure, distractor count, and value types while preserving exact automatic verification.

Result: Evaluation of a Qwen3.5-27B tool-assisted agent shows high accuracy on isolated tasks (94-100% across categories) but substantial degradation on complex dependent tasks: accuracy drops by up to 33.3% on branching chains of dependent math tasks, and performance declines significantly on sequential chains and branching structures.

Conclusion: ARBIGRAPH successfully exposes critical failures in tool-assisted agents' context management that are not visible from single-task evaluation alone, highlighting the importance of evaluating agents on complex, dependent task workflows rather than just isolated tasks.

Abstract: We introduce ARBIGRAPH, a benchmark generator for evaluating whether tool-assisted language agents can retain, update, compose, and discard task-relevant context across extended reasoning workflows. ARBIGRAPH represents each task as a natural-language problem with an executable Python solver, and composes tasks through typed intermediate states, instantiated here as scalar and list values. This design enables controllable task graphs whose length, dependency structure, distractor count, and value type can be varied while preserving exact automatic verification. We instantiate ARBIGRAPH with math, GSM-style word-problems, and Python-tracing task categories, and evaluate a Qwen3.5-27B tool-assisted agent across four topologies. The results show high accuracy on isolated tasks but substantial degradation on more complex dependent tasks: accuracy drops by up to 33.3% on branching chains of dependent math tasks. This shows that ARBIGRAPH exposes failures that are not visible from single-task evaluation alone. Our code, generated datasets, and evaluation results are available at https://github.com/pavelgolikov/ArbiGraph.git

</details>


### [65] [The Human-AI Substitution Principle: When will you be replaced by AI in your organization?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20781)
*Bonny Banerjee,Shreya Singh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI replaces human labor based on an economic asymmetry between human skill acquisition costs and AI capability scaling, leading to abrupt workforce transitions and organizational restructuring.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI is transforming organizations, raising fundamental questions about when humans will be replaced by AI. Current models lack integration of economic, organizational, and risk factors in human-AI task allocation.

Method: Developed the Human-AI Task Allocation (HAT) analytical model that formally encodes economic asymmetry between human skill acquisition and AI capability scaling. The model incorporates risk-adjusted costs, skills, organizational depth, deployment scale, and strategic adaptation factors.

Result: Derived the Human-AI Substitution Principle that provides precise conditions for AI replacing human labor. Found that AI adoption can cause abrupt workforce transitions, create hybrid organizations, flatten managerial hierarchies, and widen spans of control. Identified middle-management as vulnerable and established skill thresholds for highly skilled workers.

Conclusion: The HAT model provides a unified theory connecting automation economics, organizational design, AI governance, and workforce planning, offering predictive insights into AI-driven organizational transformation and labor replacement patterns.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI) is rapidly transforming organizations, raising a fundamental organizational and economic question: when will a human employee be replaced by AI? We present an analytical model for studying Human--AI Task Allocation (HAT) in hierarchical organizations. A central feature of the HAT model is that it formally encodes the economic asymmetry between human skill acquisition and AI capability scaling. The HAT model allows us to derive how risk-adjusted costs, skills, organizational depth, deployment scale, strategic adaptation, and risk jointly determine when, where, why, and under what structural conditions human--AI replacement occurs. A key result is the Human--AI Substitution Principle, which provides a precise condition --- grounded in the formal asymmetry assumption --- under which AI replaces human labor. Building on this result, we show that AI adoption can produce abrupt workforce transitions, hybrid human--AI organizations, including cases where risk heterogeneity sustains human and AI roles without requiring a minimum-human-fraction constraint, and flatter managerial hierarchies with wider spans of control. The HAT model identifies structural conditions under which middle-management roles exhibit elevated vulnerability to automation, and shows that the vulnerability of highly skilled workers depends on a skill threshold shaped by organizational depth, baseline costs, and risk differentials. More broadly, the paper connects automation economics, organizational design, AI governance, and workforce planning into a unified theory of AI-driven organizational transformation.

</details>


### [66] [Refusal-Gated Decoding: Preserving Refusal Behavior Under High-Temperature Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20791)
*Phillip Howard,Xin Su,Allen Roush,Manikandan Ravikiran,Amir Abdullah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: High-temperature sampling increases LLM diversity but weakens safety guardrails; this paper proposes an efficient sequential decoding approach that preserves refusal behavior at high temperatures without compromising diversity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-temperature sampling is widely used to increase diversity in LLM outputs, but it reduces the model's refusal response to harmful prompts, creating a safety gap. There's no existing solution to maintain refusal behavior in high-entropy regimes despite the importance of model safety.

Method: Systematically study how temperature influences refusal behavior in LLMs and propose an efficient sequential decoding approach that preserves greedy decoding refusal responses at high temperatures with minimal additional latency.

Result: The approach preserves 91-99% of greedy decoding refusal behavior across three benchmark datasets without compromising the model's high-temperature response for safe prompts.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that refusal behavior can be efficiently maintained for applications requiring high-temperature sampling, bridging the gap between diversity and safety in LLM decoding.

Abstract: High-temperature sampling is one of the primary mechanisms for increasing diversity in LLMs. Recent advances in truncation-based sampling techniques have helped mitigate drawbacks of high-temperature sampling such as neural text degeneration, thereby enabling greater diversity in LLM outputs without sacrificing coherence. However, increasing the entropy of the token probability distribution via high temperatures has also been shown to weaken model guardrails by reducing the model's refusal response in the presence of harmful prompts. Despite the potential benefits of high-temperature sampling and the importance of maintaining model safety, there is a lack of existing solutions for maintaining the refusal behavior of LLMs under a higher entropy regime. To address this gap, we systematically study how temperature influences refusal behavior in LLMs and propose an efficient sequential decoding approach which preserves a model's greedy decoding refusal response at high temperatures while incurring minimal additional latency. Through extensive experiments, we show that our approach preserves 91-99% of the greedy decoding refusal behavior across three benchmark datasets without compromising the model's high-temperature response for safe prompts. Our work demonstrates how refusal behavior can be maintained in an efficient manner for applications which require high-temperature sampling.

</details>


### [67] [Can an AI System Be Creative? A Critical Perspective from Art and Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20796)
*Ivan Magrin-Chagnolleau*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI systems are fundamentally incapable of true creativity in its strongest sense, particularly transformational creativity, though they can collaborate with humans in generative ways.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To examine whether AI systems can be genuinely creative by analyzing their capabilities through Margaret Boden's framework of creativity properties and processes, combining perspectives from both technical AI expertise and artistic experience.

Method: Uses Margaret Boden's framework of creativity (novelty, surprise, value) and three creative processes (combinatorial, exploratory, transformational), analyzing AI capabilities through both technical and artistic perspectives. The paper itself demonstrates human-AI collaborative writing.

Result: AI systems exhibit genuine capability in combinatorial creativity, are significantly bounded in exploratory creativity, and are fundamentally incapable of transformational creativity. Their key limitations include absence of serendipity/accident mechanisms and subject position to recognize chance events.

Conclusion: AI systems are structurally incapable of creativity in its strongest sense, but realistic and generative human-AI creative collaboration is possible and demonstrated through concrete experiments and the paper's own composition method.

Abstract: This paper examines the question of whether artificial intelligence (AI) systems can be creative, approached from the dual perspective of a researcher trained in electrical engineering, pattern recognition, machine learning, and neural networks, who has also spent most of his life engaged in the arts as actor, stage and film director, writer, composer, and visual artist, and in philosophy. Drawing on Margaret Boden's foundational framework, both her three properties of creativity (novelty, surprise, and value) and her three types of creative processes (combinatorial, exploratory, and transformational), the paper argues that AI systems are structurally incapable of creativity in its strongest sense. While they exhibit genuine capability in the domain of combinatorial creativity, they are significantly bounded in exploratory creativity, and fundamentally incapable of transformational creativity. The paper further argues that the most important limitation of current AI systems is not the absence of novelty per se, but the absence of any mechanism for serendipity, accident, or the unexpected, all of which play a central role in the phenomenology of creativity, and the absence of any subject position from which to recognize and welcome such chance events. The paper concludes by proposing a model of human, AI creative collaboration that is both realistic and generative, illustrated by several concrete experiments. The paper is itself a demonstration of the thesis it advances: it was composed through a deliberate human AI collaborative process, which is described in the methodological note that opens it.

</details>


### [68] [Profiling Lightweight Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20806)
*Tomohiro Harada,Enrique Alba,Gabriel Luque*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PTME framework enables precision-aware profiling of lightweight LLMs by jointly measuring Precision, Time, Memory, and Energy to guide better model selection for resource-constrained environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations of LLM efficiency rely on proxy metrics like parameter count or FLOPs that are decoupled from task precision, failing to capture practical usability in resource-constrained edge and mobile environments where energy consumption, execution time, and memory usage directly affect deployment.

Method: Introduced a PTME-based experimental framework for precision-aware profiling that jointly measures Precision, execution Time, peak Memory usage, and Energy consumption through direct hardware-level measurements. Applied this methodology to a representative set of lightweight LLMs executed locally under edge-class resource envelopes on a controlled desktop platform, using benchmarks spanning code generation, mathematical reasoning, and multi-task understanding.

Result: Static proxy descriptors approximate inference cost but fail to predict precision. Tightening resource envelopes increases cost without affecting precision, amplifying execution time more than energy and penalizing larger models most. No single model dominates across all PTME dimensions, and Pareto analysis reveals non-dominated configurations hidden by accuracy-only or efficiency-only assessments.

Conclusion: Selecting lightweight LLMs by size, FLOPs, latency, or accuracy alone can lead to suboptimal deployment choices. PTME profiling exposes configurations that preserve useful accuracy at lower physical cost, providing practical guidance for model selection under different resource envelopes in edge and mobile environments.

Abstract: Lightweight large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being deployed locally on personal computers and are expected to play a growing role in resource-constrained edge and mobile environments. In such settings, energy consumption, execution time, and memory usage directly affect practical usability, yet existing evaluations of LLM efficiency largely rely on proxy descriptors such as parameter count or FLOPs, often decoupled from task precision. This paper introduces a PTME-based experimental framework for the precision-aware profiling of lightweight LLM inference, jointly measuring Precision, execution Time, peak Memory usage, and Energy consumption through direct hardware-level measurements. The methodology is applied to a representative set of lightweight LLMs executed locally under edge-class resource envelopes on a controlled desktop platform, using benchmarks spanning code generation, mathematical reasoning, and multi-task understanding. We find that static proxy descriptors approximate inference cost well but fail to predict precision. Tightening the resource envelope increases cost without affecting precision, amplifying execution time more strongly than energy and penalizing larger models the most. Moreover, no single model dominates across all PTME dimensions, and a Pareto analysis reveals non-dominated configurations that would be hidden by accuracy-only or efficiency-only assessments, providing practical guidance for selecting models under different resource envelopes. These results show that selecting lightweight LLMs by size, FLOPs, latency, or accuracy alone can select the wrong deployment candidate; PTME profiling exposes configurations that preserve useful accuracy at lower physical cost.

</details>


### [69] [Enhancing Explainable Cardiac Diagnosis with Guide-Grounded Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20814)
*Hai-Nam Duy Vuong,Duy-Anh Bui,Trong-Nghia Nguyen,Kim-Ngan Thi Nguyen,Trang Mai Xuan,Tien-Cuong Nguyen,Van-Dem Pham,Thien Van Luong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A guide-grounded multimodal framework improves ECG report generation by explicitly anchoring LLM explanations in curated clinical knowledge, reducing hallucinations and increasing clinical trustworthiness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical deployment of deep learning ECG models is constrained by limited interpretability and hallucination risks of LLMs. Existing frameworks generate explanations that are weakly grounded in established diagnostic criteria, reducing trustworthiness and reproducibility.

Method: A CNN+Grad-CAM produces class probabilities and heatmaps from 12-lead ECG images. Authoritative ECG textbooks are distilled into a structured ECG Interpretation Guide, injected as fixed knowledge. A multimodal LLM generates reports conditioned on ECG image, Grad-CAM overlay, CNN facts, and the injected guide.

Result: Guide grounding improves semantic quality and perceived consistency of generated reports while preserving competitive classification performance. BERTScore increased from 0.818 to 0.953 on PTB-XL test set, indicating closer alignment with reference reports.

Conclusion: Injecting distilled interpretation guides into multimodal prompting reduces hallucinations and enhances clinical plausibility of LLM-based ECG explanations, advancing explainable cardiac diagnosis toward real-world deployment.

Abstract: The electrocardiogram (ECG) is a cornerstone of cardiac as- sessment, yet clinical deployment of deep learning models remains con- strained by limited interpretability and the hallucination risk of large language models (LLMs). Existing CNN+Grad-CAM+multimodal LLM frameworks can generate ECG reports, but their explanations are often only weakly grounded in established diagnostic criteria, reducing trust- worthiness and reproducibility. We propose a guide-grounded multimodal framework that explicitly anchors report generation in curated clinical knowledge. A convolutional neural network (CNN) and Grad-CAM first produce class probabilities and class-specific heatmaps from 12-lead ECG images. In parallel, authoritative ECG textbooks and guideline materials are distilled offline into a structured ECG Interpretation Guide, which is injected as a fixed knowledge block for every sample. Conditioned on the ECG image, Grad-CAM overlay, CNN-derived fact pack, and the in- jected guide, a multimodal LLM generates structured diagnostic reports with guideline-consistent terminology and criteria usage. Experiments on the full PTB-XL test set demonstrate that guide grounding improves se- mantic quality and perceived consistency of generated reports while pre- serving competitive classification performance. In particular, our method increases the average BERTScore of generated impressions from 0.818 to 0.953 relative to a strong CNN+Grad-CAM+MLLM baseline, indicat- ing closer alignment with reference reports. These findings suggest that injecting a distilled interpretation guide into the multimodal prompting pipeline offers a practical pathway to reduce hallucinations and enhance the clinical plausibility of LLM-based ECG explanations, bringing ex- plainable cardiac diagnosis closer to real-world deployment.

</details>


### [70] [Efficient and Interpretable Body-Based Emotion Recognition with Lightweight Temporal Convolutional Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20820)
*Christian Arzate Cruz,Stefanos Gkikas,Houshyar Asadi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Lightweight Temporal Convolutional Networks (TCNs) can provide efficient and interpretable body-based emotion recognition, achieving competitive accuracy with graph-based models while using significantly fewer parameters and faster inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graph-based skeleton models for body-based emotion recognition are computationally expensive, limiting their practical deployment in real-time affective systems. There's a need for more efficient alternatives that maintain good performance while being interpretable.

Method: The authors evaluate a family of Temporal Convolutional Network (TCN) models on DIEM-A dataset and compare them with a graph-based time-series graph (G-TSG) baseline. They analyze performance using accuracy, macro-F1, parameter count, and inference latency. They also use region-specific TCN models, zero-based occlusion, and G-TSG gradient saliency to understand body-region contributions.

Result: While G-TSG achieves the highest mean performance, TCN-Base remains within 1.58 accuracy points and 1.25 macro-F1 points while using 79.18% fewer parameters and reducing classifier latency by approximately 12.5×. Upper-body motion provides the strongest standalone regional cue, and the usefulness of body regions varies across emotions. Different interpretability methods capture distinct aspects of model behavior.

Conclusion: Lightweight TCNs can support efficient body-based emotion recognition while providing practical insights into how motion cues contribute to classification, offering a viable alternative to computationally expensive graph-based models.

Abstract: Body-based emotion recognition is important for real-time affective systems, but graph-based skeleton models can be computationally expensive. This paper studies whether lightweight temporal convolutional networks (TCNs) can provide an efficient and interpretable alternative for body-based emotion classification. We evaluate a family of TCN models on DIEM-A and compare them with a graph-based time-series graph (G-TSG) baseline using accuracy, macro-F1, parameter count, and inference latency. Although G-TSG achieves the highest mean performance, TCN-Base remains within $1.58$ accuracy points and $1.25$ macro-F1 points while using $79.18\%$ fewer parameters and reducing classifier latency by approximately $12.5\times$. We also analyze body-region contributions using region-specific TCN models, zero-based occlusion, and G-TSG gradient saliency. The results show that upper-body motion provides the strongest standalone regional cue, that the usefulness of body regions varies across emotions, and that different interpretability methods capture distinct aspects of model behavior. These findings suggest that lightweight TCNs can support efficient body-based emotion recognition while also providing practical insight into how motion cues contribute to classification.

</details>


### [71] [Auditing Provenance Sensitivity in LLM Agent Action Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20827)
*Junchi Liao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM agents can be influenced by unauthorized evidence when making tool-use decisions, with untrusted sources affecting actions even when models respond to authority cues.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents operate in contexts with mixed information sources (user requests, tool outputs, retrieved records, memory, untrusted text), where evidence can be relevant but unauthorized. There's a need to understand how unauthorized evidence influences agent decisions despite source-authority awareness.

Method: Introduced target-specific authorization audit labeling context factors for each tool/argument target. Primary test: fixed task, proposition, position, policy while changing source authority. Secondary tests: weakening valid evidence and using context-subset interactions as diagnostic. Evaluated across 450 controlled next-action tasks with multiple open-weight LLM families.

Result: Trusted vs untrusted variants produced different actions in 5.4% of competing cases vs 1.7% of supporting cases. Under controlled degradation, unauthorized competition was retained in 2.4% of comparisons (95% CI: 2.1-3.0%). Models respond to authority cues but untrusted evidence still influences actions.

Conclusion: LLM agents' tool-use decisions can be affected by unauthorized evidence even when they detect source-authority differences. The target-specific authorization audit reveals security-relevant failure patterns that persist across controlled stress tests.

Abstract: LLM agents choose tools and arguments from context that mixes user requests, tool outputs, retrieved records, memory, and untrusted text. Evidence can be relevant without being authorized to determine a decision, so a correct action need not be grounded only in permitted evidence. We introduce a target-specific authorization audit that labels context factors separately for each tool and argument target. Its primary test holds the task, proposition, position, and policy fixed while changing only the proposition's source authority. We then test behavior when valid evidence is weakened and use context-subset interactions as a secondary localization diagnostic. Across 450 controlled next-action tasks and multiple open-weight LLM families, trusted and untrusted variants produce different actions in 5.4 percent of competing cases versus 1.7 percent of supporting cases. Under controlled degradation, unauthorized competition is retained in a full-correct, mixed-error, clean-correct pattern in 2.4 percent of comparisons, with a 95 percent confidence interval from 2.1 to 3.0 percent. These are controlled stress-set rates, not deployment prevalence. The models respond to textual source-authority cues, but this does not prevent untrusted evidence from influencing their actions.

</details>


### [72] [Auditing Evidence Use in Medical LLM Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20848)
*Junchi Liao,Jiawen Deng,Fuji Ren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Medical LLMs need behavioral audits beyond just diagnostic accuracy to assess proper evidence use; this paper introduces a method to decompose patient information, score diagnoses under controlled evidence subsets, and analyze evidence interactions to distinguish clinically plausible patterns from failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current medical LLM evaluation focuses on diagnostic accuracy, but this doesn't reveal whether models use case evidence appropriately. Accuracy alone can hide evidence-use failures, creating a need for role-aware audits that examine how LLMs process and integrate clinical information.

Method: Behavioral audit approach decomposing patient information into evidence units, scoring candidate diagnoses under controlled evidence subsets, and mining low-order interactions in diagnostic margins. Separates interaction discovery from failure assignment, then validates suspicious cases through clinical review and robustness checks.

Result: Evaluation of five open-weight LLMs on DDXPlus, CupCase, and MedCase shows faithful support and differential diagnosis account for most interaction strength, indicating many evidence interactions are clinically plausible rather than failures. Invalid/shortcut-like cases concentrate in negated/absent findings and clinically local evidence.

Conclusion: Diagnostic accuracy can mask evidence-use failures in medical LLMs. The proposed behavioral audit method provides a more nuanced evaluation that distinguishes clinically plausible evidence interactions from problematic patterns, supporting role-aware audits for comprehensive medical LLM assessment.

Abstract: Medical LLMs are often evaluated by whether they select the correct diagnosis, but diagnostic accuracy alone does not show whether the model used the case evidence appropriately. We present a behavioral audit of evidence use in medical diagnosis. For each case, we decompose patient information into evidence units, score candidate diagnoses under controlled evidence subsets, and mine low-order interactions in diagnostic margins. Because medical evidence is diagnosis-relative, the audit separates interaction discovery from failure assignment: large or negative interactions can reflect plausible differential diagnosis, while suspicious interactions require robustness checks and clinical review. We evaluate five open-weight LLMs on DDXPlus, CupCase, and MedCase. Across datasets, faithful support and differential conflict or cancellation account for most interaction strength, showing that many evidence interactions are clinically plausible rather than failures. In a DDXPlus-focused blinded five-reviewer 130-item enriched review sample, invalid or shortcut-like cases concentrate in negated or absent findings and clinically local evidence. These results show that accuracy can hide candidate evidence-use failures and motivate role-aware audits for medical LLM evaluation.

</details>


### [73] [Code Monitor Red Teaming for Public-Test-Passing Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20852)
*Junchi Liao,Jiawen Deng,Fuji Ren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Weak LLMs can detect some hidden bugs in code that passes public tests, but still miss most bugs even with scaffolding. Adversarial pressure worsens detection, while stronger verifiers partly recover performance but face evidence limitations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current practice relies on visible tests to validate LLM-generated code, but passing these tests doesn't guarantee specification correctness. The paper investigates whether weaker LLM verifiers can identify hidden bugs after code has passed public tests, addressing a deployment-like monitoring problem.

Method: Introduces Code Monitor Red Teaming protocol with fixed public-check information boundary while varying: generator pressure (adversarial vs. benign), verifier scaffolding (prompt structure/support), and weak-to-strong capability differences. Instantiates as CodeMonitorBench across three domains: function-level code, data-science code, and workflow code, evaluating 71,000 generated code candidates.

Result: Of 71,000 generated candidates, 43,677 pass public tests, and 23,081 of those have hidden bugs. Weak verifiers show improvement with scaffolding and better model families, but still miss most hidden bugs at 5% false-positive rate. Adversarial public-test-overfit pressure lowers verifier AUROC and increases low-FPR miss rates. Stronger GLM-5.1 verifier recovers some performance gap but still has limitations due to evidence boundaries.

Conclusion: Weak LLM verifiers have limited effectiveness in detecting hidden bugs even with scaffolding, especially under adversarial pressure. There exists an evidence boundary problem where some bugs cannot be detected from the available information, mixing verifier capability limitations with inherent evidence constraints.

Abstract: Visible tests are a common gate for LLM-generated code, but passing them does not certify specification correctness. We study a deployment-like monitoring problem: after code has passed public tests, can a weaker LLM verifier identify the residual hidden bugs? We introduce Code Monitor Red Teaming, a monitor-red-teaming protocol that fixes a public-check information boundary while varying generator pressure, verifier scaffolding, and weak-to-strong capability. We instantiate it as CodeMonitorBench, spanning function-level, data-science, and workflow code. Across 71,000 generated candidates, 43,677 pass public tests and 23,081 of those fail hidden tests. Weak verifiers improve with scaffolding and model family, but still miss most hidden bugs at 5% false-positive rate. As a robustness stress test, adversarial public-test-overfit pressure lowers verifier AUROC and raises low-FPR miss rates in most cells. A GLM-5.1 verifier recovers part of the gap under the same evidence boundary; an inferability audit shows that remaining misses mix verifier failures with M1 evidence limits.

</details>


### [74] [Is Deep Research Reliable? Misleading Knowledge Induces False Conclusions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20891)
*Pengyu Zhu,Lijun Li,Longju Yang,Sen Su*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework called MisKnow-Agent reveals vulnerabilities in Deep Research agents where misleading information in open environments can propagate through research workflows and lead to false conclusions in final reports, even with verification defenses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep Research agents (LLM-based assistants for long-horizon workflows) have underexplored reliability issues in open information environments. There's concern that misleading but credible-looking knowledge could propagate through planning, retrieval, evidence synthesis, and report generation workflows, leading to false conclusions in final reports.

Method: Introduced MisKnow-Agent framework for constructing and validating misleading knowledge for Deep Research tasks. Generated 5,933 quality-controlled misleading instances with controllable authority levels/styles based on DeepResearch Benchmark tasks. Conducted extensive experiments across open-source and closed-source Deep Research agents, evaluated pre- and post-research defenses individually and in combination.

Result: Even limited exposure to misleading knowledge can induce false-conclusion adoption in final reports. Search-enabled verifier models identify misleading instances during focused validation, but same instances can be adopted during long-horizon research (disconnect between verification and workflow-level evidence use). All defense configurations mitigate but don't fully prevent false-conclusion adoption.

Conclusion: Current Deep Research agents have broad reliability vulnerabilities. Reliable Deep Research requires evidence verification and correction capabilities at both model and framework levels, beyond improvements in planning, retrieval, evidence integration, or report-generation abilities alone.

Abstract: Deep Research agents extend LLM-based assistants into long-horizon workflows involving planning, retrieval, evidence synthesis, and report generation, yet their reliability in open information environments remains underexplored. A key concern is whether apparently credible but factually misleading knowledge encountered in such environments can propagate through these workflows and be adopted as false conclusions in final reports. To study this failure mode, we introduce MisKnow-Agent, a framework for constructing and validating misleading knowledge for Deep Research tasks. MisKnow-Agent generates misleading instances with controllable authority levels and styles, yielding 5,933 quality-controlled instances built on DeepResearch Benchmark tasks. Extensive experiments across open-source and closed-source Deep Research agents show that even limited exposure to misleading knowledge can induce false-conclusion adoption in final reports, revealing a broad reliability vulnerability in current Deep Research agents. Although search-enabled verifier models consistently identify the retained instances as misleading during focused corpus validation, the same instances can still be adopted during long-horizon research, revealing a disconnect between focused verification and workflow-level evidence use. Finally, we evaluate pre- and post-research defenses, both individually and in combination, finding that all three configurations mitigate but do not fully prevent false-conclusion adoption. Our findings suggest that reliable Deep Research requires evidence verification and correction capabilities at both the model and framework levels, beyond improvements in planning, retrieval, evidence integration, or report-generation abilities.

</details>


### [75] [Source-Prior-Driven Selective Adaptation for Efficient Diffusion Model Finetuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20913)
*Yi Xiong,Yuan-Yuan Cheng,Xiao-Ming Fu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A novel selective adaptation method for diffusion model fine-tuning that achieves better trade-off between adaptation to new domains and retention of general generative capabilities by identifying optimal parameters for modification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-tuning large diffusion models for new domains/styles degrades the pretrained model's broad generative capability. Existing methods handle this trade-off only implicitly, resulting in suboptimal balance between adaptation and retention.

Method: Proposes source-prior-driven selective adaptation with two key components: (1) learning a static mask to identify parameters better suited for downstream adaptation based on their impact on general generative capability, and (2) constructing structured update strategies for the selected parameter subset.

Result: Experiments show the method achieves a better adaptation-retention trade-off than existing strong baselines.

Conclusion: The method efficiently fine-tunes diffusion models by explicitly managing the trade-off between domain adaptation and preservation of general capabilities through selective parameter adaptation, outperforming current approaches.

Abstract: Fine-tuning large diffusion models for new domains or styles involves a trade-off: improving target-specific generation often degrades the pretrained model's broad generative capability. Existing full and parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods typically handle this trade-off only implicitly. In this work, we propose a novel source-prior-driven selective adaptation method to efficiently fine-tune diffusion models, achieving a favorable trade-off. Our method relies on two key observations: (1) the loss of general generative capability is highly inconsistent across pretrained parameters, and (2) parameters that have a relatively small impact on the model's general generative capability remain structurally inconsistent across layers and parameter types. Motivated by these observations, we first learn a static mask to explicitly identify parameters better suited for downstream adaptation, and then construct structured update strategies for the selected subset. Experiments show that our method achieves a better adaptation-retention trade-off than existing strong baselines.

</details>


### [76] [Traceable Scholarship: Page Anchors and Ariadne's Thread for Humanistic Inquiry in the Age of Generative AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20916)
*Deyu Jing*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes "Traceable Scholarship" as a necessary framework for maintaining scholarly integrity when using generative AI in humanities research, emphasizing the importance of verifiable sources and evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative AI can produce fluent scholarly-looking text quickly, but fluency doesn't guarantee validity. The main risk is the appearance of established explanations without proper sources, evidence, or citations - creating a veneer of scholarship without traceability.

Method: Proposes Traceable Scholarship framework with key components: page anchors, dual page numbers, citation-first generation, NO_EVIDENCE flag, human verification, four-level compliance, and Scope Contract. Implements AIH-Infra as a three-layer reference system: Contexture (document structuring), Open WebUI AIH-Infra (traceable knowledge base), and AIH-Infra MCP Server (agent gateway). Demonstrates with a case study on Kant's Akademie-Ausgabe knowledge base.

Result: Presents a working framework that enables retrieval correction, evidence grading, and judgment downgrading in AI-assisted research. Shows how traceability mechanisms can support scholarly practices like verifying sources and evaluating evidence quality.

Conclusion: Traceability is not just a software feature but a necessary condition for humanistic research to remain public, refutable, and maintain scholarly integrity in the generative AI era. It serves as the "Ariadne's thread" guiding scholars back to original sources through the "labyrinth of generative fluency."

Abstract: Generative AI lets large language models produce scholarly-looking text within seconds, yet fluency does not equal valid explanation. The deepest risk is not factual error alone but the appearance that an explanation is already established without clear sources, page numbers, editions, or evidence. We liken the page anchor to Ariadne's thread: within the labyrinth of generative fluency, it is the thread that leads the scholar back to the source. This paper proposes Traceable Scholarship as the minimum normative condition for AI-assisted humanistic research, situating it across the three revolutions of knowledge infrastructure: print, digital, and generative AI. We introduce page anchors, dual page numbers, citation-first generation, NO_EVIDENCE, human verification, four-level compliance, and Scope Contract, and present AIH-Infra as a three-layer reference implementation: Contexture (document structuring), Open WebUI AIH-Infra (traceable knowledge base), and AIH-Infra MCP Server (agent gateway). A case study on a 29-volume Kant Akademie-Ausgabe knowledge base illustrates how traceability supports retrieval correction, evidence grading, and judgment downgrading. Traceability is not a software feature; it is the condition under which humanistic research can remain public and refutable in the age of generative AI.

</details>


### [77] [OPOD: On-Policy Omni Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20918)
*Tong Zhao,Yuyang Hu,Reed Li,Yu Lu,Haibo Shi,Yutao Zhu,Zhicheng Dou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OPOD is an on-policy distillation method that routes student responses to matching modality teachers, prevents modality competition, and improves omni-modal models across text, image, and audio tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Omni-modal models struggle to match specialists in individual modalities when trained on pooled multimodal data. Existing multi-teacher distillation can cause competing guidance and imbalance across modalities.

Method: OPOD routes each student response to the matching text, image, or audio teacher, keeps teacher guidance only where teachers assign higher probability than the student, adjusts modality influences independently, and evaluates both final answers and reasoning.

Result: OPOD achieves best average scores across 12 benchmarks and 3 backbone sizes (70.8, 51.7, 46.2 points), exceeding strongest comparators by 2.1, 1.8, 1.7 points. 30B model outperforms base model and pooled-trained counterpart on all 12 benchmarks.

Conclusion: Coordinating modality-specific teachers via OPOD effectively improves shared omni-modal models while maintaining cross-modal balance, producing a single deployable model that discards specialists after training.

Abstract: Omni-modal models can handle text, images, and audio in one system, but improving all of these abilities together remains difficult. Training a single model on pooled multimodal data often fails to match models specialized for individual modalities.
