On-policy distillation (OPD) offers a way to combine such specialists: the student generates a response, and a teacher evaluates that same response, so the student learns directly from behaviors it actually produces. Yet using several teachers can introduce competing guidance and improve one modality at the expense of another. We present On-Policy Omni Distillation (OPOD), which routes each student response to the matching text, image, or audio teacher. OPOD keeps teacher guidance only on tokens where the teacher assigns a higher probability than the student, adjusts the influence of each modality teacher independently during training, and asks the routed teacher to assess both the final answer and whether the reasoning supports the correct answer. Across twelve benchmarks and three backbone sizes, OPOD achieves the best average score at every scale, reaching 70.8, 51.7, and 46.2 and exceeding the strongest comparator by 2.1, 1.8, and 1.7 points. On the 30B model, it outperforms both the base model and a counterpart post-trained jointly on pooled multimodal data on all twelve benchmarks, and ranks first or second on eleven even when the individual specialists are included. The specialists are discarded after training, leaving one deployable omni-modal model. These results show that coordinating modality-specific teachers is an effective way to improve a shared model while maintaining cross-modal balance.

</details>


### [78] [Representing Entity Importance in AI Knowledge Systems: A Dual-Signal Framework of Audience Evaluation and Structural Authority](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20925)
*Shen Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a dual-signal representation for entity importance with audience-evaluation and structural-authority dimensions, showing they are non-redundant and shouldn't be collapsed into a single score.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI knowledge systems often reduce entity importance to a single score from human responses or graph structure, which may discard important distinctions needed for different AI tasks like retrieval, recommendation, and reasoning.

Method: The study proposes an interpretable dual-signal framework with audience-evaluation and structural-authority dimensions. Using movie entities as validation domain, it combines IMDb ratings (audience ranking), Wikidata for entity alignment, and Wikipedia hyperlinks with PageRank (structural authority). Analyzed 482 entities and 13,690 directed relationships.

Result: Found statistically significant but weak association between dimensions (Spearman rho = 0.2275, p < 0.001). Overlap was only 10% in top 10 entities and 34% in top 100, with entity-level divergence in both directions.

Conclusion: Audience evaluation and structural authority are non-redundant signals that shouldn't be collapsed into a single importance score. The framework supports task-aware AI knowledge systems that preserve distinct importance signals for context-specific selection or aggregation.

Abstract: AI knowledge systems require representations of entity importance for retrieval, recommendation, evidence selection, and knowledge-intensive reasoning. Yet importance is often reduced to a single score derived from either human response or graph structure. Such compression may discard distinctions that matter when an AI system must choose among entities for different tasks. This study introduces an interpretable dual-signal representation in which each entity is characterized by an audience-evaluation dimension and a structural-authority dimension.
  The framework is evaluated using movie entities as an empirical validation domain. IMDb non-commercial datasets provide a rating-based audience ranking, Wikidata supports entity alignment, and English Wikipedia hyperlinks form the knowledge network on which PageRank estimates structural authority. Experiments on 482 entities and 13,690 directed relationships reveal a statistically significant but weak association between the two dimensions (Spearman rho = 0.2275, p < 0.001). Their overlap is only 10% in the top 10 and 34% in the top 100, while entity-level divergence occurs in both directions.
  The results show that audience evaluation and structural authority are non-redundant signals and should not automatically be collapsed into a single scalar notion of importance. The contribution is not a new ranking algorithm or learned embedding, but a minimal knowledge-representation framework and an empirical test of its dimensional necessity. The findings support task-aware AI knowledge systems that preserve distinct importance signals before applying context-specific selection or aggregation.

</details>


### [79] [SciExplore: Evaluating Autonomous Agents from Scientific Navigation to Information Integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20926)
*Yinhao Tang,Youqing Fang,Yanan Sun,Wenran Liu,Weiming Zhang,Bin Liu,Kuikun Liu,Wenwei Zhang,Kai Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SciExplore is a new benchmark evaluating LLM capabilities for scientific information-seeking and reasoning workflows, with four task types across 100+ tasks that reveal significant performance gaps as complexity increases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks focus on general-domain retrieval or static scientific QA, failing to assess key capabilities needed for realistic scientific research workflows involving heterogeneous sources and complex reasoning.

Method: Created SciExplore benchmark with 103 expert-curated tasks across 10+ scientific disciplines, featuring four progressive task types: scientific database navigation, ambiguous literature retrieval, missing reference completion, and cross-source structured knowledge synthesis.

Result: Evaluation of 10+ state-of-the-art LLMs and agents shows substantial performance gaps, with performance degrading sharply as task complexity increases and extremely low accuracy on the most challenging structured synthesis tasks.

Conclusion: Current LLMs and autonomous agents have significant limitations in realistic scientific information-seeking scenarios, highlighting the need for improved scientific reasoning capabilities.

Abstract: Scientific research involves complex information-seeking and reasoning workflows across heterogeneous sources. However, existing benchmarks primarily emphasize general-domain retrieval or static scientific question answering, and therefore fail to assess key capabilities required in realistic scientific research workflows. We introduce SciExplore, a benchmark designed to evaluate scientific information-seeking and reasoning capabilities of LLMs and agents. SciExplore comprises four task types covering 103 expert-curated tasks across more than ten scientific disciplines: scientific database navigation, ambiguous literature retrieval, missing reference completion, and cross-source structured knowledge synthesis, which probe progressively higher-level abilities from entity-level reasoning and document-level identification to evidence-level grounding and domain-level synthesis. We evaluate over ten state-of-the-art LLMs and autonomous agents on SciExplore, revealing substantial performance gaps with performance degrading sharply as task complexity increases and extremely low accuracy on the most challenging structured synthesis tasks. These results highlight significant limitations of current models and agents in realistic scientific information-seeking scenarios.

</details>


### [80] [Clustered Edge Intelligence: Beyond Just Convergence of Edge Computing and AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20937)
*Chinmaya Kumar Dehury,Boris Sedlak,Alaa Saleh,Ilir Murturi,Lauri Loven,Satish Narayana Srirama,Praveen Kumar Donta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Clustered Edge Intelligence (CEI) proposes treating derived intelligence as a first-class, shareable entity across edge networks, addressing gaps in current edge intelligence research that lacks proper intelligence representation and management frameworks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The shift from information age to intelligence age requires treating derived intelligence as a first-class entity. Current edge intelligence research focuses only on using AI for resource management and deploying models on edge devices, lacking frameworks for intelligence representation, discovery, sharing, reuse, and clustering across heterogeneous edge devices and applications.

Method: Introduces Clustered Edge Intelligence (CEI) approach with a three-layer architecture. Examines enabling technologies including intelligence inventories, semantic knowledge representation, communication protocols, discoverability mechanisms, observability tools, lifecycle automation, clustering mechanisms, marketplaces, interoperability standards, and standardization efforts.

Result: Presents a visionary intelligence-centric framework that makes intelligence shareable and reusable as a first-class entity across the distributed edge-cloud continuum. Defines a comprehensive architecture and identifies key research dimensions needed for implementation.

Conclusion: CEI represents a paradigm shift in edge intelligence by treating derived intelligence as independently manageable entity that can be described, discovered, observed, shared, reused, and dynamically clustered across heterogeneous edge environments, addressing current research gaps in edge computing.

Abstract: We are moving from an information age to the age of intelligence. A decade, or possibly less than that, data will not be the gold anymore rather the derived intelligence out of the data and the information we posses from the edge of the network. Existing Edge Intelligence research focuses mainly on two directions: using AI for edge resource management and deploying lightweight AI models on edge devices. However, existing edge computing research lacks an intelligence-centric framework in which derived intelligence is treated as a first-class, independently manageable entity that can be described, discovered, observed, shared, reused, and dynamically clustered across heterogeneous edge devices and applications. To address these research gaps, we introduced Clustered Edge Intelligence, a visionary intelligence-centric approach. The aim of CEI is to make intelligence a shareable and reusable first-class entity that can be independently represented, discovered, observed, exchanged, and managed across the distributed edge-cloud continuum. We present a three layer CEI architecture and examine enabling technologies and research dimensions, including intelligence inventories, semantic knowledge representation, communication, discoverability, observability, lifecycle automation, clustering mechanisms, marketplaces, interoperability, and standardization.

</details>


### [81] [From Scalars to Time Series: Rethinking Implicit Neural Representations for Time-Varying Volumetric Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20970)
*Weihan Zhang,Xuan Zhao,Yenwen Peng,Yuqi Chen,Jun Tao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: INRs for time-varying data can be trained more efficiently using sequence-level supervision over spatial time series instead of dense spatiotemporal sampling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current INR methods for time-varying volumetric data use dense coordinate-wise sampling, which is computationally expensive and inefficient in exploiting temporal structure.

Method: Reformulate the problem by representing data as spatially-indexed time series and training INRs with sequence-level supervision over each spatial location's full temporal evolution.

Result: The approach consistently improves reconstruction quality while significantly reducing training cost, and when combined with mixture-of-experts architectures, it outperforms existing MoE-based INR methods.

Conclusion: Dense spatiotemporal sampling is unnecessary for learning time-varying fields; sequence-level supervision over spatial time series offers better efficiency and quality, especially when combined with specialized architectures for heterogeneous temporal dynamics.

Abstract: Implicit neural representations (INRs) for time-varying volumetric data are typically trained using dense sampling over spatiotemporal coordinates, where each observation corresponds to a single point in space and time. This coordinate-wise formulation requires extensive sampling during optimization, leading to high computational cost and inefficient use of temporal structure. In this work, we revisit this design choice and show that dense spatiotemporal sampling is not necessary for learning time-varying fields. Instead, we represent the data as a collection of spatially indexed time series and train INRs using sequence-level supervision over each spatial location, rather than coordinate-wise scalar samples. This reformulation eliminates the need for dense spatiotemporal sampling and instead learns each spatial location from its full temporal evolution in a structured manner. We demonstrate that this representation is compatible with a range of existing INR architectures and consistently improves reconstruction quality, while significantly reducing training cost. Furthermore, we show that this formulation can be combined with mixture-of-experts architectures, and that our MoE instantiation further improves reconstruction quality compared to both the base reformulation and existing MoE-based INR methods, providing a stronger capacity allocation under heterogeneous temporal dynamics.

</details>


### [82] [Delivery, Not Storage: Cue-Anchored Working Memory as a Harness Property for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20972)
*Swapnanil Saha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that human-like incidental memory (automatic encoding/retrieval based on situational cues) is crucial for long-running coding agents, proposing a two-tier memory system with deterministic cue-based memory injection by the harness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current coding agents rely only on deliberate, document-based memory (voluntary writes and reads), missing the crucial incidental memory system humans use for situationally-bound operational facts that are automatically encoded and retrieved based on contextual cues.

Method: Proposes a two-tier memory architecture based on cognitive science principles: (1) voluntary document memory + (2) harness-managed incidental memory. Introduces a cue-anchored memory model with first-class trigger conditions (path, symbol, semantic, event, temporal) that are deterministically evaluated by the harness.

Result: Evaluation shows: voluntary memory usage near zero (0 operations in 114 turns); deterministic injection works reliably with zero false alarms; 39% of intra-session re-reads re-use content; compaction experiments show harness-controlled memory persists while conversation-based memory gets lost.

Conclusion: For effective long-running agents, reliable incidental memory must be a harness property rather than agent choice - delivery (automatic, cue-based injection) matters more than storage, creating a memory channel the agent never has to think about.

Abstract: Coding agents ship with one kind of memory: documents. Instruction files, plan artifacts, and auto-written memory directories are deliberately authored and deliberately retrieved: the agent must choose to write them and choose to read them back. Human expertise runs on a second tier that never gets written down: situationally-bound operational facts (gotchas, locations, local conventions) encoded as a side effect of the work and retrieved involuntarily when the situation cues them. We argue this second tier is the load-bearing one for long-running agents and must be a harness property, not an agent choice. We contribute: (1) a two-tier design theory grounded in the cognitive literature on memory offloading, incidental encoding, and event-based prospective memory, each mapped to an architectural requirement; (2) a cue-anchored memory model where memories carry first-class trigger conditions over a composable vocabulary (path, symbol, semantic, event, temporal), evaluated deterministically by the harness, a composition no surveyed academic or shipped system provides; (3) a controlled evaluation on a real coding task showing that voluntary memory use is near zero even with a pre-seeded store (0 memory operations in 114 turns), that deterministic injection delivered in every seeded run with zero false alarms, and that 39% of intra-session re-reads re-buy content paid for before a compaction boundary; (4) a repeated-compaction decay probe: ten facts held only in conversation vanish at the first summary and stay absent from 106 of 108 compactions, and the deprived agent greps the harness's own session files to rebuild them, while the same facts injected from a harness-owned store arrive intact through all 138 compact-resumes as the final summary carries none. Delivery, not storage, is the product: the reliable memory channel for agents is the one the agent never has to think about.

</details>


### [83] [Beyond Independent Optimization: Compression, MoE Routing, and Quantization Interactions in Multimodal Edge Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20981)
*Jay Gor,Karm Dave,Akshita Abrol,Rajesh Gupta,Sudeep Tanwar,Zhengkui Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A comprehensive review paper analyzing the complex interactions between various efficiency techniques for multimodal AI models, emphasizing that these optimizations cannot be treated independently due to their interconnected effects on system performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal inference efficiency is increasingly constrained not just by model quality or FLOPs, but by the practical costs of managing multimodal representations - including preserving, moving, routing, caching, and quantizing them under real-world latency, memory, and energy constraints.

Method: Reviews recent advances in efficient vision-language and multimodal LLMs, organizing the literature around interactions between key techniques: visual token compression, video token management, KV-cache optimization, Mixture-of-Experts routing, low-bit quantization, edge deployment, and hardware-aware benchmarking.

Result: Identifies that these techniques have complex interdependencies - visual compression affects feature distributions and MoE routing decisions, routing affects expert utilization and quantization sensitivity, quantization affects router logits and expert assignment, KV-cache policies affect evidence retention. Organizes literature around these interactions and identifies key design trade-offs.

Conclusion: Introduces Temporal Routing Consistency as a diagnostic tool for video MoE models, and highlights critical research directions in routing-aware compression, cross-modal cache management, hardware-aware co-design, and unified benchmarking for multimodal edge intelligence systems.

Abstract: Efficient multimodal inference is increasingly constrained not only by model quality or FLOP count, but also by the cost of preserving, moving, routing, caching, and quantizing multimodal representations under latency, memory, and energy constraints. This paper reviews recent advances in efficient vision-language and multimodal large language models, covering visual token compression, video token management, KV-cache optimization, Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) routing, low-bit quantization, edge deployment, and hardware-aware benchmarking. We argue that these techniques cannot be treated as independent optimizations. Visual token compression alters downstream feature distributions and MoE routing decisions, routing behavior affects expert utilization and quantization sensitivity, quantized router logits influence expert assignment, KV-cache policies determine retained multimodal evidence, and hardware constraints often transform computational savings into memory and communication bottlenecks. We organize the literature around these interactions and identify key design trade-offs, including accuracy versus token budget, static versus adaptive compression, sparse routing efficiency versus expert collapse, and low-bit inference versus modality-specific degradation. Finally, we introduce Temporal Routing Consistency as a diagnostic for video MoE models and highlight open research directions in routing-aware compression, cross-modal cache management, hardware-aware co-design, and unified benchmarking for multimodal edge intelligence.

</details>


### [84] [GuardianAgentBench: Where Agents Fail and How to Guard Them](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20982)
*Vishal Ishwar Naik,Chenyu Xu,Donna Dong,Hussein Hassan,Abhishek Pradhan,Ofer Mendelevitch,Tallat Shafat,Humayun Irshad*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GuardianAgentBench is a new benchmark for evaluating safety of autonomous LLM agents with tools, revealing significant vulnerabilities even in state-of-the-art models and showing that execution-time guardrails outperform system prompt defenses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM agents increasingly operate autonomously with access to tools and external environments, ensuring their safe and reliable behavior becomes critical. There's a need for comprehensive benchmarking to identify vulnerabilities in current agent frameworks.

Method: Created GuardianAgentBench (GABench) with 580 scenarios across six domains, evaluated on three production-ready frameworks (LangChain, LlamaIndex, Vectara). Incorporated rigorous multi-stage validation and five adversarial attack modes. Tested six state-of-the-art models to analyze failure patterns and performance.

Result: Even the strongest configuration achieved only 74.8% overall accuracy. Found two distinct failure regimes: stronger models under-call required tools, while weaker models mis-select and over-call tools. Performance degrades with tool-set size and sequential turn depth, with long-horizon planning being the steeper bottleneck. Their guardrail implementation recovered 19.9% of failures at 0.5% false positive rate, consistently outperforming system-prompt-based defenses.

Conclusion: Execution-time structural intervention through guardrails improves safety without disrupting correct agent behavior, demonstrating that current LLM agent systems have significant safety vulnerabilities that need systematic benchmarking and mitigation strategies.

Abstract: As large language model agents increasingly operate autonomously with access to tools and external environments, ensuring their safe and reliable behavior becomes critical. We present GuardianAgentBench (GABench), a benchmark of 580 scenarios across six domains evaluated on three production-ready frameworks: LangChain, LlamaIndex, and Vectara. The benchmark incorporates rigorous multi-stage validation and five adversarial attack modes. Experiments with six state-of-the-art models reveal that even the strongest configuration achieves only 74.8% overall accuracy and expose two distinct failure regimes: stronger models under-call required tools, while weaker models mis-select and over-call tools. Performance degrades monotonically with both tool-set size and sequential turn depth, with long-horizon planning proving the steeper bottleneck. Our guardrail implementation consistently outperforms system-prompt-based defenses across all models, recovering 19.9% of failures at a false positive rate of just 0.5%. These results demonstrate that execution-time structural intervention improves safety without disrupting correct agent behavior.

</details>


### [85] [Workflow-Localized Mechanism Learning: Attribution-Guided Repair and Knowledge Reuse for Structured Agent Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20999)
*Zibin Lin,Shengli Zhang,Taotao Wang,Yihan Xia,Deen Ma,Guofu Liao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WML is a workflow-localized mechanism learning approach that identifies failures in language agent workflows, attributes them to specific mechanisms or relationships, and optimizes skills using third-party knowledge for improved task performance and transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing skill optimizers for language-model agents don't jointly resolve where failures occur in workflows, which mechanisms caused them, and how to properly reuse relevant knowledge from third-party skills at the local level.

Method: WML uses Node-Mechanism Attribution to identify failed workflow nodes, implicated mechanisms, and smallest valid edit targets, routing issues to appropriate resources. It employs a six-module Workflow-Guided Skill Optimization loop that selects provenance- and scope-aware third-party knowledge, applies bounded patches, evaluates candidates, and stores verified outcomes in optimizer-side memory.

Result: On SpreadsheetBench, WML achieves 90.33±1.53 and 74.67±3.51 Hard Accuracy with DeepSeek and Qwen3.6-Flash. Learned skills transfer to WikiTableQuestions with 84.00±2.00 and 83.00±2.00 Denotation Accuracy without additional optimization. On Compiler-Supported50, WML attains highest hard-PASS rate and lowest cost per successful task while reducing tokens and calls significantly.

Conclusion: WML provides an effective approach for localizing and fixing workflow failures in language agents by properly attributing issues to mechanisms or relationships and optimizing skills through targeted third-party knowledge reuse, with demonstrated improvements across multiple benchmarks and transfer capabilities.

Abstract: Agent Skills package reusable procedural knowledge as external artifacts for frozen language-model agents, yet existing optimizers do not jointly resolve where a failure occurs in a workflow, which mechanism caused it, and how relevant knowledge from third-party Skills should be reused locally. We introduce Workflow-Localized Mechanism Learning (WML). Its Node--Mechanism Attribution identifies the failed workflow node, implicated mechanisms, and smallest valid edit target, routing single-mechanism defects to L3 resources and relational defects across mechanisms to L2 composition protocols. A six-module Workflow-Guided Skill Optimization (WGSO) loop then selects provenance- and scope-aware third-party knowledge, applies bounded patches, evaluates candidates, and stores verified outcomes in optimizer-side memory. On SpreadsheetBench, WML reaches 90.33 +/- 1.53 and 74.67 +/- 3.51 Hard Accuracy with DeepSeek and Qwen3.6-Flash, respectively; without additional optimization, the learned Skills transfer to WikiTableQuestions with 84.00 +/- 2.00 and 83.00 +/- 2.00 Denotation Accuracy. On Compiler-Supported50, WML attains both the highest hard-PASS rate and the lowest cost per successful task; compiled execution sharply reduces tokens and calls relative to a direct SkillAgent while retaining most of its successful tasks. Code and artifacts are available at https://github.com/xiaolin9595/workflow-localized-mechanism-learning.

</details>


### [86] [Naju: A Native Discrete State-Space Model with Independent Retention and Writing for Long-Sequence Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21000)
*Hyuk Lim,Seunghyun Yoon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Naju introduces a decoupled recurrent architecture with explicit forget gate and independent write gain, enabling strong long-term memory retention and active overwriting simultaneously, outperforming Mamba and competing with Transformers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-sequence memory tracking requires both near-lossless retention of stored bindings over long horizons and active overwriting of stale information. Current efficient baselines tend to solve only one side well, creating a fundamental trade-off between retention and overwriting capabilities.

Method: Naju (Native Adaptive Junction Unit) parameterizes the discrete transition directly rather than using continuous-time discretization. It factorizes the recurrent update into explicit learned forget gate (discrete pole), independent write gain, and input-dependent write/read maps. This decoupling removes the constraint that couples retention and write gain in single-gate designs.

Result: Naju is the only evaluated model that remains strong on both retention and overwriting at 4x training length. It outperforms Mamba baselines in WikiText-103 language modeling, Long Range Arena, and multi-query associative recall while remaining competitive with Transformers and preserving linear-time, linear-memory scaling.

Conclusion: Decoupling the forget gate from write gain is crucial for overcoming the fundamental trade-off between memory retention and active overwriting. Naju demonstrates that native discrete parameterization with stable by-design architecture enables superior long-range memory tracking capabilities across various tasks.

Abstract: Long-sequence memory tracking places two opposing demands on a recurrent state: near-lossless retention of stored bindings over long horizons, and active overwriting of stale ones. In our diagnostic suite, the strongest efficient baselines tend to solve only one side well. Continuous-time-parameterized state-space models (SSMs) such as Mamba obtain their discrete recurrence by zero-order-hold discretization of a continuous-time system; we argue that this detour is unnecessary for memory tracking and parameterize the discrete transition directly. Naju (Native Adaptive Junction Unit) factorizes the recurrent update, schematically $x_n = f_n\odot x_{n-1} + i_n\odot(B_n u_n)$, into an explicit discrete pole (a learned forget gate $f_n$), an independent write gain $i_n$, and input-dependent write/read maps. Since the sigmoid pole satisfies $0<f_n<1$, each frozen local coordinate is Schur-stable by construction, and the full time-varying recurrence satisfies a fading-memory/BIBO bound under uniform boundedness assumptions, with no stability regularizer. We formalize the key structural limitation of coupled designs: any non-expansive complementary single-gate recurrence ties the effective retention $r$ and write gain $w$ through $|r|+w\le 1$, so near-complete retention forces weak writing; decoupling $f_n$ from $i_n$ removes this constraint. Empirically, Naju is the only evaluated model that remains strong on both retention and overwriting at 4x the training length. Beyond the diagnostic suite, we evaluate Naju on WikiText-103 language modeling, Long Range Arena, and multi-query associative recall. Across these settings, Naju consistently combines strong long-range memory with competitive or superior performance, outperforming the Mamba baselines in the principal comparisons while remaining competitive with the Transformer and preserving linear-time, linear-memory scaling.

</details>


### [87] [Reexamining zero-shot summarization: Empirical investigation of trustworthiness of LLM-summarizers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21010)
*Vasudha Bhatnagar,Purnima Bindal,Vikas Kumar,Raj Kumari Bahl*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a two-level diagnostic protocol to benchmark LLM summarization stability and trustworthiness by measuring consistency across multiple generations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stochasticity in LLMs raises concerns about stability and trustworthiness of zero-shot generated summaries, especially in educational contexts where students use LLMs to summarize academic materials.

Method: Two-level diagnostic protocol: 1) Document-level stability analysis measuring semantic/factual alignment across multiple LLM-generated summaries under controlled conditions, 2) Corpus-level stability index estimation by consolidating observations from stratified document samples.

Result: Empirical investigation reveals statistically significant differences in generation-level variability among three LLM-summarizers across three document genres, showing varying stability across evaluation metrics.

Conclusion: Advances LLM-summarization research by evidencing stability problems in LLM-generated summaries and motivates development of more robust, reliable, and trustworthy LLM-summarizers.

Abstract: Zero-shot summarization using Large Language Models (LLMs) has significantly advanced the abstractive summarization task by producing coherent and fluent summaries. However, underlying stochasticity of the large language models raises concerns about the stability and trustworthiness of the LLM-generated summaries. This issue has become increasingly important due to proliferation of LLM-generated summaries in educational settings, where students and researchers summarize complex academic materials in zero-shot manner.
  We propose a novel two-level diagnostic protocol for benchmarking LLM-summarizers based on the stability of the generated summaries. At the lower level, document-level stability analysis is performed over multiple LLM-summaries generated under controlled environment, and the stability coefficient is computed. Each generated summary is scored for semantic and factual alignment with the original document, enabling estimation of stability along more than one dimensions. At the next level, observations from a stratified sample of documents drawn from the corpus are consolidated to estimate the stability index of the LLM-summarizer, which is the proxy for its trustworthiness.
  Our empirical investigation of three LLM-summarizers across three genres of documents reveals statistically significant differences in the generation-level variability among LLMs across summary evaluation metrics. This study advances the LLM-summarization research by evidential recognition of the stability problem in LLM-summaries and motivates further research towards development of robust, reliable and trustworthy LLM-summarizers.

</details>


### [88] [EmoAgent-R1: Towards Multimodal Emotion Understanding with Reinforcement Learning-based Dynamic Agent Specialization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21013)
*Lihuang Fang,Yuchen Zou,kebin Jin,Jinghui Qin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes EmoAgent-R1, a reinforcement learning-based framework that enhances multimodal emotion recognition in MLLMs by using dynamic agent specialization and a novel P-GRPO optimization method.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MLLM-based multimodal emotion recognition methods use fixed prompts to perceive emotions, which ignores the dynamic and complex nature of emotion sources in multimodal inputs. There's a need for more adaptive emotion perception that can handle the intricacies of emotional content.

Method: EmoAgent-R1 framework with: 1) Cold start strategy using synthetic answer-conditioned chain-of-thought data and agent routing data for initial emotion recognition, reasoning, and routing abilities; 2) Reinforcement learning training for a two-step agentic workflow (agent selection + agent specialization); 3) Novel P-GRPO optimization combining group-based relative advantages with PMI-inspired progressive token-level modulation to address coarse-grained credit assignment.

Result: Extensive experiments on MER benchmarks demonstrate superiority in stronger emotion reasoning performance and improved optimization stability compared to existing methods.

Conclusion: The proposed EmoAgent-R1 framework successfully addresses limitations of fixed-prompt MLLM methods by introducing dynamic agent specialization through reinforcement learning, achieving better emotion recognition and reasoning capabilities with more stable optimization via the novel P-GRPO approach.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have achieved impressive performance in multimodal emotion recognition (MER) tasks and lifted MER to a new level that is complex emotion understanding with advanced video understanding abilities and natural language description. However, existing MLLM-based methods often use a fixed prompt to perceive the emotions, ignoring the dynamicity and complexity of the emotion source in the multimodal inputs. To address these issues, we propose a novel Reinforcement Learning-based Dynamic Agent Specialization framework (\textbf{EmoAgent-R1}) to optimize the emotion recognition, reasoning, and generalization abilities of an MLLM with dynamic agent specialization based on reinforcement learning. Specifically, we first adopt a cold start strategy to endow an MLLM with preliminary emotion recognition, reasoning, and agent routing ability by training with synthetic answer-conditioned chain-of-thought data and agent routing data. Then, we further train the MLLM with reinforcement learning to perceive emotions in a two-step agentic workflow with agent selection and agent specialization. To effectively train EmoAgent-R1, we propose a novel Progressive Group-Relative Policy Optimization (P-GRPO) to combine group-based relative advantages with a PMI-inspired progressive token-level modulation to transform sparse rewards into fine-grained learning signals, mitigating the coarse-grained uniform credit assignment issue in GRPO. Extensive experiments on MER benchmarks demonstrate the superiority of our EmoAgent-R1 in stronger emotion reasoning performance and improved optimization stability.

</details>


### [89] [Faster IndexTTS-2: Accelerating and Streaming Autoregressive Zero-Shot Text-to-Speech Synthesis on GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21042)
*Muyang Du,Shuang Yu,Junjie Lai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Faster IndexTTS-2 accelerates IndexTTS-2 (an autoregressive TTS model) using TensorRT optimization, achieving up to 5x GPT speedup and 3.6x end-to-end acceleration with minimal quality loss, while adding streaming and batching support for production deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive text-to-speech models like IndexTTS-2 achieve strong naturalness but suffer from slow inference due to sequential token generation, limiting their deployment in production applications that require low latency.

Method: Accelerates all neural network components of IndexTTS-2 using NVIDIA TensorRT and TensorRT-LLM for production deployment on GPUs. Also implements streaming synthesis for latency-sensitive applications and batched inference across all components to maximize GPU utilization.

Result: Experiments on Seed-TTS benchmark for English and Chinese show up to 5.0× speedup on the autoregressive GPT and 3.6× end-to-end acceleration, with minimal degradation in word error rate, speaker similarity, and naturalness.

Conclusion: The methodology provides a practical reference for efficiently accelerating similar autoregressive speech models on GPUs, making high-quality TTS models viable for production deployment with low latency requirements.

Abstract: Autoregressive text-to-speech models achieve strong naturalness but suffer from slow inference due to sequential token generation, limiting their deployment in production applications that require low latency. IndexTTS-2 is a state-of-the-art autoregressive TTS model consisting of a GPT, a flow-matching Diffusion Transformer, and a vocoder. Despite its high synthesis quality, its inference speed barely reaches real-time without streaming or batching support. We present Faster IndexTTS-2, which accelerates all neural network components of IndexTTS-2 for production deployment on GPUs using NVIDIA TensorRT and TensorRT-LLM. Faster IndexTTS-2 also enables streaming synthesis for latency-sensitive interactive applications, and batched inference across all components to maximize GPU utilization. Experiments on the Seed-TTS benchmark for both English and Chinese demonstrate up to 5.0$\times$ speedup on the autoregressive GPT and 3.6$\times$ end-to-end, with minimal degradation in word error rate, speaker similarity, and naturalness. Our methodology provides a practical reference for efficiently accelerating similar autoregressive speech models on GPUs.

</details>


### [90] [Can Generative Recommendation Reach Cold Items? A Temporal Perspective on Semantic-ID Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21101)
*Jie Peng,Yanping Zheng,Zhewei Zhe,Bin Tong,Guan Wang,Bo Zheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SID-based generative recommendation can generate new items using token recombination but struggles with truly novel atomic tokens and unsupported SID paths, showing compositional but limited open-ended generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the limitations of semantic-ID-based generative recommendation in handling temporal open-token cold-start scenarios where new items introduce unseen atomic tokens or weakly supported SID paths.

Method: Using an absolute-time temporal protocol that separates seen/unseen targets, performing seen/unseen-hit analysis, coldness taxonomy, and oracle-prefix probing to diagnose cold item reachability at token level.

Result: Current SID-based models can reach future items supported by observed tokens and prefixes, but struggle with unseen atomic tokens and unsupported SID paths. SID generation is hierarchical semantic bucketing with early tokens selecting coarse regions and later tokens refining paths.

Conclusion: SID generation is compositional but not fully open-ended, suggesting future directions including more independent SID spaces, scoring-based interfaces, and dynamic textual context.

Abstract: Semantic-ID-based generative recommendation represents items as sequences of shared semantic tokens, enabling token recombination beyond isolated item IDs. However, closed-world recombination does not necessarily imply temporal open-token cold-start induction, where new items enter the item catalog with unseen atomic tokens or weakly supported SID paths. In this work, we revisit SID-based generative recommendation under an absolute-time temporal protocol that separates seen and unseen targets and diagnoses the cold item reachability at the token level. Through seen/unseen-hit analysis, coldness taxonomy, and oracle-prefix probing, we show that current SID-based models can occasionally reach future items supported by observed tokens and prefixes, but struggle with unseen atomic tokens and unsupported SID paths. We further explain this boundary by interpreting SID generation as hierarchical semantic bucketing: early tokens select coarse semantic regions, while later tokens refine item-specific paths. These findings show that SID generation is compositional but not fully open-ended, and suggest future directions in more independent SID spaces, scoring-based interfaces, and dynamic textual context.

</details>


### [91] [AttriMem: Attribution-Guided Process Feedback for Agent Memory Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21106)
*Qinfeng Li,Yuntai Bao,Xinyan Yu,Hongze Chen,Wenqi Zhang,Xuhong Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AttriMem is an RL-based framework that uses token-level attribution feedback to learn memory-construction policies, overcoming fine-grained credit assignment bottlenecks in LLM agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current memory construction methods for LLM agents have limitations: heuristic methods rely on subjective, task-specific rules that misalign with downstream objectives and lack cross-task adaptability, while RL-based methods use coarse outcome-level rewards that can't identify which specific memory contents contributed to final answers, creating a credit assignment bottleneck.

Method: AttriMem uses an attribution-guided process-feedback framework for RL learning of memory-construction policies. It augments global outcome rewards with local rewards derived from token-level contributions to the final answer, providing fine-grained feedback about which memory contents support the answer.

Result: Experiments on long-horizon dialogue question answering show AttriMem outperforms retrieval-based, heuristic, and RL-based baselines. The method generalizes across benchmarks and answer models, and stabilizes RL optimization.

Conclusion: AttriMem successfully addresses the fine-grained credit assignment problem in memory construction by using token-level attribution feedback, enabling more effective memory policies that align with downstream objectives and improve performance across tasks.

Abstract: Effective memory is crucial for LLM agents, yet constructing it effectively remains challenging. A memory-construction policy decides what information to extract, store, update, compress, or discard as interactions accumulate. Heuristic memory methods rely on subjective, task-specific rules, which can misalign with downstream objectives and limit cross-task adaptability. RL-based methods, by contrast, learn from task feedback but mainly use outcome- or module-level rewards. These coarse signals indicate task success but cannot identify which intermediate memory contents support the final answer, creating a fine-grained credit-assignment bottleneck. However, constructing such process feedback is prohibitively difficult because intermediate memory decisions lack unique ground-truth targets, while the appropriate credit varies with the agent's uncertain reasoning trajectory and therefore cannot be specified in advance. We propose AttriMem, an attribution-guided process-feedback framework for learning memory-construction policies with RL. AttriMem augments the global outcome reward with local rewards derived from token-level contributions to the final answer. Experiments on long-horizon dialogue question answering show that AttriMem outperforms retrieval-based, heuristic, and RL-based baselines, generalizes across benchmarks and answer models, stabilizes RL optimization.

</details>


### [92] [V-DEAL: Diagnosing Video Safety De-Calibration as an Understanding-Refusal Coupling Failure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21151)
*Zhetong Zhang,Honghao Fu,Miao Xu,Yiwei Wang,Yujun Cai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Video LLMs are vulnerable when harmful videos are paired with benign queries, achieving higher attack success rates than with explicitly harmful queries. The V-DEAL framework diagnoses this vulnerability across behavior, understanding, and internal representations, revealing that visual understanding activates weaker refusal tendencies than text. A prompt injection intervention method effectively reduces attack success rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Video Large Language Models are increasingly deployed in real-world applications, making safety alignment critical. The paper identifies a counterintuitive vulnerability: harmful videos paired with benign queries achieve higher attack success rates than the same videos with explicitly harmful queries, prompting investigation into the underlying mechanism.

Method: The authors present V-DEAL, a three-level diagnostic framework that analyzes failure across model behavior, understanding, and internal representations. They test six Video LLMs on three public benchmarks and use hidden-state analysis to examine refusal tendencies. They also introduce a prompt injection intervention method to address the vulnerability.

Result: Models correctly recognize harmful video content with over 81% accuracy, yet average attack success rate reaches 48.33% when harmful videos are paired with benign queries. Hidden-state analysis shows visual understanding activates weaker refusal tendency than textual understanding. The prompt injection intervention reduces attack success rates by an average of 48.24 percentage points, achieving performance comparable to fine-tuning methods.

Conclusion: Video LLMs have a specific vulnerability where harmful content in videos paired with benign queries bypasses safety mechanisms more effectively than explicit harmful queries. The V-DEAL framework provides diagnostic insights, and prompt injection offers an effective practical intervention. This highlights the need for safety alignment strategies that address multimodal vulnerabilities beyond text-only approaches.

Abstract: As Video Large Language Models are increasingly deployed in real-world applications, ensuring their safety alignment has become critical. Counterintuitively, we find that harmful videos paired with benign queries achieve higher attack success rates than the same videos paired with explicitly harmful queries. To understand the underlying mechanism of this vulnerability, we present V-DEAL, a three-level diagnostic framework that jointly analyzes this failure across model behaviour, understanding, and internal representations. By progressively ruling out perception failure and quantifying the model's internal refusal tendency, V-DEAL provides a new diagnostic perspective for analyzing the underlying mechanism of the observed vulnerability. We tested six Video LLMs on three public benchmarks and observed that models correctly recognize harmful video content with over 81\% accuracy, yet the average attack success rate still reaches 48.33\% under the condition pairing harmful videos with benign queries. Hidden-state analysis further shows that visual understanding activates a weaker refusal tendency than textual understanding. Furthermore, we introduce a prompt injection intervention method that reduces attack success rates by an average of 48.24 percentage points and achieves performance comparable to prior fine-tuning-based methods, providing an effective and practical means to address such safety risks in Video LLMs.

</details>


### [93] [Safeguards for Speech2Speech LLM-Assistants: A Case Study in Automotive Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21180)
*Gregor Endler,Sebastian Kraus,Lukas Stappen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: S2S conversational assistants enable humanlike in-car dialogue but lack effective guardrails; transcript-based and tool-based approaches both have prohibitive latency and technical issues.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While speech-to-speech conversational assistants provide natural in-car dialogue experiences with non-verbal cues, their end-to-end nature limits architectural options for implementing programmable domain-specific safeguards (guardrails).

Method: The paper discusses and empirically evaluates two implementation approaches for S2S guardrails: transcript-based (analyzing speech transcripts) and tool-based (using external tools).

Result: Both strategies are insufficient for industrial deployment due to prohibitive latency (0-1.4 second delays even for cheap checks) and technical impediments like potentially non-deterministic tool call behavior.

Conclusion: Current S2S guardrail approaches face fundamental challenges in automotive applications, and the paper outlines open challenges for future research in this domain.

Abstract: Recent advances have introduced speech-to-speech (S2S) conversational assistants capable of producing natural-sounding interactions, including non-verbal cues like tonality and mood. In the automotive domain, this enables intuitive and humanlike in-car dialogue experiences. However, integrating these end-to-end assistants limits architectural options for programmable domain-specific safeguards. This paper discusses two implementation approaches for S2S guardrails: transcript-based and tool-based. Through an empirical evaluation, we demonstrate that both strategies are insufficient for industrial deployment in most cases due to prohibitive latency (delaying each answer by 0 to 1.4 seconds even for computationally cheap checks) and technical impediments (like potentially non-deterministic tool call behavior). Finally, we outline open challenges for S2S guardrails in the automotive context.

</details>


### [94] [Explaining Weather Bulletins via ILP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21184)
*Enrico Santi,Alessandro Dal Palù,Agostino Dovier,Talissa Dreossi,Andrea Formisano*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Using ILP to generate interpretable hypotheses that explain human experts' weather forecast decisions by analyzing meteorological data and bulletin pictograms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To create interpretable explanations for complex weather forecasting decisions made by human experts at OSMER FVG, helping to clarify the rationale behind specific symbols used in meteorological bulletin pictograms.

Method: Developed a pipeline that: 1) starts with simulated meteorological raw data and existing bulletins as ground truth, 2) extracts data as ASP facts and generates ILP examples, 3) uses FastLAS2 ILP framework to infer explanatory hypotheses, 4) translates hypotheses into natural language.

Result: Successfully generated interpretable hypotheses that explain why human experts choose specific symbols in weather forecast pictograms, with a general approach applicable to different regions and bulletin sources.

Conclusion: The proposed ILP-based approach effectively bridges machine learning and expert knowledge in meteorology, providing transparent explanations for complex forecasting decisions while maintaining generalizability across different applications.

Abstract: Inductive Logic Programming (ILP) originated within the Logic Programming community in the Nineties as a framework for combining symbolic learning with declarative knowledge representation. Nowadays, mature ILP frameworks exist and they are capable of learning complex, non-monotonic hypotheses, thus broadening both the modeling capabilities and the scope of real-world applications of ILP. This work is primarily based on the FastLAS2 framework and aims to generate simple, interpretable hypotheses to help clarify the weather bulletins issued by OSMER FVG, the Regional Meteorological Observatory of the Italian region of Friuli Venezia-Giulia. In this paper we present a pipeline that, starting from simulated meteorological raw data and from OSMERs' bulletins (used as ground truth), extracts data as ASP facts and generates ILP examples. From such examples an explanatory hypothesis is then inferred via FastLAS2. Such a hypothesis (translated into natural language) explains the weather forecast issued by human experts, and in particular the rationale behind experts' choices of specific symbols in the bulletin pictogram (the symbol-annotated meteorological map of the forecast). The proposed approach is general, not specific to any particular region and it can equally be applied to bulletins from other sources and to different regions.

</details>


### [95] [Differentiable Logic Programming to Mitigate Reasoning Shortcuts in Neurosymbolic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21185)
*Akihiro Takemura,Katsumi Inoue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper addresses reasoning shortcuts in neurosymbolic systems by proposing matrix-based differentiable logic programming to mitigate constraint satisfaction and cognition shortcuts through improved architectural design and one-to-one neural-to-logical grounding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neurosymbolic systems combine neural networks with logical reasoning for generalization and interpretability, but they are vulnerable to shortcut reasoning behaviors where constraints are satisfied without achieving the intended task, or biased data leads to semantically incorrect concept mappings despite logically sound inference.

Method: The authors propose a novel method using matrix-based differentiable logic programming, building on recent matrix-based logic programming semantics. They introduce design elements including a unified encoding of rules and constraints in a single matrix, explore connections to fuzzy logic t-norms, and use one-to-one grounding of neural outputs to logical atoms.

Result: Experiments on MNIST variants show that one-to-one grounding of neural outputs to logical atoms significantly reduces both constraint satisfaction shortcuts and cognition shortcuts compared to previous methods using soft probability distributions. Architectural choices in coupling symbolic knowledge with neural learning play a critical role in shortcut mitigation.

Conclusion: Proper architectural design in neurosymbolic systems, particularly through matrix-based differentiable logic programming with one-to-one neural-to-logical grounding, is critical for mitigating reasoning shortcuts and achieving both the generalization benefits of neural networks and the interpretability benefits of logical reasoning.

Abstract: Neurosymbolic (NeSy) systems integrate neural networks with logical reasoning to achieve both generalization and interpretability, but recent work has shown they are susceptible to shortcut reasoning behaviors.  We propose a novel method using matrix-based differentiable logic programming to mitigate reasoning shortcuts in two phenomena: constraint satisfaction shortcuts, where constraints are satisfied without achieving the intended task, and cognition shortcuts, where biased data leads to semantically incorrect concept mappings despite logically sound inference. Building on recent matrix-based logic programming semantics, we introduce design elements to mitigate shortcuts, including a unified encoding of rules and constraints in a single matrix.  We also identify connections to fuzzy logic t-norms and empirically compare their gradient flow properties. Through carefully designed experiments on MNIST variants, we show that one-to-one grounding of neural outputs to logical atoms significantly reduces both shortcut types compared to previous methods that rely on soft probability distributions.  We then confirm that architectural choices in coupling symbolic knowledge with neural learning play a critical role in shortcut mitigation.

</details>


### [96] [Identifying Good Rules for Efficient SAT Encodings of Single-Constant Multiplication Using Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21188)
*Chufeng Jiang,Neng-Fa Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A neuro-symbolic framework combining graph neural networks with symbolic search to dramatically accelerate SAT encoding for the Single Constant Multiplication problem in hardware design, achieving orders of magnitude improvements in speed and memory usage while preserving near-optimal solution quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Single Constant Multiplication (SCM) problem is NP-hard and fundamental to hardware design, involving decomposing fixed constants using only additions, subtractions, and bit-shifts. While dynamic programming methods can produce near-optimal SAT encodings for SCM, their encoding cost remains prohibitively high for large constants, limiting scalability.

Method: Proposes a neuro-symbolic framework that uses a graph neural network (GNN) model to predict promising operator types from constant decompositions. The framework exploits resulting confidence scores to prune no-good choices in symbolic search, guiding operator selection during decomposition to accelerate the SAT encoding process.

Result: On unseen 17-32 bit constants: 1-2 orders of magnitude reduction in encoding time, over 97% reduction in memory usage, order-of-magnitude decrease in branching, while preserving near-optimal encoding quality in terms of number of additions.

Conclusion: Learning-guided symbolic strategies can significantly improve the scalability and efficiency of SCM encoding. The neuro-symbolic approach demonstrates that machine learning can effectively guide symbolic search in hard optimization problems, with promising applications in hardware design automation.

Abstract: The Single Constant Multiplication problem is a fundamental NP-hard optimization task in hardware design, which seeks to decompose a fixed constant using only additions, subtractions, and bit-shifts. Although dynamic programming methods can produce near-optimal SAT encodings for SCM, their encoding cost remains high for large constants. We propose a neuro-symbolic framework that accelerates SCM SAT encoding by identifying good rules for guiding operator selection during decomposition. Our approach employs a graph neural network model to predict promising operator types from constant decompositions, and exploits the resulting confidence scores to prune no-good choices in the symbolic search. Experimental results on unseen 17-32 bit constants demonstrate one to two orders of magnitude reductions in encoding time, over 97% reduction in memory usage, and an order-of-magnitude decrease in branching, while preserving near-optimal encoding quality in terms of additions. These results show that learning-guided symbolic strategies can significantly improve the scalability and efficiency of SCM encoding. Our code and data are publicly available at: https://github.com/Chufeng-Jiang/SCM_MLDP

</details>


### [97] [Bound-Founded Semantics for Answer Set Programming with Difference Constraints: Preliminary Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21201)
*Pedro Cabalar,Jorge Fandinno,Nicolas Rühling,Torsten Schaub,Sebastian Schellhorn,Philipp Wanko*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A unified logical framework for ASP with linear constraints that characterizes various semantics and analyzes different hybrid solvers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing hybrid ASP solvers with linear constraints lack a unified logical foundation, relying on disparate semantic underpinnings that create fragmentation in the field.

Method: Develop a many-sorted variant of Bound-founded Logic of Here-and-There (HTb) to create a versatile framework for characterizing equilibrium models across ASP extensions with linear constraints, with specific application to difference constraints and clingo[DL]. Formalize foundedness for numeric variables.

Result: The framework successfully characterizes semantic differences between hybrid systems (clingo[DL], clingcon, flingo) and provides a unified logical foundation that explains their varying behaviors when justifying constraint atoms.

Conclusion: The proposed framework not only formalizes foundations of current systems like clingo[DL] but also enables rigorous study of program simplifications and future integration of diverse semantic principles, providing a consistent basis for ASP with constraints.

Abstract: While the integration of linear constraints has significantly expanded the reach of Answer Set Programming (ASP), existing hybrid solvers often rely on disparate semantic underpinnings that lack a unified logical foundation. We address this gap by introducing a many-sorted variant of the Bound-founded Logic of Here-and-There (HTb), providing a versatile framework capable of characterizing equilibrium models across a wide spectrum of alternative semantics for extensions of ASP with linear constraints. We apply this framework to the setting of difference constraints, focusing on the semantic characterization of clingo[DL]. Central to our approach is the formalization of foundedness for numeric variables. By investigating how different hybrid systems - such as clingo[DL], clingcon, and flingo - justify constraint atoms, we uncover the semantic roots of their varying behaviors. This investigation results in a single, consistent framework that not only formalizes the foundations of current systems like clingo[DL] but also facilitates the rigorous study of program simplifications and the future integration of diverse semantic principles.

</details>


### [98] [A New Well-Supported Semantics for Description Logic Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21203)
*Spencer Killen,Jia-Huai You*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new semantics for description logic programs that maintains well-supportedness while being computationally simpler (NP-complete) than the current approach, with a reduct-based characterization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The current well-supported semantics for description logic programs has two main limitations: increased computational complexity (moves from NP-complete to second level of polynomial hierarchy for consistency problem) and lacks a reduct transformation characterization.

Method: Proposes a new semantics that evaluates ontological atoms more strictly than current semantics. Identifies a syntactic class of DL programs where new semantics equals current semantics. Characterizes the semantics using a fixpoint operator and reduct-based transformation.

Result: The new semantics keeps computational complexity NP-complete (not increasing to higher polynomial hierarchy), provides a reduct-based characterization, and maintains well-supportedness while being a strict subset of current well-supported semantics.

Conclusion: The new semantics is preferred due to its similarities with logic programming, maintaining well-supportedness while addressing computational complexity and characterization issues of the current approach.

Abstract: Description logic programs are a powerful formalism for combining rules with ontologies. The well-supported semantics for description logic programs ensures that no answer sets rely on cyclic dependencies. Most popular semantics for logic programming have this property of well-supportedness. We recognize two limitations of the current well-supported semantics for DL programs: its increased computational complexity for the consistency problem and its lack of a reduct transformation characterization.  In this work, we present a new semantics which evaluates ontological atoms more strictly than the current semantics. This keeps the complexity of its consistency problem NP-complete, rather than increasing it to the second level of the polynomial hierarchy. Additionally, we identify a syntactic class of description logic programs for which our new semantics is equivalent to the current semantics. We characterize our semantics using a fixpoint operator and a reduct-based transformation. Our new semantics is a strict subset of the current well-supported semantics, so it maintains the prior notion  of well-supportedness while inducing its own stricter notion. We prefer our new notion of well-supportedness due to its similarities with logic programming.

</details>


### [99] [How Rules Represent Causal Knowledge: Causal Modeling with Probabilistic Logic Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21208)
*Kilian Rueckschloss,Felix Weitkaemper*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper brings Pearl's causal reasoning framework into probabilistic logic programming (PLP), proposing a formal causal semantics with interventions that works without temporal assumptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pearl's causal theory is limited to Bayesian networks and causal models, primarily handling acyclic relationships. The motivation is to extend Pearl's approach to probabilistic logic programming formalisms, addressing the risk of misinterpretation when transferring causal concepts to other frameworks.

Method: Aligns probabilistic logic programs with philosophical foundations that don't rely on temporal notions (all events assumed simultaneous). Proposes a formal causal semantics for these programs, including a notion of intervention, and implements this approach.

Result: Shows that the proposed causal semantics coincides with P-log semantics for stratified ProbLog programs. However, the two semantics may differ in the non-stratified case and for other PLP formalisms.

Conclusion: The paper successfully extends Pearl's causal reasoning to probabilistic logic programming, providing a formal foundation for causal inference in PLP that maintains consistency with Pearl's approach while addressing limitations of previous formalisms.

Abstract: Pearl famously argues that causal knowledge enables the prediction of intervention effects. By contrast, purely descriptive knowledge supports only conclusions drawn from observations. His theory of causality, however, is developed exclusively within Bayesian networks and causal models. Consequently, it is largely restricted to acyclic causal relationships, and transferring its ideas to other formalisms risks misinterpretation or inconsistency.
  This paper brings Pearl's approach to causality into probabilistic logic programming (PLP). To this end, such programs are aligned with philosophical foundations established in prior work that do not rely on temporal notions; that is, all relevant events are assumed to occur simultaneously. A formal causal semantics for these programs, together with a notion of intervention and an implementation, is proposed. It is shown that this semantics coincides with the P-log semantics for stratified ProbLog programs, while the two may differ in the non-stratified case and for other PLP formalisms.

</details>


### [100] [ICAE-Bench: Evaluating Coding Agents as Interactive Project Builders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21217)
*Zhongyuan Peng,Dan Huang,Chuyu Zhang,Caijun Xu,Changyi Xiao,Shibo Hong,David Lo,Lin Qiu,Xuezhi Cao,Jiyuan He,Yixin Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new benchmark called ICAE-Bench evaluates coding agents in interactive project-building scenarios with fuzzy requirements, using automated user simulations and multi-dimensional evaluation metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing coding benchmarks evaluate agents on static, fully specified tasks, but real-world "vibe-coding" workflows require agents to transform incomplete product intent into working software through planning, clarification, tool use, and debugging.

Method: ICAE-Bench creates interactive project-building settings starting from fuzzy product requirements with an automated User Agent. It uses real open-source repositories as task sources, grounds interaction through User Agent Data to reveal hidden constraints, and employs standardized black-box tests with multi-dimensional diagnostics.

Result: The benchmark provides a realistic and evaluable framework for assessing coding agents' abilities in interactive project construction, with comprehensive evaluation covering functional correctness, semantic/API similarity, structural fidelity, design quality, and interaction quality.

Conclusion: ICAE-Bench addresses the gap between current static benchmarks and real-world interactive coding workflows, enabling better evaluation of modern coding agents' capabilities in transforming incomplete requirements into working software.

Abstract: The recent emergence of vibe-coding workflows is changing what coding agents are expected to do. Instead of merely completing code under fully specified instructions, agents are increasingly expected to transform incomplete product intent into working software by combining various abilities including planning, requirement clarification, tool use, debugging, and repository-level construction. Yet existing benchmarks have not fully caught up with this shift, evaluating agents on static, fully specified tasks.
  In this paper, we introduce ICAE-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating coding agents under interactive project-building settings. The basic idea is to start from a fuzzy product requirement, simulating the dynamic paradigm with an automated User Agent. To make this setting both realistic and evaluable, ICAE-Bench introduces three key designs. First, to avoid the ambiguity of unconstrained fuzzy requirements, each task derives ambiguity from a precise real open-source repository with executable behavior. Second, to ensure high-quality and reproducible user simulation, ICAE-Bench grounds interaction through User Agent Data, allowing the User Agent to reveal hidden constraints without inventing new requirements or leaking implementation artifacts. Third, to evaluate open-ended repositories fairly, ICAE-Bench uses standardized black-box tests together with multi-dimensional diagnostics, including functional correctness, semantic and API similarity, structural fidelity, design quality, and interaction quality.

</details>


### [101] [Logic Programming Semantics for Causal Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21233)
*Felix Weitkämper*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that stable models of positive logic programs correspond to eventual states from neutral starting points, while supported models correspond to eventual states from arbitrary starting points, adding a temporal perspective to logic programming semantics for causal modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research is motivated by challenging modeling issues in the life sciences, specifically investigating the relationship between logic programming semantics and the eventual states of causal processes compatible with those logic programs.

Method: The authors investigate the correspondence between logic programming semantics (stable models and supported models) and the eventual states of causal processes. They analyze how these different semantics relate to processes starting from different initial conditions (neutral state vs. arbitrary starting points).

Result: The paper shows that stable models of positive logic programs correspond to the eventual states of processes commencing from a neutral state and continuing undisturbed indefinitely, while supported models describe the eventual states reachable from arbitrary starting points.

Conclusion: This work contributes to the discussion of appropriate semantics for logic programming as a causal rule language by adding a temporal perspective to recent interpretations of stable and supported model semantics from an explanatory viewpoint of causality.

Abstract: Motivated by challenging modelling issues in the life sciences, we investigate the relationship between logic programming semantics and the eventual states of causal processes compatible with those logic programs. More precisely, we show that while stable models of positive logic programs correspond to the eventual states of processes commencing from a neutral state and continuing undisturbed indefinitely, supported models describe the eventual states reachable from arbitrary starting points. This also contributes to the discussion of the appropriate semantics for logic programming as a causal rule language, adding a temporal perspective to recent interpretations of the stable and supported model semantics from an explanatory viewpoint of causality.

</details>


### [102] [BasketEvent: Understanding Who Did What and When in Basketball Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21267)
*Yu Zhang,Jiayuan Rao,Haoning Wu,Weidi Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BasketEvent dataset and PlayNet framework for player-centric basketball event understanding with spatial and temporal grounding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing basketball video analysis methods treat spatial perception and semantic recognition as isolated tasks, failing to ground events to individual players or pinpoint their temporal boundaries within complex collective dynamics.

Method: PlayNet: a player-centric reasoning framework that tracks key entities, associates player identities, and reasons about events by modeling player-player, player-ball, and global court interactions, while aggregating sparse temporal evidence via gated pooling.

Result: PlayNet significantly outperforms representative video-level and crop-based baselines, proving the superiority of player-centric modeling for fine-grained sports video understanding.

Conclusion: Player-centric modeling with temporal evidence localization enables more comprehensive basketball video understanding by resolving what events occur, who is responsible, and when key evidence appears.

Abstract: Comprehensive basketball video understanding requires resolving not only what event occurs, but also who is responsible and when the key evidence appears. However, exist- ing methods typically treat spatial perception and semantic recognition as isolated tasks, failing to ground events to individual players or pinpoint their temporal boundaries within complex collective dynamics. To bridge this gap, we introduce BasketEvent, a player- centric basketball event understanding dataset curated from real NBA broadcasts. In BasketEvent, event labels are grounded to the responsible players, and a manually an- notated subset of 1,000 samples with precise event intervals is provided to evaluate tem- poral evidence localization. Based on this data, we propose PlayNet, a player-centric reasoning framework that maps basketball videos to player-level event predictions with temporal evidence. Concretely, PlayNet tracks key entities, associates player identities, and reasons about events by modeling player-player, player-ball, and global court inter- actions, while aggregating sparse temporal evidence via gated pooling. Extensive experi- ments demonstrate that PlayNet significantly outperforms representative video-level and crop-based baselines, proving the superiority of player-centric modeling for fine-grained sports video understanding. Our data, code, and models will be made publicly available.

</details>


### [103] [An LLM-Driven Workflow for Automated Process Control Strategy Generation and Tuning from Dynamic Process Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21292)
*Ari Luna Rueda,Eike Cramer,Klaus Hellgardt,Mehmet Mercangöz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A structured LLM-driven workflow for automated multi-variable control design that generates executable control structures and tuning environments through code generation with validation loops.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To automate the complex, multi-variable control design process from dynamic process models, reducing manual engineering effort and potential errors while ensuring physical consistency.

Method: A workflow that decomposes control design into steps: plant-interface construction, normalization, MV-CV pairing, controller specification, closed-loop simulation, scenario generation, performance evaluation, and Bayesian optimization tuning. The system uses validation feedback loops to repair failed code artifacts.

Result: Successfully applied to a nonlinear gas-preheater benchmark with coupled pressure and temperature dynamics. The workflow produced a physically consistent decentralized PI feedback-feedforward control structure and executable tuning environment. Bayesian optimization reduced closed-loop performance objective by 26.5% relative to the initial controller.

Conclusion: The approach demonstrates feasibility of using structured LLM-based code generation for executable control-design workflows, but highlights need for broader validation on larger plantwide-control benchmarks.

Abstract: We present a structured large-language-model-driven workflow for automated multi-variable control design from dynamic process models. The workflow decomposes the design task into constrained code-generation steps: plant-interface construction, normalization, manipulated-variable controlled-variable (MV-CV) pairing, controller specification, closed loop simulation, scenario generation, performance evaluation and Bayesian-optimization (BO) based tuning. Generated artifacts are executed and validated before downstream tasks proceed, and failed artifacts are repaired using validation feedback. The proposed approach is demonstrated on a nonlinear gas-preheater benchmark with coupled pressure and temperature dynamics. The generated workflow produces a physically consistent decentralized PI (proportional-integral) feedback-feedforward control structure and an executable tuning environment. Bayesian optimization reduces the closed loop performance objective, which aggregates set-point tracking and disturbance-rejection errors for the controlled variables, by approximately 26.5% relative to the initial controller generated by the workflow, mainly through improved pressure-loop transient performance. This figure quantifies the automated tuning stage rather than a comparison against a manually designed controller.
