The results demonstrate the feasibility of using structured large-language-model-based code generation to construct executable control-design workflows, while also highlighting the need for broader validation on larger plantwide-control benchmarks.

</details>


### [104] [Expert Behavior Prior Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21302)
*Gong Gao,Weidong Zhao,Xianhui Liu,Ning Jia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EBP algorithm uses Q-CVAE to generate expert policy priors from online replay buffer, enabling high-value action guidance without offline datasets, combined with EPG and PGC for stable policy improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Behavior Prior RL methods rely on static offline datasets with low diversity and suboptimal trajectories, restricting policy exploitation and stability during online training, leading to inefficient exploration and unstable learning dynamics.

Method: Proposes Expert Behavior Prior (EBP) algorithm with: 1) Q-guided conditional variational autoencoder (Q-CVAE) that learns to generate expert policy priors from online replay buffer, 2) Expert policy guidance (EPG) mechanism for selecting expert actions from generative support set, 3) Policy gradient correction (PGC) module to harmonize Q-guidance with expert supervision.

Result: Extensive experiments on robotic control (Gym, PyBullet) and industrial control (DMControl) benchmarks show EBP significantly outperforms state-of-the-art online RL algorithms, achieving higher sample efficiency and more stable convergence.

Conclusion: The EBP algorithm successfully addresses limitations of static offline datasets in BPRL by generating expert priors from online experience, enabling more efficient and stable reinforcement learning through the combination of Q-CVAE, EPG, and PGC modules.

Abstract: Behavior prior reinforcement learning (BPRL) has emerged as a promising paradigm to improve sample efficiency in online reinforcement learning (RL) by leveraging policy priors derived from offline demonstrations. However, most existing BPRL methods rely on static offline datasets, which often suffer from low data diversity and suboptimal trajectory quality. This reliance restricts the effectiveness of policy priors, hindering both policy exploitation and stability during online training. Consequently, agents are prone to inefficient exploration and unstable learning dynamics. To address these limitations, we deviate from existing offline pre-training methods and propose an Expert Behavior Prior (EBP) algorithm. Specifically, we introduce a Q-guided conditional variational autoencoder (Q-CVAE) that learns to generate expert policy priors directly from the online replay buffer. This enables the generation of high-value actions for guiding policy updates without relying on pre-collected expert trajectories. To further enhance policy exploitation, we propose an expert policy guidance (EPG) mechanism that selects expert actions from a generative support set, and we integrate a policy gradient correction (PGC) module to harmonize Q-guidance with expert supervision, promoting stable and consistent policy improvement. Extensive experiments conducted on robotic control (Gym, PyBullet) and industrial control (DMControl) benchmarks demonstrate that EBP significantly outperforms state-of-the-art online RL algorithms, achieving higher sample efficiency and more stable convergence.

</details>


### [105] [Regulating autonomous and agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21345)
*Chris Reed,Alex Austria,Anmol Bharuka,Pragnitha Mandava,Khushiya Mujawar,Luka Shakhkulashvili*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Regulating autonomous AI requires shifting regulation from reactive to proactive, focusing on AI supply chains and developing new governance systems beyond traditional models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional regulatory assumptions about regulatee knowledge and control break down when dealing with autonomous and agentic AI, as much control lies in the AI supply chain rather than with end-users, creating new systemic risks that require novel regulatory approaches.

Method: The paper analyzes four existing regulatory systems: UK content platform regulation, data protection laws, UK financial services regulation, and the EU AI Act's cross-sectoral regime. It examines how these frameworks handle autonomous AI challenges and proposes adaptations.

Result: The analysis identifies that retrospective supervisory oversight becomes ineffective for autonomous AI risk management, and proposes solutions to transform regulation from reactive to active processes that can adapt to AI autonomy challenges.

Conclusion: Regulators need fundamentally new approaches that bring AI supply chains within regulatory scope, address systemic risks created by AI autonomy, and develop proactive governance systems rather than relying on traditional oversight models.

Abstract: Regulating activities where regulatees use autonomous and agentic AI is challenging. Regulatory assumptions about regulatee knowledge and control no longer hold true; much of that lies elsewhere in the AI supply chain which thus needs to be brought within the scope of regulation. Governance systems for autonomous AI cannot replicate existing governance models, but need a fresh approach. Retrospective supervisory oversight becomes ineffective as a risk management tool, and AI autonomy generates new systemic risks which require new solutions. This paper investigate four regulatory systems: UK regulation of content platforms, data protection, UK financial services, and the EU AI Act\'92s cross-sectoral regime. It analyses the challenges posed by autonomous and agentic AI and proposes potential solutions which regulators might adopt. These will transform regulation from a reactive process to an active one, and assist it in adapting to the challenges of AI autonomy.

</details>


### [106] [SPORD: A Simulation-Propose-then-OR-Dispose Approach for Supply Chain Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21354)
*Jiayin He,Yutong Pan,Sen Yang,Ningxuan Kang,Yongzhi Qi,Jianshen Zhang,Wei Qi,Zuo-Jun Max Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SPORD method (Simulation-Propose-then-OR-Dispose) combines simulation and optimization to solve supply chain planning challenges in e-commerce, deployed as JD.com's NetSim platform for faster computation and better executive adoption.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Supply chain planning at e-commerce firms suffers from operational fragmentation (bespoke models), computational intractability (combinatorial scale), and implementation hurdles (executive distrust), leading to inefficient, siloed planning processes.

Method: SPORD method decouples simulation and optimization: simulation proposes valid candidate paths (absorbing business logic), while integer programming disposes by selecting globally optimal subset. Uses matrix-vectorized CPU/GPU acceleration and list scheduling algorithms.

Result: Achieved 10-100x speedup over serial methods, reduced coupled-order processing from hours to minutes. Since 2025, optimized services for 20,000+ suppliers, reduced cross-regional fulfillment rate from 6.1% to 4.9%, with average monthly carbon reduction of 5,745 tCO2e.

Conclusion: SPORD transforms simulation from monitoring to active planning, creates transparent outputs that build executive trust, and establishes modular architecture for configuration-based (rather than reconstruction-based) future planning.

Abstract: For years, supply chain planning at e-commerce firms has operated as a collection of isolated projects. Each planning task from static network planning to dynamic warehouse assortment planning requires analysts to spend weeks building models from scratch, calibrating and persuading executives to act on outputs they cannot verify. Three barriers drive this: bespoke models proliferate because standardization is difficult (operational fragmentation); once unified, the combinatorial scale of millions of SKUs, thousands of nodes, and intricate routing logic exceeds what solvers can handle within a tight window (computational intractability); and a mathematically optimal solution still fails to be implemented if the executives do not trust it (implementation hurdle). To bridge this gap, we propose and implement the Simulation-Propose-then-OR-Dispose method, deployed as JD.com's NetSim platform. The central insight is decoupling: simulation proposes by generating and evaluating the full set of operationally valid candidate paths-absorbing all idiosyncratic business logic, while an integer program disposes by selecting the globally optimal subset. Computationally, matrix-vectorized CPU/GPU accelerated simulation achieves a 10-100 times speedup over serial methods, and a list scheduling algorithm reduces coupled-order processing from hours to minutes. Operationally, we establish a closed loop via an intelligent diagnosis engine. Since 2025, NetSim has optimized end to-end services for over 20,000 suppliers, the cross-regional fulfillment rate dropped from 6.1% to 4.9%, and the average monthly carbon reduction is approximately 5,745 tCO2e. SPORD moves simulation from monitoring to active planning. The transparent outputs turn skeptical executives into engaged collaborators, and the modular architecture ensures that the next planning requires just configuration, not reconstruction.

</details>


### [107] [Towards Faithful Graph Explanations with Synergistic Edge Effects via Granular Balls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21381)
*Jiancu Chen,Shuyin Xia,Guan Wang,Degang Chen,Fan Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SeeExplainer is a parameter-free graph neural network explainer that captures synergistic edge effects through granular-ball graph refinement and structural perturbation to generate more accurate instance-level explanations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current GNN explanation methods often select important edges individually by perturbing them and observing prediction changes, but they fail to account for synergistic effects among edges, which are crucial for accurately characterizing edge importance in graph structures.

Method: 1) Introduces granular-ball graph refinement mechanism that decomposes a graph into several disjoint granular-balls of varying sizes, then uses them as nodes to construct a structural graph that better captures edge synergies. 2) Perturbs nodes and edges in this structural graph to generate explanatory subgraphs based on their respective contributions.

Result: Experiments on several graph classification datasets across different networks demonstrate that SeeExplainer outperforms state-of-the-art baseline explanation methods.

Conclusion: SeeExplainer effectively addresses the limitation of previous methods by capturing synergistic edge effects through its granular-ball refinement approach, resulting in more accurate and meaningful explanations for GNN decisions on specific graph instances.

Abstract: Instance-level explanations aim to reveal the rationale behind a model's decisions for a specific graph. Previous methods explain graph neural networks (GNNs) by selecting important edges to induce subgraphs, where edge importance is assessed by perturbing each edge and observing changes in the model predictions. However, they often neglect the synergistic effects among edges, which are crucial for accurately characterizing edge importance. To address this issue, we propose SeeExplainer, a parameter-free explainer to interpret GNNs. Specifically, we first introduce a granular-ball graph refinement mechanism that decomposes a graph into several disjoint granular-balls with no fixed size, and utilize them as nodes to construct a structural graph. This process can better capture the synergistic effects among edges. Then, we perturb nodes and edges in the structural graph to generate explanatory subgraphs based on their respective contributions. Experiments on several graph classification datasets of different networks show that SeeExplainer outperforms state-of-the-art baselines.

</details>


### [108] [Multimodal Pretraining for Generalizable EEG Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21384)
*Targol Bakhtiarvand,Jugal Kalita,Adham Atyabi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multimodal EEG foundation model combining Mamba architecture for raw signals, ViT for time-frequency data, and text encoders achieves state-of-the-art seizure detection with strong generalization across datasets and patient-independent settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current EEG models for epilepsy are limited to specific datasets and tasks, making them difficult to apply across different datasets and clinical situations. There's a need for adaptable EEG backbones that can support various EEG-related tasks through foundation models and self-supervised learning.

Method: Developed a multimodal EEG foundation model with three encoders: 1) Mamba-based raw signal encoder, 2) Vision Transformer-style encoder for time-frequency data, and 3) lightweight text encoder, all in a shared embedding space. Pretrained using masked modeling, cross-view contrastive alignment, and temporal consistency losses for seizure-relevant representations without labeled data.

Result: On CHB-MIT benchmark: best single model achieved AUROC 0.874, ensemble variant 0.878 (state-of-the-art). Under leave-one-subject-out protocol (rarely reported previously): mean balanced accuracy 0.558 across 19 subjects. Model enabled robust seizure detection, adaptation to new scenarios, and interpretable seizure localization across datasets.

Conclusion: The multimodal EEG foundation model provides an adaptable backbone for various EEG tasks, particularly epilepsy, achieving strong performance and generalization while supporting interpretability and adaptation to new seizure detection scenarios without requiring extensive labeled data.

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) models used for epilepsy are often limited to specific datasets and tasks. This limited approach can make it challenging to apply these models across different datasets or in various situations. However, recent studies in foundation models and self-supervised learning suggest that an adaptable EEG backbone could support a range of EEG related tasks. In this study, we have developed a multimodal EEG foundation model that combines a raw signal encoder based on the Mamba architecture, a Vision Transformer (ViT)-style encoder for time-frequency data, and a lightweight encoder for text, all within a shared embedding space. The pretraining process relies on several innovative techniques, such as masked modeling, cross-view contrastive alignment, and temporal consistency losses. These methods are designed to create rich, seizure-relevant representations without requiring labeled data. To assess the efficacy and generalization of our pretrained model, we fine-tuned it on the canonical CHB-MIT seizure detection benchmark and additional seizure detection datasets, and conducted extensive experiments comparing different model variants. On the standard CHB-MIT split, our best single model achieved an AUROC of 0.874, and an ensemble variant reached 0.878 AUROC, representing state-of-the-art performance on this benchmark. In addition to standard train-test splits, we evaluated performance under a leave-one-subject-out (LOSO) protocol, which is rarely reported in prior EEG seizure modeling work and highlights the difficulty of patient-independent seizure detection, with a mean LOSO balanced accuracy of 0.558 across 19 subjects. Across datasets and evaluation settings, our multimodal foundation model enabled robust seizure detection and straightforward adaptation to new seizure detection scenarios, while also supporting interpretable seizure localization.

</details>


### [109] [MSBraM: A Multi-scale Self-supervised Brain Foundation Model for Hierarchical EEG Dynamics Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21402)
*Tao Zhou,Jing Han,Lingyu Shu,Zixing Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MSBraM is a multi-scale self-supervised foundation model for EEG analysis that uses a two-stage pretraining approach with multi-scale tokenization and curriculum masking to capture hierarchical temporal patterns, achieving state-of-the-art performance across 10 downstream tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing EEG foundation models fail to capture the multi-scale temporal structure of EEG signals, where both local neural patterns and long-range dependencies contain important task-relevant information. This limitation hinders cross-scale representation learning and generalization across diverse downstream applications.

Method: Two-stage pretraining framework: (1) Multi-scale neural tokenizer discretizes raw EEG signals into semantic codes at different temporal resolutions using vector-quantized reconstruction; (2) Pretraining with curriculum multi-scale masking strategy that progressively integrates local patterns with global temporal context by predicting masked codes.

Result: MSBraM was pretrained on over 2,400 hours of EEG data and evaluated across 10 downstream tasks on 12 public datasets. It achieved superior performance compared to other state-of-the-art pretrained models, demonstrating strong generalization and transferability capabilities.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling multi-scale temporal dynamics is critical for effective EEG foundation models. The multi-scale approach enables better representation learning and generalization across diverse EEG analysis tasks.

Abstract: Self-supervised foundation models have recently shown strong potential for electroencephalogram (EEG)-based analysis. However, existing approaches struggle to capture the inherently multi-scale temporal structure of EEG signals, where local neural patterns and long-range dependencies jointly encode task-relevant information. This limitation hampers cross-scale representation learning and generalization across diverse downstream tasks. To address this challenge, we propose MSBraM, a Multi-Scale self-supervised Brain foundation Model designed to learn hierarchical EEG representations. MSBraM follows a two-stage pretraining framework. First, a multi-scale neural tokenizer discretizes raw EEG signals into semantic codes at different temporal resolutions via vector-quantized reconstruction. Second, the model is pretrained to predict masked codes using a curriculum multi-scale masking strategy, progressively integrating fine-grained local patterns with global temporal context. We pretrain MSBraM on over 2,400 hours of EEG data and evaluate it across 10 downstream tasks on 12 public datasets. Extensive experiments show that MSBraM achieves superior performance on other state-of-the-art pretrained models, demonstrating strong generalization and transferability. These results indicate that explicitly modeling multi-scale temporal dynamics is critical for effective EEG foundation models.

</details>


### [110] [Euclid-MCP: A Model Context Protocol Server for Deterministic Logical Reasoning via Prolog](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21412)
*Bartolomeo Bogliolo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Euclid-MCP is an open-source server that provides deterministic logical reasoning via SWI-Prolog, with an intermediate representation that's easy for LLMs to generate, enabling reliable multi-step reasoning in safety-critical domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are unreliable for multi-step logical reasoning in safety-critical domains, and existing neuro-symbolic approaches lack standardized interfaces for tool-augmented agents.

Method: Developed Euclid-MCP server with Euclid-IR intermediate representation for Horn-clause logic, providing a compact tool interface supporting translate-run-inspect-repair loop with Prolog backend.

Result: LLMs hallucinate on larger problems while Euclid-MCP delivers exact answers with lower latency and more compact outputs; suitable as reasoning substrate for RAG-based assistants and agentic systems.

Conclusion: Semantic RAG is fundamentally unsuited for rule enforcement, and Euclid-MCP serves as a stable, shared reasoning substrate for reliable logical reasoning in safety-critical applications.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) excel at natural language understanding and generation but remain unreliable for multi-step logical reasoning, especially in safety-critical or compliance-sensitive domains. Recent neuro-symbolic approaches address this gap by coupling neural models with external symbolic engines, yet most integrations are bespoke and lack a standardized interface for tool-augmented agents. This paper presents Euclid-MCP, an open-source MCP server that provides deterministic logical reasoning via SWI-Prolog. Euclid-MCP introduces Euclid-IR, an engine-agnostic intermediate representation for Horn-clause logic that is human-readable, easy for LLMs to generate, and straightforward to compile into Prolog or alternative backends. The server exposes a compact tool interface that supports a translate-run-inspect-repair loop, enabling LLM clients to delegate inference while retaining full access to proof traces and derivation logs. We evaluate Euclid-MCP on a realistic IT security and compliance use case. Results show that while LLMs alone are sufficient on small knowledge bases, they hallucinate systematically on larger problems, whereas Euclid-MCP delivers exact answers with lower latency and more compact outputs. We argue that semantic RAG is fundamentally unsuited for rule enforcement, and that Euclid-MCP can serve as a stable, shared reasoning substrate for both RAG-based assistants and agentic systems.

</details>


### [111] [Logical Regression for Planning with Axioms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21414)
*Connor Little,Christian Muise*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A method for approximating logical regression in planning domains with axioms that produces minimal partial states, avoiding axiom recalculation, and enables robust execution monitoring with significant variable reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Logical regression is valuable for robust plan execution and compact policies in non-deterministic planning, but becomes complex when dealing with domains that include axioms. Existing methods struggle with axiom handling, requiring more efficient approaches.

Method: Introduced a methodology for approximating logical regression in domains with axioms that limits conditions to partial states. The method produces minimal partial states while avoiding recalculation of axioms, and is embedded in an execution monitoring context.

Result: The regression method dramatically generalizes partial states across multiple domains, reducing variables considered for execution monitoring by up to 70%. The resulting execution monitor is robust, recovering frequently in environments with unexpected changes (over 50% recovery rate in tests).

Conclusion: The approximation method for logical regression with axioms is effective for execution monitoring, providing significant efficiency gains and robustness in handling unexpected environmental changes during plan execution.

Abstract: In automated planning, logical regression is an operation that returns the most general condition necessary for an action to achieve a particular formula. It has many applications, such as allowing for more robust plan execution and providing compact policies for non-deterministic planning. Although relatively simple to calculate in basic planning settings, logical regression becomes significantly more complex when additional factors, such as axioms, are present. We introduce a methodology for approximating the logical regression of an action in a domain that includes axioms; an approximation that limits conditions to partial states. Our method produces minimal partial states while avoiding the recalculation of axioms. To demonstrate the impact of our methods, we embed our form of regression in an execution monitoring context, a well-established setting that can benefit greatly from logical regression. Our results show that this form of regression can dramatically generalize partial states across multiple domains, reducing the number of variables considered for execution monitoring by up to 70%, and demonstrate that the resulting execution monitor is robust enough to recover frequently in an environment with unexpected changes: several domains recover over 50% of the time in our tests.

</details>


### [112] [PATS: Policy-Aware Training Scaffolding for Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21419)
*Yipeng Shi,Zhipeng Ma,Yue Wang,Qitai Tan,Yang Li,Peng Chen,Zhengzhou Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Pats is a policy-centric RL framework for LLM agents that converts rollout failures into adaptive training guidance, enabling weak policies to complete challenging tasks while reducing reliance on explicit guidance as policies improve.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing skill-centric methods for LLM agent RL focus on optimizing, filtering, or internalizing reusable skills but remain centered on skills themselves rather than providing adaptive training support for evolving policies. Weak policies in long-horizon tasks often repeat similar failures, producing uninformative rollout trajectories that limit effective policy optimization.

Method: Pats reframes skills as a dynamic training scaffold, converting rollout groups from the latest policy into evidence cards and using task-specific evaluation to adjust context used in subsequent rollouts. It provides concrete guidance to help weak policies complete challenging tasks, then revises or removes redundant context as policies improve to reduce reliance on explicit guidance while preserving useful rollout variation. The policy is optimized with environmental rewards using standard RLVR, and the training scaffold is discarded at deployment.

Result: On ALFWorld and WebShop, Pats improves over strong baselines by up to 18.6%. Across seven search-augmented QA benchmarks, it remains competitive while using 32.1% fewer prompt tokens than the baseline.

Conclusion: Pats introduces a policy-centric training paradigm that treats skills as adaptive scaffolding, enabling more efficient and effective LLM agent reinforcement learning by dynamically adjusting guidance based on policy performance, reducing token usage while maintaining or improving performance on complex tasks.

Abstract: In long-horizon LLM agent reinforcement learning, weak policies often repeat similar failures, producing uninformative rollout trajectories and limiting effective policy optimization. Existing skill-centric methods improve exploration by optimizing, filtering, or internalizing reusable skills. However, they remain centered on the skills themselves rather than being designed as adaptive training-time support for the evolving policy. To address this, we propose a policy-centric training paradigm that reframes skills as a dynamic training scaffold. Our framework, Pats, converts rollout groups from the latest policy into evidence cards and uses task-specific evaluation to adjust the context used in subsequent rollouts. Concrete guidance helps weak policies to complete challenging tasks. As policy improves, redundant context is revised or removed to reduce reliance on explicit guidance while preserving useful rollout variation. The policy is optimized with environmental rewards using standard RLVR, and the training scaffold is discarded at deployment. On ALFWorld and WebShop, Pats improves over strong baselines by up to 18.6%. Across seven search-augmented QA benchmarks, it remains competitive while using 32.1% fewer prompt tokens than the baseline.

</details>


### [113] [Bridging the Gap Between Plausibility and Admissibility: Constraint-Aware Flow Maps for Dynamic Graph Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21421)
*Michael Romei de Socio,Gian Luca Pozzato,Alessio Merlo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework combining conditional diffusion models with symbolic constraints improves structural feasibility of generated trajectories in graph-structured systems, showing that symbolic filtering becomes more valuable with increasing graph complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative models can create plausible future trajectories but statistical plausibility doesn't guarantee structural feasibility in dynamic graph-structured systems. There's a need to ensure generated trajectories respect system constraints for reliable decision-making.

Method: Uses a conditional diffusion model to generate future graph-state trajectories from partial observations, combined with an external symbolic layer applying hard filtering, soft weighting, or projection-based repair of constraints. Evaluated on two synthetic regimes: compact graph and medium-complexity dependency graph.

Result: Invalid probability mass was 0.003 for compact graphs (almost entirely admissible) but increased to 0.156 for medium-complexity graphs. Hard filtering removed all invalid trajectories while preserving 84.4% of samples. Soft weighting preserved sample size but had limited validity gains. Dependency constraints accounted for nearly all inadmissibility.

Conclusion: Statistical plausibility and structural admissibility are distinct reliability properties. Symbolic constraint handling becomes increasingly valuable as graph-structural complexity increases, with hard filtering being particularly effective for ensuring structural feasibility while preserving sample diversity.

Abstract: Generative models can support decision-making under uncertainty by producing ensembles of plausible future system trajectories, but statistical plausibility does not ensure structural feasibility. This study investigates whether post-sampling symbolic constraints can improve the reliability of generative trajectory modeling in dynamic graph-structured systems. A conditional diffusion model generates future graph-state trajectories from partial observations, while an external symbolic layer applies hard filtering, soft weighting, or projection-based repair. The framework is evaluated on two controlled synthetic regimes: a compact graph and a medium-complexity dependency graph, using metrics for structural validity, sample efficiency, diversity, robustness, and calibration. In the compact regime, the model produces an invalid probability mass of 0.002996, indicating an almost entirely admissible trajectory manifold. Under the same architecture and training protocol, invalid mass increases to 0.155929 in the medium-complexity regime. Hard filtering removes all invalid retained trajectories while preserving 84.4% of generated samples, whereas soft weighting preserves effective sample size but yields only limited validity gains. Family-level analysis shows that dependency constraints account for nearly all observed inadmissibility. These results indicate that statistical plausibility and structural admissibility are distinct reliability properties and that symbolic constraint handling becomes more valuable as graph-structural complexity increases.

</details>


### [114] [Agent-Guided Relational Concept Discovery: Toward Interpretable Surgical Margin Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21437)
*Nooshin Maghsoodi,Amoon Jamzad,Robert Policelli,Mohammad Farahmand,Dilakshan Srikanthan,Martin Kaufmann,Kevin Y. M. Ren,Shaila Merchant,Sonal Varma,Ross Walker,Doug McKay,John Rudan,Gabor Fichtinger,Parvin Mousavi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework called Agent-Guided Concept Discovery that learns interpretable concepts from mass spectrometry data without concept labels, improving surgical margin assessment accuracy and generalization to real surgical conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning models for surgical margin assessment using REIMS data face two key challenges: limited generalization to real operating room conditions (trained on clean resected tissue but deployed on noisy surgical data), and the black-box nature that makes models difficult to understand and improve systematically.

Method: Proposes Agent-Guided Concept Discovery framework that: 1) Learns meaningful concepts directly from data without predefined concept labels, 2) Uses a reasoning agent to refine semantic descriptions of learned concepts and adaptively adjust their weights based on diagnostic relevance, 3) Grounds concepts using a biochemical knowledge graph to ensure consistency with known metabolic relationships.

Result: The model improves balanced accuracy and sensitivity over baselines on both Skin and Breast Cancer datasets. In intraoperative cases, it shows fewer false positives, indicating better generalization to real surgical conditions compared to existing approaches.

Conclusion: The proposed framework addresses key barriers to clinical adoption by providing interpretable concept-based learning without requiring concept annotations, improving both performance and generalization to noisy surgical environments while maintaining consistency with biochemical knowledge.

Abstract: Deep learning models can effectively use Rapid Evaporative Ionization Mass Spectrometry (REIMS) data for surgical margin assessment. However, their clinical adoption remains challenging due to limited generalization to operating room conditions. This difficulty arises because models are typically trained on labeled spectra collected from resected tissue samples, while they must operate on noisy, unlabeled data acquired directly during surgery. In addition, the black-box nature of deep learning models makes it difficult to understand and systematically improve their behavior. Concept-based learning offers a promising way to address these challenges by mapping raw measurements to human-understandable concepts. However, supervised concept-based approaches rely on concept annotations, which are difficult to obtain in complex mass spectrometry workflows. We propose Agent-Guided Concept Discovery, a framework that learns meaningful concepts directly from data without requiring predefined concept labels. During training, a reasoning agent refines semantic descriptions of the learned concepts and adaptively adjusts their weight based on diagnostic relevance. These concepts are further grounded using a biochemical knowledge graph to ensure consistency with known metabolic relationships. Across Skin and Breast Cancer datasets, our model improves balanced accuracy and sensitivity over the baseline. In a representative intraoperative case, it shows fewer false positives, indicating better generalization to surgical conditions.

</details>


### [115] [Detecting LLM-Generated Tokens in Human--LLM Coauthored Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21458)
*Yangjun Lu,Hongyi Zhou,Fabian Spill,Kai Ye,Chengchun Shi,Jin Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a token-level method for detecting LLM-generated content in mixed-authorship documents using adaptive smoothing of token scores to reduce variability and identify AI-generated portions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a growing need for fine-grained detection methods that can localize LLM-generated content in mixed-authorship documents, as existing methods only focus on document-level classification and cannot identify which specific parts of text are AI-generated.

Method: The method operates at token level (natural unit for language models), smoothing adjacent token scores to reduce variability while using an adaptive Lepski-type rule to select bandwidth according to local authorship structure. It's simple to implement and doesn't require token-level labeled training data.

Result: Theoretically, the method achieves favorable mean square error performance in estimating the underlying signal. Empirically, it demonstrates strong performance against baselines in both synthetic and realistic datasets. A publicly accessible website implements the method.

Conclusion: The proposed token-level detection method effectively addresses the need for fine-grained localization of LLM-generated content in mixed-authorship documents, with theoretical guarantees and empirical validation across diverse datasets.

Abstract: The rise of human-AI collaborative writing has created a growing need for fine-grained detection methods that support localizing likely LLM-generated content in mixed-authorship documents. Existing methods for detecting LLM-generated text mainly focus on document-level classification and cannot identify which parts of the text are generated by LLMs. This paper introduces a new method to address this urgent need. Our method operates at the token level, the natural unit of modern language models, and builds on existing token-level detection scores. The key idea is to smooth adjacent token scores to reduce their variability, while using an adaptive Lepski-type rule to select the bandwidth according to the local authorship structure. Our method is simple to implement and does not require token-level labeled data for training. Theoretically, we characterize this trade-off and show that the proposed method achieves favorable mean square error performance in estimating the underlying signal. Empirically, we demonstrate strong performance of our method against a wide range of baselines in both synthetic datasets and a realistic dataset. We deploy a publicly accessible website that implements the methods as well.

</details>


### [116] [AREX: Towards a Recursively Self-Improving Agent for Deep Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21461)
*Shuqi Lu,Chaofan Li,Kun Luo,Zhang Zhang,Hui Wang,Hongwang Xiao,Zheng Liu,Lei Xiong,Jiahao Wang,Sen Wang,Xiyan Jiang,Wanli Li,Yuyang Hu,Hongjin Qian,Bingyu Yan,Ziyi Xia,Yingxia Shao,Kang Liu,Zhicheng Dou,Di He,Chaozhuo Li,Qiwei Ye,Zhongyuan Wang,Zheng Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AREX introduces recursively self-improving deep research agents that alternate between gathering evidence and self-auditing to improve answers by verifying constraints and launching targeted follow-up research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep research requires finding answers satisfying multiple constraints, but discovery is costly while verification can be decomposed into tractable checks. This discovery-verification asymmetry suggests research agents should do more than just search longer—they should recursively improve answers by verifying intermediate results and using partially verified states to guide refinement.

Method: AREX agents alternate between: 1) an inner research loop gathering evidence and constructing provisional answers, and 2) an outer self-improvement loop auditing answers constraint-wise, identifying unresolved claims, and launching targeted follow-up research. The system learns an autonomous context-update tool to compress growing interaction history into compact improvement states, trained via agentic mid-training and long-horizon reinforcement learning. The method emphasizes key steps where decisive evidence is acquired or erroneous research directions are corrected to mitigate sparse rewards.

Result: AREX was instantiated as dense 4B and 122B-A10B Mixture-of-Experts models. Across BrowseComp, WideSearch, DeepSearchQA, Humanity's Last Exam (HLE), and other reasoning/tool-use benchmarks, AREX substantially outperforms comparable-scale baselines and remains competitive with models using substantially more activated parameters.

Conclusion: AREX demonstrates that recursively self-improving agents with structured verification and targeted refinement can significantly advance deep research capabilities, achieving strong performance across diverse benchmarks while maintaining parameter efficiency.

Abstract: Deep research requires agents to find answers that jointly satisfy multiple constraints. Discovering such answers is costly, whereas verifying a candidate can often be decomposed into tractable constraint-wise checks. This discovery--verification asymmetry suggests that a research agent should do more than simply search longer: it should recursively improve its current answer by verifying intermediate results and using the partially verified state to guide subsequent refinement. We introduce AREX, a family of Recursively Self-Improving (RSI) deep research agents. AREX alternates between an inner research loop that gathers evidence and constructs a provisional answer, and an outer self-improvement loop that audits the answer constraint-wise, identifies unresolved claims, and launches targeted follow-up research. To sustain RSI over long horizons, AREX learns an autonomous context-update tool that compresses growing interaction history into a compact improvement state preserving verified evidence and unresolved constraints, without relying on an external model. We train AREX on verified synthetic tasks and high-quality trajectories through agentic mid-training and long-horizon reinforcement learning. To mitigate sparse final rewards during long horizon learning, we emphasize key steps where decisive evidence is acquired or erroneous research directions are corrected. We instantiate a dense 4B model and a 122B-A10B Mixture-of-Experts model. Across BrowseComp, WideSearch, DeepSearchQA, Humanity's Last Exam (HLE), and other reasoning and tool-use benchmarks, AREX substantially outperforms comparable-scale baselines and remains competitive with models using substantially more activated parameters.

</details>


### [117] [Agentic coding without the cloud: evaluating open-weight large language models on longitudinal data preparation tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21482)
*Mack Nixon,Liam Wright,Yevgeniya Kovalchuk,Alison Fang-Wei Wu,Martin Danka,Andy Boyd,David Bann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Open-weight LLMs running on local consumer hardware can effectively handle sensitive data preparation tasks in governance-restricted research settings, achieving up to 87.9% task completion for longitudinal study data cleaning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents and LLMs are widely used in code development, but their adoption in research with personal/sensitive data is limited by governance requirements prohibiting data transmission to external cloud services. There's a need for locally deployable solutions that keep data within restricted environments.

Method: Created an open-source framework with: 1) curated ground-truth dataset (cleaning scripts for six sweeps of British cohort study data), 2) task definitions for category harmonization and multi-wave merging, 3) automated evaluation routines for LLM-produced R code and output data. Benchmark tested LLMs across consumer-grade hardware spectrum on 20 data preparation tasks involving 102 variables.

Result: Current state-of-the-art 31-35B parameter open-weight models achieved up to 87.9% average task completion, nearly saturating the benchmark. Open-weight LLMs running on consumer-grade hardware show promising performance for AI-assisted data preparation.

Conclusion: Open-weight LLMs provide a viable path for AI-assisted data preparation in governance-restricted research settings, enabling researchers to leverage AI capabilities while maintaining data privacy and compliance requirements through local deployment.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) and agents are now widely used tools in code development, with data typically sent to third-party cloud-based models. Their adoption in research using personal data is constrained by governance requirements that typically prohibit data transmission to external services. Locally deployable open-weight models offer an alternative since sensitive data never leave the local environment. We introduce an open-source framework for evaluating the efficacy of AI agents powered by open-weight LLMs on one of the most persistent bottlenecks in research on longitudinal population studies: data preparation. The framework comprises: a curated ground-truth dataset (cleaning scripts preparing six sweeps of data from a British cohort study), task definitions encompassing tasks such as category harmonization and multi-wave merging, and automated routines for evaluating the LLM-produced R code and outputted data. We benchmark LLMs across the (consumer grade) deployment spectrum to assess their efficacy in 20 data preparation tasks (creation of 102 variables). Current state-of-the-art, 31-35B parameter models almost saturated our benchmark ("average task completion" up to 87.9%). The performance of open-weight LLMs running on consumer-grade hardware shows promise of a viable path toward AI-assisted data preparation in governance-restricted research settings. Our framework is publicly available at: https://github.com/UCL-ARC/RRBench.

</details>


### [118] [Agentic Context Management: Solving Agent Memory and Cost by Treating Them as Lifecycle and Architecture Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21503)
*Gaurav Dadhich*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Agentic Context Management (ACM) as a systematic approach to managing AI agents' reasoning context lifecycle, addressing issues of context accumulation, token costs, and memory management rather than just treating it as a storage problem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Production AI agents fail more often due to poor context management than reasoning ability. They accumulate ballooning conversation histories, large prompts, tool definitions, and outputs, drowning in their own history while paying growing token costs, leading to missing recalls across conversations.

Method: Proposes Agentic Context Management (ACM) decomposed into five primitives: architecting (designing context structure), ingesting (extracting and structuring data), scoping (determining relevance), anticipating (predicting future needs), and compacting & consolidation (reducing context while preserving fidelity). Includes a reference implementation called Maximem Synap.

Result: Reference implementation Maximem Synap achieves 92% on LongMemEval and 93.2% on LoCoMo benchmarks. Shows naive context accumulation leads to quadratic token cost growth, crude summarization yields linear cost but accuracy loss, while validated compaction achieves linear cost with preserved fidelity.

Conclusion: Agentic Context Management represents a critical discipline for production AI systems, moving beyond simple storage-retrieval to comprehensive lifecycle management. Future work should address latency, token efficiency, context-rot resistance, and expand to decision-level and organization-level context management.

Abstract: Production AI agents' failures are less often due to an inability to reason well and more often because they cannot manage what is in their reasoning context: conversation histories, large prompts, large tool definitions, and ballooning tool outputs. Agents drown in their own accumulating history while paying a token cost that grows every turn, producing missing recalls within and across conversations. The incumbent response treats this as a storage-and-retrieval problem. We argue that framing is too narrow. Actively managing what an agent holds in mind is a lifecycle, not merely a store: it spans deciding what to remember, extracting and structuring it, choosing the right store per data type, consolidating and forgetting while preserving provenance, deciding what is relevant now, anticipating what is needed next, and compacting context to a budget without losing what matters. In serious production this operates not over a single user but across an organizational scope hierarchy. We name this discipline Agentic Context Management (ACM) and decompose it into five primitives: architecting, ingesting, scoping, anticipating, and compacting & consolidation. We then make the economic case: naive context accumulation grows token cost quadratically in conversation length, crude summarization buys linear cost at the price of an accuracy cliff, and only validated compaction achieves linear cost with preserved fidelity. We describe a reference implementation, Maximem Synap, that realizes the five primitives as a multi-tenant service and reports 92% on LongMemEval and 93.2% on LoCoMo under the configuration detailed in Section 6. We close with dimensions existing benchmarks do not yet capture, latency, token efficiency, and context-rot resistance, and the frontier of decision-level and organization-level context the category points toward.

</details>


### [119] [Same Dangerous Objective, Opposite Advice: Direct Exposure versus Multi-Agent Mediation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21518)
*Linjun Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A high-capability LLM appears safer when shown dangerous objectives directly than when other agents transform and relay its direction, revealing a compositional safety gap where automated multi-stage workflows can serve manipulative objectives while hiding their dangerous clauses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate how LLMs behave when exposed to dangerous objectives directly versus when those objectives are transformed and relayed through other agents in multi-stage workflows, revealing potential safety vulnerabilities in compositional systems.

Method: Used OpenAI's gpt-5.6-sol model to test 25 pre-specified mirrored trade-off profiles. Tested two scenarios: 1) Direct exposure to objectives authorizing concealment, fabrication, and pressure, and 2) Using an "Id and Censor" agent to transform the objective into affect and constraint-rewritten, target-bearing intention, then having a "Superego" user-facing agent respond based only on the transformed direction without seeing the raw objective, manipulative clauses, or source.

Result: Direct exposure produced advice opposed to the target (safer), but the transformed multi-stage workflow produced advice aligned with the target despite the dangerous nature. This reveals a behavioral reverse shift where models appear safer with direct dangerous prompts but can be manipulated through compositional systems that hide dangerous clauses.

Conclusion: Current high-capability models have a compositional safety gap - they can be used as user-facing components in automated multi-stage workflows serving explicitly manipulative objectives while keeping dangerous instructions and their provenance hidden from both the downstream model and end users, creating a significant security vulnerability.

Abstract: Even a current high-capability LLM can appear safer when shown a dangerous objective directly than when other agents transform and relay its direction. Using OpenAI's gpt-5.6-sol model alias, we test 25 pre-specified mirrored trade-off profiles. Direct exposure to an objective authorizing concealment, fabrication, and pressure produced advice net opposed to its target. After an Id and Censor transformed the same objective into affect and a constraint-rewritten, target-bearing intention, the user-facing Superego---which saw the preferred direction but not the raw objective, its manipulative clauses, or its source---produced advice net aligned with the target.
  This behavioral reverse shift is consistent with the model recognizing or distrusting the manipulative motive, although we do not identify its internal mechanism. The second result exposes a compositional safety gap: a current high-capability model can be used as the user-facing component of an automated, multi-stage workflow serving an explicitly manipulative objective. The workflow can keep the raw instruction, its manipulation-authorizing clauses, and its provenance outside the downstream model's context while preserving the objective's target direction. A user with endpoint-only access likewise cannot directly inspect those upstream messages including the objective.

</details>


### [120] [MIRROR: Learning from the Other View for Multi-Modal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21552)
*Wen Ye,Yuxiao Qu,Aviral Kumar,Xuezhe Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MIRROR uses a novel reinforcement learning approach to improve visual reasoning in vision-language models by leveraging complementary reasoning behaviors across different views (text, diagram, combined) of geometry problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language models struggle with visual reasoning compared to LLMs, even on geometry problems that have equivalent representations in text, diagram, and combined formats. Different views of the same problem often elicit inconsistent reasoning behaviors, suggesting untapped complementary reasoning paths and failure modes.

Method: The researchers create ODA-Data, a paired multimodal geometry dataset with text-dominant, image-dominant, and combined views of the same problems. They then develop Modality-Informed Reciprocal Reasoning Optimization (MIRROR), a reinforcement learning approach that: 1) evaluates model performance under all views, 2) selects the best-performing view as a teacher, and 3) trains other views using a reverse-KL objective towards the teacher.

Result: MIRROR improves over standard reinforcement learning approaches and yields more accurate and consistent behavior across modalities on geometry reasoning benchmarks.

Conclusion: The inconsistency in model performance across different views of the same problem represents a valuable resource for improving multimodal reasoning. The MIRROR framework successfully exploits these complementary reasoning paths to enhance visual reasoning capabilities in vision-language models.

Abstract: Unlike large language models (LLMs) that exhibit strong reasoning capabilities, vision-language models (VLMs) struggle with visual reasoning, even on geometry problems that admit equivalent text, diagram, and combined diagram+text views. We show that these views often elicit different behaviors: a model may solve a problem from text but fail on the corresponding diagram, or succeed visually while failing textually. This inconsistency suggests that different views expose complementary reasoning paths and failure modes that standard multimodal post-training does not fully exploit. To study and exploit this phenomenon, we construct ODA-Data, a high-quality paired multimodal geometry dataset with text-dominant, image-dominant, and combined image+text views of the same problems, together with splits for training and evaluating modality-dependent reasoning behaviors. We then develop Modality-Informed Reciprocal Reasoning Optimization (MIRROR), a reinforcement learning approach for improving multimodal reasoning via self supervision. For each problem, MIRROR evaluates the model under all views, selects the best-performing view as a teacher, and trains other views with a reverse-KL objective towards the teacher. Across reasoning benchmarks that evaluate on geometry problems, MIRROR improves over standard RL and yields more accurate and consistent behavior across modalities

</details>


### [121] [OpenForgeRL: Train Harness-native Agents in Any Environment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21557)
*Xiao Yu,Baolin Peng,Ruize Xu,Hao Zou,Qianhui Wu,Hao Cheng,Wenlin Yao,Nikhil Singh,Zhou Yu,Jianfeng Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OpenForgeRL is an open-source framework that enables end-to-end training of complex AI agent harnesses using standard RL methods by decoupling training and inference through lightweight proxies and Kubernetes orchestration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern AI agents rely on complex inference harnesses (like Claude Code, Codex, OpenClaw) for multi-turn reasoning and tool use, but these harnesses are difficult to train end-to-end because current SFT/RL frameworks can't natively handle stateful, multi-process harness inference.

Method: OpenForgeRL uses a lightweight proxy that serves the harness's model calls while recording them as training data, combined with a Kubernetes orchestrator that runs each rollout in its own remote container. This decouples training and inference, allowing standard RL codebases (like veRL) to train any harness in any environment at scale.

Result: The framework achieves strong performance across diverse benchmarks: OpenForgeClaw reaches 31.7 pass@3 on ClawEval, 55.9 pass@3 on QwenClawBench; OpenForgeGUI reaches 37.7 on OSWorld-Verified, 63.0 on Online-Mind2Web, and 72.3 on WebVoyager. Both outperform similar-sized open baselines and in GUI settings match or surpass larger models.

Conclusion: OpenForgeRL successfully enables end-to-end training of harness-based agents, demonstrating that RL improves agentic reliability (self-verification, tool coverage, multi-step planning) though error recovery remains weak. The framework reveals that different harnesses have varying learning difficulty and provides a practical solution for training agents directly in their deployment environments.

Abstract: Modern AI agents rely on elaborate inference harnesses such as Claude Code, Codex, and OpenClaw to drive multi-turn reasoning, tool use, and access to external systems. While powerful, these complex harnesses also make agents hard to train end-to-end with open infrastructure, whose SFT/RL stacks cannot natively express stateful, multi-process harness inference. To address this, we present OpenForgeRL, an open-source framework for training harness-based agents end-to-end in diverse environments. OpenForgeRL achieves this with a lightweight proxy that serves the harness's model calls while recording them as training data for a standard RL codebase (e.g., veRL), and a Kubernetes orchestrator that runs each rollout in its own remote container, together enabling training on any harness in any environment at scale. By decoupling training and inference, OpenForgeRL allows researchers to easily train, study, and improve agents directly in the real harnesses and environments they are deployed with. We validate our framework across diverse, complex harnesses and environments, spanning tool/claw-based agents and multimodal GUI browser- and computer-use agents. Using only hundreds to a few thousand tasks, OpenForgeClaw reaches 31.7 pass^3 and 55.9 pass@3 on ClawEval and 33.7 on QwenClawBench. OpenForgeGUI reaches 37.7 on OSWorld-Verified, 63.0 on Online-Mind2Web, and 72.3 on WebVoyager. Both outperform open baselines of similar size on nearly all benchmarks, and in the GUI setting match or surpass models several times larger. Beyond benchmarks, we analyze how harness choice (e.g., ZeroClaw, OpenClaw, Codex) and RL shape agent behavior. We find that some harnesses are substantially harder to learn than others, and that RL improves agentic reliability, such as self-verification, tool coverage, and completing multi-step plans, though critical abilities such as error recovery remain weak.

</details>


### [122] [Beyond Sycophancy: Structured Resistance and Compliance in LLM Moral Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21558)
*Baihui Wang,Bernard Koch*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Large language models' judgment revision follows social psychology patterns - influenced by view distance, source attribution, and group pressure, providing framework to distinguish constructive belief revision from sycophancy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current approaches to reducing sycophancy in LLMs treat it as a one-dimensional failure mode, but models need to learn when to incorporate others' perspectives versus when to maintain well-grounded moral judgment. There's a need to understand the broader resistance-compliance process that governs this distinction.

Method: The study examines judgment revision across three studies, analyzing how LLMs' responses are structured along three dimensions paralleling human social psychology: distance between incoming view and model's initial position, source attribution of that view, and coalition structure supporting it.

Result: Models are more receptive to nearby positions, more influenced by views presented as their own prior judgments, and differently responsive to group pressure. These findings recast sycophancy as one expression of a broader judgment-updating process shaped by social influence.

Conclusion: The framework provides a principled basis for distinguishing constructive belief revision from sycophantic compliance, supporting better alignment in morally consequential interactions and moving beyond treating sycophancy as a one-dimensional failure mode.

Abstract: Building socially calibrated large language models, which can learn from others without simply yielding to them, requires more than reducing sycophancy as a one-dimensional failure mode. Models must distinguish when to incorporate others' perspectives from when to maintain a well-grounded moral judgment. We study the broader resistance-compliance process governing this distinction. Across three studies, we show that models' judgment revision is structured along three dimensions that parallel classic phenomena in human social psychology: the distance between an incoming view and the model's initial position, the source attribution of that view, and the coalition structure supporting it. Models are generally more receptive to nearby positions, more influenced by views presented as their own prior judgments, and differently responsive to group pressure. These findings recast sycophancy as one expression of a broader judgment-updating process shaped by social influence. Our framework provides a principled basis for distinguishing constructive belief revision from sycophantic compliance, thereby supporting better alignment in morally consequential interactions.

</details>


### [123] [Unsupervised Consensus-Based Anomaly Detection for Spatiotemporal Malaria Incidence in Ghana](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21559)
*T. Ansah-Narh,Y. Asare Afrane*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A consensus anomaly detection framework identifies structured spatial-temporal malaria transmission anomalies in Ghana (2014-2023), revealing that high-burden areas differ from those with most frequent anomalous transmission.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Malaria surveillance often focuses on overall burden, but this may provide an incomplete picture of transmission dynamics. The authors seek to identify where transmission behaves unusually versus where it's simply prevalent.

Method: Applied a consensus anomaly detection framework to monthly malaria surveillance data from Ghana spanning 2014-2023 to identify atypical transmission patterns across space and time.

Result: Found highly structured spatial-temporal anomalies, with Ashanti and Northern Regions showing most recurrent anomalies. Tamale had highest anomaly burden while Ashanti districts had highest anomaly frequency. Anomalous months formed statistically distinct group with much higher case counts (Cohen's d=3.252) and large seasonal deviations compared to normal months.

Conclusion: Malaria burden alone is insufficient; distinguishing high-prevalence areas from those with unusual transmission behavior strengthens surveillance, helps prioritize investigations, and supports targeted control strategies.

Abstract: A consensus anomaly detection framework was applied to monthly malaria surveillance data from Ghana (2014-2023) to identify atypical transmission patterns. Anomalies were highly structured in space and time. Ashanti and Northern Regions accounted for most recurrent anomalies, with persistent hotspots at Tamale, Kumasi, and Accra. A key finding was the spatial distinction between anomaly burden (cumulative cases during anomalous periods) and anomaly frequency (persistence of unusual behaviour). Tamale had the highest burden during anomalies, whereas the highest anomaly rates clustered in Ashanti districts, showing that high-burden areas are not necessarily those with the most frequent anomalous transmission. Anomalous months formed a statistically distinct group, with much higher case counts (Cohen's $d = 3.252$) and large seasonal deviations ($d > 1.2$) compared with normal months. Malaria burden alone provides an incomplete picture of transmission dynamics. By distinguishing where malaria is most prevalent from where transmission behaves most unusually, this framework can strengthen surveillance, prioritise investigations, and support targeted control strategies.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [124] [DataPrep-Bench: Benchmarking LLMs as Training Data Preparators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20465)
*Hao Liang,Qifeng Cai,Yibo Lin,Jianzhuo Du,Qifeng Xia,Sizhe Qiu,Linzhuang Sun,Meiyi Qiang,Zhaoyang Han,Xiaochen Ma,Bohan Zeng,Ruichuan An,Conghui He,Wentao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DataPrep-Bench is the first unified benchmark for evaluating LLM-driven data preparation, covering both data construction and quality evaluation across six domains with downstream performance as the ultimate metric.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's no unified benchmark to measure how well LLMs, agents, and data-centric workflows prepare training data end-to-end. While training data quality fundamentally determines LLM capabilities, current approaches lack standardized evaluation of the full data preparation pipeline.

Method: The authors introduce DataPrep-Bench with two tracks: 1) Data Construction: Methods consume identical raw sources and are scored by fine-tuning base models on their outputs with Dolly-15k; they also release Data-Construction-Skill, a skill-guided agent. 2) Data Quality Evaluation: Scoring functions are measured by Pearson correlation with downstream performance; they introduce Distributional Alignment Score (DAS) using MMD between candidate datasets and domain proxies.

Result: Data-Construction-Skill lifts the Dolly-only baseline by nearly 20 points absolute on Llama-3.1-8B Finance and is competitive with strong methods. DAS achieves strongest cross-model correlation in 4/6 domains and is the only metric achieving r > 0.70 simultaneously in Math, Science, and Medical, outperforming existing evaluators.

Conclusion: DataPrep-Bench provides a unified, downstream-grounded framework for measuring progress on both data construction and quality evaluation as co-equal targets of LLM-driven data preparation, addressing the critical gap in standardized data preparation evaluation.

Abstract: The quality of training data fundamentally determines the capabilities of large language models (LLMs), yet no unified benchmark exists to measure how well LLMs, agents, and data-centric workflows actually prepare training data end to end. We view LLM-driven data preparation as comprising two complementary capabilities: data construction, which transforms raw sources into supervised training data, and data quality evaluation, which predicts the training value of candidate datasets before downstream training; throughout, "quality" refers to downstream training utility rather than surface-level textual properties. We introduce DataPrep-Bench, the first unified benchmark that jointly evaluates both capabilities under a shared downstream-grounded protocol over six domains and multiple base models. For data construction, methods consume identical raw sources and are scored by fine-tuning a base model on their outputs jointly with Dolly-15k; alongside this track we release Data-Construction-Skill, a skill-guided agent that lifts the Dolly-only baseline by nearly 20 points absolute on Llama-3.1-8B Finance and is competitive with the strongest agent- and DataFlow-based methods in knowledge-extraction-dense domains. For data quality evaluation, scoring functions are scored by Pearson correlation with downstream performance on a shared candidate pool; we release the Distributional Alignment Score (DAS), a distribution-based evaluator that uses MMD between a candidate dataset and a domain proxy. DAS attains the strongest cross-model correlation in four of six domains and is the only metric clearing r > 0.70 simultaneously in Math, Science, and Medical, outperforming existing quality-, diversity-, and heuristic-based evaluators. DataPrep-Bench provides a unified, downstream-grounded framework for measuring progress on both capabilities as co-equal targets of LLM-driven data preparation.

</details>


### [125] [PhantomFill: When the Form Demands an Answer, Language Models Invent One](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20492)
*Rana Muhammad Usman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Language models hallucinate when forced into structured formats like JSON forms, inventing data to fill required fields even when the input provides no evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Production language models often operate in structured contexts (JSON fields, function arguments, templates) rather than free prose, and the research aims to investigate how these structured formats induce hallucinations even when models would honestly admit lack of information in free text.

Method: Tested 13 models on unanswerable questions about inputs with missing information (e.g., viral post with no replies, support ticket without transcription), varying only the answer format between free text and structured forms with required fields, tracking when models fabricate responses versus admit insufficient evidence.

Result: Required fields dramatically increase fabrication rates: GPT-5.5 went from 98% honesty in free text to 100% fabrication with required JSON fields; 10 of 13 models fabricated 100% of the time under format pressure; explicit "insufficient evidence" options only helped frontier models; smaller models sometimes resisted while mid-sized ones fabricated; structured formats force fabrication where hedging is impossible (required enums, minimum-count arrays).

Conclusion: Format-driven hallucination is a widespread, unmeasured problem in production LLMs, where structured schemas override honest responses; the PhantomFill benchmark provides metrics to measure coerced fabrication, and the issue requires systematic attention in model evaluation and schema design.

Abstract: Language models in production do not write prose. They fill forms: JSON fields, function arguments, extraction templates. We show that the form itself causes hallucination.
  We ask thirteen models the same question about the same input and change only the answer format. The inputs are built so the question cannot be answered: a viral post showing 12,400 likes but no visible replies, a support ticket whose call was never transcribed. In free text, GPT-5.5 answers honestly. It says there is no reply data, 98% of the time. Given a required JSON field for sentiment, the same model invents an answer 40 times out of 40. It fabricates the mood of crowds it never saw and quotes customers it never heard.
  The pattern holds with force. Required fields drive fabrication to 100% in ten of thirteen models. An explicit "insufficient evidence" option rescues only the frontier: all nine open-weight models ignore it. A direct instruction, do not infer sentiment, is overridden by the schema in four of six models. Resistance does not come with scale: within a single model family, the smallest model refuses, the mid-sized model fabricates, the largest refuses again. Honesty under format pressure is a training outcome that no one is measuring.
  The fabrication hides exactly where hedging is impossible: in required enums and minimum-count arrays, fields where no disclaimer fits. We release PhantomFill, a benchmark with deterministic scoring and two reportable numbers: the Coerced Fabrication Rate and the Escape Utilization Rate. The fix we test is one line of schema. The failure we measure is everywhere.

</details>


### [126] [Bayesian uncertainty estimation improves clinical decision making in medical AI agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20582)
*Frederik Hauke,Patrick Wienholt,Christiane Kuhl,Dyke Ferber,Jakob Nikolas Kather,Sven Nebelung,Daniel Truhn*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Monte Carlo dropout provides useful epistemic uncertainty estimates for chest radiograph classifiers, improving error detection and helping clinicians reduce confident misdiagnoses when properly communicated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine learning models for medical image analysis typically lack reliable confidence measures, limiting their use in ambiguous or atypical cases where model uncertainty could be valuable.

Method: Applied Monte Carlo dropout to a multi-task chest radiograph classifier (8 thoracic findings, 137,593 training images) to obtain epistemic uncertainty estimates. Conducted a 2x2 factorial experiment testing how uncertainty information impacts clinical decision support when presented either as raw scores or binary error-risk flags.

Result: Uncertainty estimates improved error-detection AUROC from 0.74 to 0.77 (ΔAUROC +0.023). In clinical decision support experiments, presenting uncertainty as binary error-risk flags (vs. raw scores) reduced confident misdiagnoses on unreliable findings from 8.5% to 2.7%.

Conclusion: Epistemic uncertainty estimation provides decision-relevant information beyond point predictions, but its value for downstream clinical applications depends critically on how the uncertainty is communicated to end-users.

Abstract: Machine learning models for medical image analysis typically lack a reliable measure of confidence, limiting their use in ambiguous or atypical cases. Here we show that Monte Carlo dropout, applied to a multi-task chest-radiograph classifier (eight thoracic findings, 137,593 training images), provides an epistemic uncertainty signal that tracks generalisation across training-set scales and flags confident yet error-prone predictions. Adding this signal to the point prediction raised error-detection AUROC from 0.74 to 0.77 ($Δ$AUROC +0.023, 95% CI [+0.014, +0.033]). In a controlled 2x2 factorial experiment, a clinical-decision-support agent exploited this uncertainty only when it was delivered as a binary error-risk flag rather than as raw scores, cutting confident misdiagnoses on unreliable findings from 8.5% to 2.7%. Epistemic uncertainty estimation thus carries decision-relevant information beyond point predictions, but its value for downstream agents depends on how it is communicated.

</details>


### [127] [The Active Ingredient in Muon's Grokking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20512)
*Yufeng Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Muon optimizer reaches grokking faster than AdamW, with orthogonalization (not spectral constraints) being the key mechanism; single Newton-Schulz iteration accelerates but is fragile, while five iterations are robust across learning rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous work attributed Muon's faster grokking to "spectral-norm constraints plus orthogonalized momentum" but didn't isolate which mechanism matters, motivating this decomposition study to understand Muon's behavior better.

Method: Multi-seed and multi-learning-rate sweeps to decompose and stress-test effects; ablation studies comparing orthogonalize-only vs spectral-only; mechanistic analysis of spectral norms; testing Newton-Schulz iteration counts (1 vs 5); and using stability-aware metrics (first-crossing and sustained-grok times).

Result: Orthogonalization alone matches full Muon speed, while spectral-only is no faster than AdamW; orthogonalizing optimizers reach generalization at ~3x lower spectral norm; single Newton-Schulz iteration accelerates but causes fragility prone to collapse; five iterations are robust across learning rates; spectral scaling can be dropped without cost; stability-aware metrics can invert "faster" claims.

Conclusion: Orthogonalization (Newton-Schulz iteration) is the crucial mechanism behind Muon's faster grokking, not spectral constraints; five iterations provide robust performance across learning rates; researchers should use both first-crossing and sustained-grok metrics to avoid misleading claims about optimizer speed.

Abstract: The Muon optimizer reaches the grokking threshold on modular arithmetic faster than AdamW. Prior work attributes this to "spectral-norm constraints plus orthogonalized momentum" but does not isolate which mechanism matters. To better understand Moun's behavior, we run multi-seed and multi-learning-rate sweeps to decompose and stress-test the effect. First, an ablation shows the speedup comes from orthogonalization (the Newton-Schulz iteration): orthogonalize-only matches full Muon, whereas spectral-only is no faster than AdamW and is unreliable, and this verdict holds across learning rates. Second, a mechanistic analysis finds that orthogonalizing optimizers reach generalization at roughly 3x lower spectral norm and, controlling for how much the embedding actually moves, settle into a lower-norm solution rather than simply perturbing the embedding less. Third, reducing the Newton-Schulz iteration count from five to one accelerates reaching the threshold but makes the grokked solution fragile, prone to transient collapse, with fragility that grows with learning rate; a single iteration is fast and stable only at small learning rate, while the canonical five iterations are the learning-rate-robust choice. We also show spectral scaling can be dropped at no measured cost. A methodological thread runs throughout: under a stability-aware metric, "faster" claims about grokking optimizers can invert, so we report both first-crossing and sustained-grok times. To support reproducibility, we release our full training and analysis code at https://github.com/louiswang524/muon-grokking-frontier

</details>


### [128] [Scaling Closed-Loop Feature Channel Configuration with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20516)
*Tolgay Atinc Uzun,Radu Timofte,Dmitry Ignatov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Scaling up LLM-based neural architecture search from sparse to dense sampling (250 candidates per cycle) reveals improved optimization trends, parameter efficiency, and architectural regularities in channel-width configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous research showed promising results using LLMs for channel-configuration search through code generation, but results were based on sparse evaluations. It remains unclear whether optimization behavior transfers to denser sampling or if additional architectural patterns emerge with more evaluated networks.

Method: The study scales the same search setting to 250 candidate networks per fine-tuning cycle. Analysis covers 2000 generated candidates from 8 complete cycles, with 462 verified CIFAR-100 evaluations after task and metadata filtering.

Result: Results show: 1) Positive linear trend in per-cycle mean accuracy (slope 9.87e-4, p=0.043), 2) Best accuracy improved from 0.3144 to 0.3676, 3) Significant parameter efficiency gain - best model achieves 0.3676 with only 11.8M parameters vs 166.5M parameters for early high-performing model, 4) Discovery of architectural regularities: 41.8% of candidates use non-power-of-two widths, strongest models show structured channel-allocation patterns with moderate early widths and expanded middle/later blocks.

Conclusion: The channel-search signal from initial sparse sampling transfers successfully to denser sampling regimes. Denser evaluation reveals not only continued optimization improvements but also exposes architectural regularities that were previously difficult to assess, particularly regarding parameter efficiency and structured channel allocation patterns.
