Abstract: Promising initial results in closed-loop large-language-model-based channel-configuration search demonstrated that neural-network widths can be optimized directly through executable code generation and accuracy feedback. However, those results were obtained from a relatively sparse set of valid evaluations, leaving open whether the observed optimization behavior transfers to a denser sampling regime and whether additional architectural regularities emerge when more generated networks are evaluated. To test this, the same search setting is scaled to 250 candidate networks per fine-tuning cycle. The analysis covers 2000 generated candidates from 8 complete cycles, yielding 462 verified CIFAR-100 evaluations after task and metadata filtering. Per-cycle mean accuracy exhibits a positive linear trend with slope 9.87e-4 (p=0.043), while the high-performing frontier improves more strongly: the best observed accuracy increases from 0.3144 to 0.3676, and both the top-5 and top-10 cycle-level means exhibit positive trends. The scaled run also reveals improved parameter efficiency. The best model reaches 0.3676 with 11.8M parameters, compared with an early high-performing model at 0.3144 with 166.5M parameters. Beyond accuracy, the larger sample exposes architectural regularities that were difficult to assess from sparse observations. Non-power-of-two channel widths occur in 41.8% of verified candidates, and the strongest models share structured channel-allocation patterns characterized by moderate early widths and expanded middle or later blocks. These findings indicate that the channel-search signal observed in the initial study transfers

</details>


### [129] [Multimodal CoLRAG-TF: Triple-Filtered Retrieval for Complex PDFs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20517)
*Takato Yasuno*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Multimodal CoLRAG-TF is a four-axis fusion RAG system that integrates text, BM25, knowledge graph triples, and image similarity for retrieval over heterogeneous PDFs, achieving high recall and multi-hop reasoning improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Retrieval-augmented generation over heterogeneous PDF collections is challenging due to multimodal content (text/images), domain-specific terminology, complex layouts, and the need for multi-hop reasoning across dispersed evidence in documents.

Method: A four-axis fusion architecture combining dense text embeddings, BM25 keyword matching, knowledge-graph triple filtering (using OpenIE triples indexed with FAISS), and image-based similarity. The system uses a hybrid OCR pipeline, LLM-based caption generation, and a HippoRAG2-inspired coarse-to-fine retriever with hierarchical propagation.

Result: Achieved Retrieval Recall of 0.9909 on a 457-pair benchmark, with 71.6% improvement in multi-hop answer similarity over single-hop queries. Bayesian optimization showed triple axis dominance (α_triple = 0.44) is crucial for countering lexical bias and sustaining multi-hop quality.

Conclusion: Triple-filtered multimodal fusion is essential for structured reasoning over noisy, heterogeneous PDFs and provides a general framework applicable beyond the disaster domain, demonstrated through both text and vision-based applications.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) over heterogeneous PDF collections remains challenging due to multimodal content, domain-specific terminology, and the need for multi-hop reasoning across dispersed evidence. We present Multimodal CoLRAG-TF, a four-axis fusion architecture that integrates dense text embeddings, BM25 keyword matching, knowledge-graph triple filtering, and image-based similarity for robust retrieval over complex documents. Our system constructs a multimodal index of 2,403 blocks extracted from 43 Japanese disaster lesson PDFs, supported by a hybrid OCR pipeline and LLM-based caption generation. To enhance compositional reasoning, we extract 11,414 OpenIE triples and index them with FAISS, enabling sub-second triple lookup and hierarchical propagation of relevance signals. A HippoRAG2-inspired coarse-to-fine retriever (volume $\to$ chapter $\to$ block) narrows the search space before final fusion scoring. Bayesian optimization over fusion weights reveals that the triple axis must dominate ($α_\text{triple} = 0.44$) to counteract lexical bias and sustain multi-hop retrieval quality. Evaluated on a 457-pair benchmark, Multimodal CoLRAG-TF achieves a Retrieval Recall of 0.9909 and a 71.6$\%$ improvement in multi-hop answer similarity over single-hop queries. An image-to-lesson pipeline using a vision LLM further demonstrates the applicability of the approach to visual inputs. These results show that triple-filtered multimodal fusion is essential for structured reasoning over noisy, heterogeneous PDFs and provides a general framework applicable beyond the disaster domain.

</details>


### [130] [Compact Latent Coordination for Autonomous Vehicles at Unsignalized Intersections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21488)
*Gil Lifshits,Igal Bilik,Gilad Katz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MAPS is a hierarchical DRL system where a Master agent generates proto-plan embeddings for global coordination, while Worker agents use these with local observations for vehicle control, achieving efficient collision-free intersection navigation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current MARL systems for autonomous vehicle coordination at unsignalized intersections struggle with combinatorial action spaces, reliance on privileged information, and rigid agent designs that limit their effectiveness in real-world scenarios.

Method: MAPS (Master-Agent Proto-plan System) uses hierarchical DRL with a centralized Master agent generating continuous proto-plan embeddings encoding global coordination strategies, which decentralized Worker agents integrate with local observations for vehicle-specific control execution.

Result: MAPS achieved collision-free navigation across 72 intersection configurations while significantly reducing average travel time, outperforming state-of-the-art baselines. It showed strong generalization with 94% success rate when deployed zero-shot from 3 to 5-agent scenarios.

Conclusion: Proto-plan-based hierarchical learning provides a promising framework for multi-vehicle coordination at intersections by decoupling strategic intent from tactical execution and enabling independent module optimization, with demonstrated generalization capabilities.

Abstract: Coordinating autonomous vehicles at unsignalized intersections remains a critical challenge for multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) systems, which typically struggle with combinatorial action spaces, reliance on privileged information, or rigid agent designs. We propose Master-Agent Proto-plan System (MAPS), a hierarchical deep reinforcement learning (DRL) architecture in which a centralized Master agent generates a compact, continuous embedding, denoted as proto-plan, that encodes a global coordination strategy. Decentralized Worker agents integrate this embedding with local observations to execute vehicle-specific control, decoupling strategic intent from tactical execution and enabling independent optimization of each module.
  As a proof-of-concept evaluation of this coordination mechanism, we test MAPS across 72 intersection configurations in HighwayEnv. MAPS achieves collision-free navigation while significantly reducing average travel time, outperforming state-of-the-art baselines. The learned proto-plans further exhibit robust generalization: a system trained with three agents achieves a 94% success rate when deployed zero-shot to five-agent scenarios, confirming that proto-plan-based hierarchical learning provides a promising framework for multi-vehicle coordination.

</details>


### [131] [Adaptive Depth in Looped Transformers: Diagnosing Learned Halting Gates and Trajectory Readouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20519)
*Andrei Cristian Popescu,Haitz Sáez de Ocáriz Borde,Pietro Liò*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Looped Transformers with learned halting suffer from entangling exit selection with trajectory formation; separating these via fixed-depth supervision produces better difficulty-aware trajectories, and simple confidence readouts often outperform learned gates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current learned halting objectives in looped Transformers entangle exit selection (inference-time stopping rule) with trajectory formation (training-time supervision), causing poor adaptive-compute performance that can stem from either the readout, induced trajectory, or their interaction.

Method: Study adaptive depth through trajectory-readout lens using: (1) controlled synthetic tasks (modular arithmetic, binary parity), (2) large-scale Ouro-1.4B/2.6B checkpoints, (3) fixed-prior depth supervision to shape trajectories without input-dependent halting, and (4) simple post-hoc confidence readouts compared to learned linear/MLP gates.

Result: Fixed-prior depth supervision produces difficulty-aware trajectories exposing useful stopping signals; simple confidence readouts often match/outperform learned gates; failure stems mainly from trajectory induced by joint gate training rather than limited gate expressivity; same pattern in Ouro evaluations with practical inference-time savings.

Conclusion: Adaptive depth in looped Transformers should be reframed as a joint problem of trajectory formation and exit readout, not just gate learning; separating these aspects reveals that trajectory quality is crucial, and simple readouts on well-formed trajectories often suffice.

Abstract: Looped Transformers increase test-time computation by repeatedly applying a shared recurrent block. Learned halting objectives in looped Transformers typically use a single exit distribution both as the inference-time stopping rule and as the training-time weighting of per-depth losses. This entangles exit selection with trajectory formation: the gate not only chooses which recurrent state to use, but also determines how strongly each intermediate state is supervised. Consequently, poor adaptive-compute performance can arise from the readout, the induced trajectory, or their interaction. We study adaptive depth in looped Transformers through this trajectory--readout lens, across controlled synthetic tasks (modular arithmetic and binary parity) and large-scale Ouro-1.4B and 2.6B checkpoints. We find that fixed-prior depth supervision, which shapes the trajectory without an input-dependent halting policy, produces difficulty-aware trajectories whose intermediate states expose useful stopping signals, and that simple post-hoc confidence readouts often match or outperform learned linear and MLP gates. Fitting gates on frozen trajectories localizes the failure: it appears to stem mainly from the trajectory induced by joint gate training rather than from limited gate expressivity. The same pattern is present in Ouro evaluations, where pretrained ponder gates are competitive but not uniformly Pareto-optimal, and measured latency confirms that the resulting reductions in average exit depth translate into practical inference-time savings. Our systematic diagnostic evaluation reframes adaptive depth in looped Transformers as a joint problem of trajectory formation and exit readout, rather than gate learning alone, highlighting a distinction that prior learned-halting work has often left implicit.

</details>


### [132] [Generative Bayesian Filtering for State Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20521)
*Lei Cao,Sihang Feng,Jixin Yan,Tao Sun,Naichen Shi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GBF is a filtering framework that replaces restrictive observation models with pretrained conditional generative models for improved state estimation in dynamic systems with nonlinear, heterogeneous sensor signals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical filtering approaches like Kalman filters rely on simple observation models (linear-Gaussian) that cannot handle the increasingly nonlinear and heterogeneous patterns in high-dimensional sensor signals from modern dynamic systems.

Method: Proposes Generative Bayesian Filtering (GBF) which replaces restrictive observation models with pretrained conditional generative models parametrized by conditional variational autoencoders (CVAE). Uses Bayesian prediction-update recursion where measurement update is formulated as a posterior sampling problem combining dynamical prior with CVAE-induced likelihood, then transformed into a score-based sampling problem.

Result: Experiments on synthetic datasets and real-world applications (manufacturing system monitoring and arrhythmia diagnosis) demonstrate that GBF improves state estimation accuracy and robustness relative to baseline approaches.

Conclusion: GBF provides a flexible filtering framework that inherits flexibility from generative models and uncertainty quantification capabilities from ensembling, effectively handling nonlinear and heterogeneous patterns in high-dimensional sensor signals.

Abstract: The state of a dynamic system evolves over time, switching among several latent modes that govern its observable behavior. Filtering methods infer the latent state from observations. Classical filtering approaches, including Kalman filters, typically rely on simple observation models, such as linear-Gaussian models, that are incapable of characterizing the increasingly nonlinear and heterogeneous patterns in high-dimensional sensor signals. To tackle the challenge, we propose Generative Bayesian Filtering (GBF), a filtering framework that replaces restrictive observation models with pretrained conditional generative models parametrized by conditional variational autoencoders (CVAE). For online inference, GBF performs a Bayesian prediction-update recursion in which the measurement update is formulated as a posterior sampling problem that combines the dynamical prior with the CVAE-induced likelihood. The resulting filtering problem is then transformed into a score-based sampling problem, which naturally inherits the flexibility from generative models and the uncertainty quantification capabilities from ensembling. Experiments on synthetic datasets and real-world applications involving manufacturing system monitoring and arrhythmia diagnosis demonstrate that GBF improves state estimation accuracy and robustness relative to baseline approaches.

</details>


### [133] [Do Active SAE Feature Planes Carry More Holonomy? A Preregistered Reversal in Gemma](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20522)
*Larry Richards*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper tests whether holonomy (geometric curvature) concentrates on active sparse-autoencoder feature planes in Gemma 2 2B, but finds the opposite: active-feature planes carry LESS holonomy than mixed-feature controls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test the semantic-concentration prediction - whether geometric curvature (holonomy) concentrates on active semantic features in language models, operationalized through sparse-autoencoder feature planes.

Method: Pre-registered design measuring holonomy at final-token layer-12 to layer-13 residual-stream readout using restricted-Jacobian transport rule, carrying a local frame around small loops, normalizing rotation by enclosed area. Compared active-feature planes against matched mixed-feature controls.

Result: Prediction falsified in reverse: active-feature planes carried LESS holonomy than matched mixed-feature controls (adjusted log contrast: -0.29439, 95% CI: [-0.43989, -0.14889]). Magnitude-only explanation not supported, three-way ordering undefined due to common support failure. Post-freeze diagnostics supported area law, identified transport distortion as mechanism/confound.

Conclusion: Narrow, auditable operational reversal of the prediction - not a causal claim that meaning suppresses holonomy. Cause remains open with multiple live alternatives: activation-strength geometry, feature engagement, dictionary geometry, displacement, manifold proximity, and transport shear.

Abstract: This paper tests whether holonomy concentrates on active sparse-autoencoder (SAE) feature planes in Gemma 2 2B, a concrete operationalization of the broader semantic-concentration prediction. Holonomy is measured at the final-token layer-12 to layer-13 residual-stream readout by carrying a local frame around small loops using the instrument's restricted-Jacobian transport rule, then normalizing the resulting rotation by enclosed area. The design, materiality threshold, analysis, and verdict rules were preregistered and frozen before the analysed measurements were inspected. The prediction was falsified in reverse: active-feature planes carried less holonomy than matched mixed-feature controls, with an adjusted log contrast of -0.29439 and 95% interval [-0.43989, -0.14889]. A magnitude-only explanation was not supported in this design, while the three-way ordering across random, mixed-feature, and active-feature planes was undefined at matched magnitude because common support failed. Post-freeze diagnostics at the same readout supported the area law on a small validation subset, bounded matched-center displacement under a simple paired regression, and identified transport distortion as a live mechanism or confound. The result is therefore a narrow, auditable operational reversal, not a causal claim that meaning suppresses holonomy. The cause remains open, with activation-strength geometry, degree of feature engagement, dictionary geometry, matched-center displacement, activation-manifold proximity, and transport shear as live alternatives.

</details>


### [134] [Uncertainty-Aware Trust Estimation for Multi-LLM Systems via Structured Expert Judgement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20529)
*Jiawei Zheng,Jiazhen Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Cooke-style log weighting for LLM ensembles improves aggregation by estimating model trustworthiness through uncertainty calibration, outperforming traditional methods especially with heterogeneous or unreliable models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM aggregation methods assume all models are equally trustworthy, which is problematic for heterogeneous LLMs where reliability and capabilities vary significantly, making naive aggregation vulnerable to unreliable or adversarial models.

Method: Formulates multi-LLM aggregation as uncertainty-aware trust estimation using structured expert judgment from decision theory. Uses context-aware calibration questions to estimate model reliability based on quality of probabilistic predictions, specifically employing Cooke-style log weighting that penalizes overconfident incorrect predictions and favors well-calibrated experts.

Result: Evaluated on MMLU and MMLU-Pro across homogeneous, heterogeneous, and contaminated expert panels. While aggregation methods perform similarly in homogeneous settings, Cooke weighting becomes critical under heterogeneity and contamination. It achieves superior accuracy-reliability balance and remains robust when unreliable experts are introduced.

Conclusion: Multi-LLM aggregation requires not just combining predictions, but calibrating trust under uncertainty. Cooke weighting provides a principled approach to handle heterogeneous LLM ensembles by estimating and weighting models based on the quality of their uncertainty estimates rather than assuming equal trustworthiness.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) ensembles are increasingly used to improve reliability by combining predictions from multiple LLMs. However, existing aggregation methods typically assume that all models are equally trustworthy, overlooking differences in uncertainty quality. This assumption is poorly suited to heterogeneous LLMs, whose reliability and capability vary significantly, making naive aggregation vulnerable to unreliable or adversarial experts. In this work, we formulate multi-LLM aggregation as a problem of uncertainty-aware trust estimation. We adapt structured expert judgment from decision theory, using context-aware calibration questions to estimate expert reliability based on the quality of its probabilistic predictions. Specifically, we employ Cooke-style log weighting, which penalises overconfident incorrect predictions and favours well-calibrated experts. We evaluate our approach on MMLU and MMLU-Pro across homogeneous, heterogeneous, and contaminated expert panels. Results show that while aggregation methods perform similarly in homogeneous settings, Cooke weighting becomes critical under heterogeneity and contamination. It achieves a superior accuracy-reliability balance and remains robust when unreliable experts are introduced. These findings suggest that Multi-LLM aggregation requires not just combining predictions, but calibrating trust under uncertainty.

</details>


### [135] [CLOE: Christoffel Loss Autoencoder for Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20530)
*Léa Billet,Louise Travé-Massuyès,Elodie Chanthery,Alexandre Gaffet*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CLOE combines autoencoder dimensionality reduction with Christoffel Function-based anomaly detection, using a novel CF-guided loss for better representation learning and achieving superior performance on high-dimensional tabular data with minimal hyperparameter tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Semi-supervised anomaly detection is crucial in fields like process monitoring, healthcare, and finance, but lightweight methods struggle with high-dimensional data and require extensive hyperparameter tuning. Christoffel Function methods are attractive for their simplicity (single hyperparameter) and theoretical foundation, but they don't scale well to high-dimensional settings.

Method: The CLOE method combines an autoencoder for dimensionality reduction with a Christoffel Function-based detector in the latent space. A novel loss function leverages the Christoffel Function to guide the autoencoder toward representations that better capture the support of normal data. The paper also proposes a principled threshold-setting procedure and efficient hyperparameter tuning strategy.

Result: Experiments on multiple high-dimensional tabular anomaly detection benchmarks demonstrate that CLOE achieves superior performance compared to existing methods while preserving the lightweight and low-tuning advantages of Christoffel Function-based approaches.

Conclusion: CLOE successfully addresses the scalability limitations of Christoffel Function methods while maintaining their simplicity advantages, providing an effective solution for high-dimensional semi-supervised anomaly detection with minimal tuning requirements.

Abstract: Semi-supervised anomaly detection plays a key role in diverse fields such as process monitoring, healthcare, and finance. However, lightweight methods often struggle with high-dimensional data and typically require careful tuning of multiple hyperparameters. Among existing approaches, Christoffel Function--based methods are attractive due to their simplicity, requiring at most a single hyperparameter. They also benefit from a well-established theoretical foundation that yields several interesting results for data science. However, their main limitation is poor scalability to high-dimensional settings. In this paper, we introduce CLOE, a new method that combines an autoencoder for dimensionality reduction with a Christoffel Function--based detector applied in the latent space. To better align representation learning with anomaly detection, we design a novel loss function that leverages the Christoffel Function to guide the autoencoder toward representations that better capture the support of the normal data distribution. We further propose a principled procedure to set the detection threshold and an efficient strategy to tune the single remaining hyperparameter. Experiments on multiple high-dimensional tabular anomaly detection benchmarks demonstrate that CLOE achieves superior performance compared to existing methods, while preserving the lightweight and low-tuning advantages of Christoffel Function--based approaches.

</details>


### [136] [Position: Stop Reactively Patching Your Model Every Time and Start Proactive Test-Driven AI Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20532)
*Nadine Chang,Maying Shen,Jialiang Wang,Rafid Mahmood,Jose M. Alvarez*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Modern AI maintenance relies on reactive "flywheels" that patch models based on user errors, but this approach is limited by ignoring broader context and failing to preempt future issues. The paper argues for transitioning to proactive, test-driven flywheels that map feedback to objectives in a structured "test space," proving mathematically that this approach scales better with fewer iterations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI maintenance pipelines use reactive flywheels that observe user errors and patch models accordingly, but these systems fail to consider the broader context of errors within system objectives and cannot preempt potential future edge cases. Additionally, collecting remaining errors becomes statistically difficult due to the long-tail nature of open-world use-cases, leading to more unnecessary iterations.

Method: Proposes transitioning from reactive to proactive test-driven flywheels by creating a structured "test space" that technically maps feedback data to task objectives. This approach involves systematically organizing tests and evaluations to cover potential failure modes before they occur in production.

Result: The paper provides mathematical proof that a proactive flywheel achieves better long-term scaling with fewer iterations than reactive flywheels. The proposed approach helps address the limitations of reactive maintenance by preempting edge cases and reducing unnecessary iteration cycles.

Conclusion: A proactive test-driven flywheel is necessary to overcome the limitations of reactive AI maintenance systems and approach truly generalizable AI systems. By creating structured test spaces that map feedback to objectives, organizations can achieve better scalability with fewer iterations, moving from reactive patching to proactive system improvement.

Abstract: Many modern AI systems are designed to operate under diverse, open-ended, use-cases. To help generalize deployed systems, many deployed-system maintenance pipelines use a reactive AI flywheel that observes emerging feedback from user behavior (errors) and patches the model accordingly. However, when used as the primary maintenance mechanism, these flywheels often ignore the broader context of these errors within the system's objectives, failing to preempt potential future edge cases, which leads to more unnecessary flywheel iterations. Also, it is statistically increasingly difficult to collect remaining errors due to the long-tail nature of open-world use-cases. This position paper argues that a proactive test-driven flywheel is required to address reactive flywheel's limitations and to approach a generalizable system. We advocate for creating a "test space" to technically map feedback data to task objectives, evolving the flywheel from reactive to proactive. We augment our position by mathematically proving a proactive one achieves better long-term scaling with fewer iterations than the reactive flywheel.

</details>


### [137] [Grounding Investor Views: Neural Predicates in the Black-Litterman Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20533)
*Marcos Florencio*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Neural predicates generate structured probabilistic views for Black-Litterman portfolio construction, replacing subjective uncertainty with data-driven confidence measures while maintaining interpretability and differentiability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Black-Litterman portfolio construction requires investors to specify subjective views with explicit uncertainty estimates, which is difficult to scale and remains largely subjective. There's a need for a more systematic, data-driven approach to view generation and uncertainty quantification.

Method: The authors propose using neural predicates as a structured probabilistic mechanism. Structured financial data is processed through a compositional hierarchy of neural predicates that output probability distributions over market stances. These distributions are then mapped to the Black-Litterman model's pick matrix (P), view return vector (q), and view uncertainty matrix (Ω). View confidence is derived from predicate output distributions.

Result: The approach provides a data-driven alternative to subjective uncertainty elicitation. The resulting method is interpretable (portfolio weights can be traced back through predicate logical chains to underlying data) and fully differentiable, enabling end-to-end learning.

Conclusion: Neural predicates offer a formal, scalable, and interpretable framework for automating view generation in Black-Litterman portfolio construction, replacing subjective uncertainty estimates with data-driven probabilistic measures while maintaining full differentiability for optimization.

Abstract: Portfolio construction under the Black-Litterman model requires investors to specify views on asset returns alongside explicit uncertainty estimates -- a process that remains largely subjective and difficult to scale. We propose a formal approach in which neural predicates serve as a structured, probabilistic mechanism for view generation. In our formulation, structured financial analysis data is processed through a compositional hierarchy of neural predicates whose outputs -- probability distributions over market stances -- are mapped to the pick matrix $\mathbf{P}$, the view return vector $\mathbf{q}$, and the view uncertainty matrix $\boldsymbolΩ$ of the Black-Litterman model. View confidence is derived from predicate output distributions, providing a data-driven alternative to subjective uncertainty elicitation. The resulting approach is interpretable, in the sense that any portfolio weight can be traced back through the predicate's logical chain to the underlying data, and fully differentiable, enabling end-to-end learning.

</details>


### [138] [A Graph Neural Network approach to zero-shot Digital Twins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20535)
*Alicia Tierz,Icíar Alfaro,David González,Elías Cueto*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Zero-shot digital twins framework that combines visual perception with physics-informed graph neural networks for real-time simulation of unseen geometries without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional predictive digital twins require extensive retraining when physical domains or boundary conditions change, limiting flexibility and real-time applicability.

Method: Uses Thermodynamics-Informed Graph Neural Network architecture with metriplectic thermodynamic formalism, plus auxiliary GNN for inferring unobservable fields. Integrates real-time visual perception via deep segmentation and optical flow with continuous closed-loop data assimilation.

Result: Demonstrated extreme generalization across viscoelastic beam deformations and viscous fluid sloshing on novel geometries without retraining, achieving real-time performance (~25 ms/frame) and enabling AR projection of latent variables.

Conclusion: The framework enables zero-shot digital twins that are geometry-agnostic, physics-accurate, and operate in real-time, bridging sim-to-real gap through continuous data assimilation and visual perception integration.

Abstract: Traditional Predictive Digital Twins often remain geometrically rigid, requiring extensive retraining or fine-tuning whenever the underlying physical domain or boundary conditions change. To overcome this limitation, we present a novel framework for \textit{Zero-Shot Digital Twins} that seamlessly couples real-time visual perception with a geometry-agnostic, physics-informed reasoning engine. At the core of our architecture is the Thermodynamics-Informed Graph Neural Network architecture, a Geometric Deep Learning solver grounded in a metriplectic thermodynamic formalism that enforces energy conservation and non-negative entropy production locally through graph message passing. The framework integrates an auxiliary Graph Neural Network to infer unobservable fields (such as stress tensors or velocity and energy distributions) directly from sparse initial visual boundaries, mitigating numerical start-up transients. To bridge the sim-to-real gap, we implement a continuous closed-loop data assimilation mechanism; the pipeline tracks macroscopic deformations and free-surface fluid boundaries in real-time using deep segmentation networks combined with sparse optical flow, dynamically correcting the autoregressive simulation rollout and eliminating numerical drift. To test the validity of our approach, we demonstrate the extreme generalization capabilities of our approach across two disparate physical regimes: the large deformations of a viscoelastic beam and the non-linear sloshing of a viscous fluid. In both scenarios, the unified framework instantiates physically accurate simulations on novel, unseen geometries without case-specific retraining, operating well within real-time latency budgets (approximately 25 ms per frame) and enabling the direct projection of latent mechanical variables via Augmented Reality.

</details>


### [139] [ReliableTableQA:How Much Supervision Does Reliability Annotation Need?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20537)
*Huei-Chung Hu,Hsin-Tai Wu,Koyo Kobayashi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ReliableTableQA trains LLMs to identify statistically unreliable tabular QA answers using a novel reliability taxonomy, showing that minimal supervised fine-tuning outperforms reinforcement learning for this task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise analytics systems often return statistically meaningless results (small samples, wide confidence intervals, confounding) with confidence, creating a failure mode called Unreliable Confident Answer Rate (UCAR). Existing QA systems don't flag such statistical reliability issues.

Method: Developed: (1) 10-category reliability taxonomy covering statistical hazards; (2) program-first data pipeline generating 50k labeled examples via context-free grammar over retail schemas; (3) schema-stratified SFT/GRPO training study measuring supervision needs.

Result: Minimal SFT (200 examples) raises reliability-flag F1 from 0.61 to 0.98, drives UCAR to zero, and generalizes to unseen domains (0.997 F1). GRPO only helps when SFT is under-trained (100 examples) and provides no benefit once SFT is adequate, refuting common assumptions.

Conclusion: Reliability annotation is a data-efficiency problem requiring minimal supervision. The study delineates precise conditions where reinforcement fine-tuning pays off vs. when it doesn't, with significant implications for enterprise analytics deployment.

Abstract: We introduce ReliableTableQA, a framework for training an LLM to annotate the statistical reliability of tabular QA results, not whether the query is answerable, but whether the computed answer is statistically meaningful. In real enterprise analytics, a syntactically correct SQL query can return a value that is based on too small a sample, has an excessively wide confidence interval, or is too confounded to support action. Existing systems answer confidently in all such cases, a failure we quantify as the Unreliable Confident Answer Rate (UCAR). We contribute (1) a ten-category reliability taxonomy (R1-R10) covering hazards such as small-sample aggregates, multiple-comparison inflation, and distribution-tail mismatch; (2) a program-first data pipeline that generates 50,000 reliability-labeled training examples from a context-free grammar over public retail schemas, with schema-stratified SFT/GRPO splits; and (3) a controlled study of how much supervision calibrated reliability annotation actually requires. We find that a small, schema-stratified SFT set is remarkably sufficient: 200 examples raise reliability-flag F1 from 0.61 to 0.98 and parse rate from 0.52 to 1.00, drive UCAR to zero, and yield a model that generalizes to an unseen retail domain (Rel-F1 0.997 on held-out H&M). Against this strong SFT baseline, GRPO, commonly assumed to be essential, helps only when SFT is under-trained (+0.06-0.16 exact-flag-set match at 100 examples, in- and out-of-distribution) and provides no measurable benefit once SFT is adequate, a null result we confirm across a hard compound-flag slice, a strict exact-match metric, and out-of-distribution evaluation. Our findings reframe reliability annotation as a data-efficiency problem and delineate precisely when reinforcement fine-tuning does and does not pay off.

</details>


### [140] [Codec-Gauge: Learning Compression-Friendly Gauges for Transformer KV Caches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20538)
*Yitao Jiang,Yaoqing Yang,Luyang Zhao,Muhao Chen,Devin Balkcom*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Codec-Gauge learns small orthogonal channel transforms to optimize KV-cache compression/quantization by concentrating energy in low-frequency layouts, improving fidelity without changing model weights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context Transformer inference requires efficient KV-cache compression/quantization, and prior work shows channel basis affects compression fidelity. The authors aim to improve compression quality by optimizing the coordinate system used for compression backends.

Method: Introduces Codec-Gauge, a post-training cache-coordinate layer that learns small orthogonal channel transforms around existing compression/quantization backends. Uses frequency-distribution objective combining token-channel DCT spectral-centroid loss with smooth rate proxy to concentrate KV energy in low-frequency layouts.

Result: Across six models at 3, 4, and 6 bits/value, learned gauges reduce zfp KL divergence by 44.0% on average vs raw coordinates, outperform random/Hadamard/DCT/PCA/KLT controls. Same gauges improve quality for block-uniform and KIVI-style quantization. Experiments on 27B model and long-context tasks reproduce quality trends.

Conclusion: Cache-coordinate geometry is a practical post-training variable for improving compression fidelity without changing model weights, attention semantics, or backend coding rules.

Abstract: Long-context Transformer inference increasingly relies on KV-cache compression or quantization. Prior rotation and transform-coding results suggest that the channel basis of each key/value vector affects how faithfully a fixed backend preserves model behavior. We introduce Codec-Gauge, a post-training cache-coordinate layer that learns small orthogonal channel transforms around existing compression and quantization backends. Its frequency-distribution objective combines a token-channel DCT spectral-centroid loss with a smooth rate proxy to concentrate KV energy in low-frequency codec-facing layouts. We evaluate actual compression and decompression using measured bytes and rolling compressed-history scoring. Across six models at $3$, $4$, and $6$ bits/value, learned gauges reduce zfp KL divergence by $44.0\%$ on average relative to raw coordinates and outperform random, Hadamard, DCT, and PCA/KLT controls. The same gauges improve quality preservation for block-uniform and KIVI-style quantization. Experiments on a 27B model and long-context task prompts reproduce the quality trend, while serial storage and timing measurements validate the implemented compressed-cache paths. These results establish cache-coordinate geometry as a practical post-training variable for improving compression fidelity without changing model weights, attention semantics, or backend coding rules.

</details>


### [141] [Leveraging Biokinetic Knowledge Priors for Data-Scarce Bioprocess Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20539)
*Kyunghoon Hur,Eunjung Jeon,Hyun Woo Kim,Gyubok Lee,Seongjun Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Simulation pre-training of generic neural decoders matches performance of bio-structured decoders for bioreactor optimization, offering data-efficient deep learning under data scarcity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning has limited impact on biomanufacturing due to data scarcity - bioreactor experiments are expensive, time-consuming, and rarely shared publicly, leaving researchers with very few experiments to work with.

Method: Systematic study comparing two methods of injecting biokinetic ODE knowledge into neural networks: 1) data-level prior that pre-trains a generic decoder on simulated ODE curves, and 2) architecture-level prior that embeds the ODE directly inside the decoder structure.

Result: Both prior-injection methods consistently outperform no-prior baselines across 11 datasets and 7 microbial species. The central finding is that the two approaches are substitutable - a generic decoder pre-trained on simulation matches the performance of a fully bio-structured decoder trained on real data.

Conclusion: Simulation pre-training offers a simple, data-efficient recipe for applying deep learning to bioprocess optimization under data scarcity, making it possible to leverage decades of biokinetic knowledge without complex architecture modifications.

Abstract: While deep learning has accelerated drug discovery, its impact on biomanufacturing has been considerably more limited. The reason is data scarcity. Bioreactor experiments are high-cost, take days to weeks, and are rarely shared in public form, leaving each research work with only a handful of experiments. The domain itself, however, is rich in prior knowledge. Biokinetic ordinary differential equation (ODE) models have described microbial growth for decades, yet how to inject this knowledge into a neural network has not been studied systematically. We present the first systematic study of how to inject this ODE knowledge into a neural network, comparing a data-level prior that pre-trains a generic decoder on simulated ODE curves against an architecture-level prior that embeds the ODE inside the decoder. Both consistently outperform no-prior baselines across 11 datasets and 7 microbial species. Our central finding is that the two are substitutable. A generic decoder pre-trained on simulation matches a fully bio-structured decoder trained on real data. Simulation pre-training therefore offers a simple, data-efficient recipe for deep learning under bioprocess data scarcity.

</details>


### [142] [From Atoms to Entropy: Optimal Noise Allocation for Diffusion Training in the Convex Regime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20540)
*Luca Ambrogioni,Giulio Franzese,Alberto Foresti,Gabriel Raya,Bac Nguyen,Georgios Batzolis,Yuhta Takida,Naoki Murata,Chieh-Hsin Lai,Yuki Mitsufuji*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A statistical framework for optimizing noise schedules in diffusion models reveals optimal training uses finitely many noise levels, with an entropic proxy improving training efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current noise schedules in diffusion model training are based on heuristics or empirical tuning rather than principled optimization, creating a need for a theoretical framework to determine optimal noise-level allocation.

Method: Developed a statistical framework for asymptotically optimal noise-level allocation, analyzing both fully coupled and independent-learner regimes. Used convexity/Polyak-Lojasiewicz assumptions, random-matrix analysis, and information-theoretic proxy based on generative entropy rate.

Result: In fully coupled regime: optimized schedules concentrate on finitely many noise levels. In independent-learner regime: optimal decoupled sampling density is proportional to square root of generative entropy rate. Validated in controlled settings (Dirac mixtures, manifolds, MNIST) and larger-scale experiments showing improved training efficiency.

Conclusion: Theoretical framework reveals atomic minimizers for noise schedules, with square-root entropy scheduling providing an effective proxy that improves training efficiency while remaining competitive with existing heuristics.

Abstract: How should a diffusion model decide which noise levels to train on, and how much? Despite the importance of this choice, current noise schedules are based largely on heuristics or empirical tuning. Here, we develop a general statistical framework for studying asymptotically optimal noise-level allocation in diffusion training. Our first main result concerns the fully coupled regime, where information can spread between different time points. Under convexity or Polyak-Lojasiewicz-type assumptions, we show that the optimized training schedule admits an atomic minimizer, concentrated on finitely many noise levels. Our second main result specializes this framework to an idealized independent-learner regime, intended to model temporal specialization in neural networks. Under an additional feature-noise decoupling condition, a random-matrix analysis leads to an information-theoretic proxy: the decoupled sampling density is proportional to the square root of the generative entropy rate, the rate at which conditional entropy grows along the forward process. We test these predictions in controlled settings where the coupled objective can be optimized directly, including Dirac mixtures, low-dimensional manifolds, and MNIST. In these settings, the optimized schedules are consistently finite-support, while the smooth entropic proxy closely tracks the atomic optimum in neural-network models and breaks down mainly in the fully coupled parametric case, as the theory suggests. We then evaluate the entropic schedule in larger-scale experiments, where full schedule optimization is currently intractable. The results indicate that square-root entropy scheduling can substantially improve training efficiency on discrete domains and remains competitive with standard EDM-style heuristics on continuous images.

</details>


### [143] [HypNO: A Graph-Based Neural Operator with Physics-Informed Message Passing for Hyperbolic Conservation Laws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20541)
*Dimitrije Ždrale,Cassie An Jeng,Katie Wang,Sonia Vanier,Alexandre Bayen,Hossein Nick Zinat Matin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HypNO is a graph-based neural operator that accurately solves hyperbolic conservation laws like traffic flow models, capturing shocks and discontinuities while respecting physics-informed constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing operator-learning methods struggle with hyperbolic conservation laws like traffic flow models, which combine global transport with shock formation, creating a challenging test case that requires proper treatment of upwinding and entropy admissibility near shocks.

Method: HypNO operates directly on space-time graphs of finite-volume cells using adjacency-factored, physics-informed message passing to respect upwinding and entropy admissibility near shocks. It's applied to Lighthill-Whitham-Richards (LWR) and Aw-Rascle-Zhang (ARZ) traffic-flow models.

Result: HypNO accurately predicts solution snapshots across various initial conditions while successfully capturing shocks and discontinuities in the solutions, demonstrating effectiveness on challenging traffic flow benchmark problems.

Conclusion: HypNO provides a promising neural operator approach for solving hyperbolic conservation laws, effectively handling the dual challenges of global transport and shock formation while maintaining physical constraints.

Abstract: We introduce HypNO, a graph-based neural operator for scalar hyperbolic conservation laws. HypNO operates directly on a space-time graph of finite-volume cells and uses adjacency-factored, physics-informed message passing to respect upwinding and entropy admissibility near shocks. We benchmark the architecture on the Lighthill-Whitham-Richards (LWR) and Aw-Rascle-Zhang (ARZ) traffic-flow models, a stress test for operator-learning methods because of their simultaneous global transport and shock formation. HypNO predicts solution snapshots accurately across a range of initial conditions while capturing the shocks and discontinuities of the solution.

</details>


### [144] [Improving Access to Essential Medicines via Decision-Aware Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20542)
*Angel Tsai-Hsuan Chung,Jatu Abdulai,Patrick Bayoh,Lawrence Sandi,Francis Smart,Hamsa Bastani,Osbert Bastani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A machine learning framework for equitable allocation of essential medicines in LMICs, tested in Sierra Leone with 19% increase in medicine consumption in treated districts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Critical challenge in LMIC healthcare systems is efficient and equitable allocation of scarce resources, especially essential medicines, complicated by limited high-quality data that restricts traditional data-driven techniques.

Method: Novel decision-aware machine learning framework using multi-task learning for sample efficiency and catalytic priors for equitable allocation, deployed as staggered nationwide decision support tool in Sierra Leone.

Result: 19% increase in consumption of allocated products in treated districts based on econometric evaluation; system scaled nationwide covering ~2 million women and children under five.

Conclusion: Machine learning methods can improve efficiency at very low cost in resource-constrained global health settings, as demonstrated by successful nationwide deployment and scaling.

Abstract: A critical challenge in healthcare systems in low- and middle-income countries (LMICs) is the efficient and equitable allocation of scarce resources, particularly essential medicines. This problem is complicated by limited high-quality data, which restricts the applicability of traditional data-driven techniques. We propose a novel decision-aware machine learning framework for essential medicines allocation, which additionally leverages multi-task learning to ensure sample efficiency and catalytic priors to ensure equitable allocation. In collaboration with the Sierra Leone national government, we performed a staggered, nationwide deployment of our system as a decision support tool. Our econometric evaluation finds an estimated 19% increase in consumption of allocated products in treated districts, demonstrating its efficacy at improving access to essential medicines. Our tool was subsequently scaled nationwide, covering an estimated 2 million women and children under five. Our work demonstrates how machine learning methods can improve efficiency at very low cost in resource-constrained global health settings.

</details>


### [145] [When RLVR Shrinks the Reasoning Boundary: Diagnosing Pass@k Inversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20543)
*Todd Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RLVR improves single-sample accuracy but can worsen performance under repeated sampling (pass@k inversion) due to losing rare correct trajectories. Per-Problem Base Anchoring (PBA) preserves these trajectories by anchoring risky prompts to base distribution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) exhibits a paradoxical failure: it improves one-sample accuracy (pass@1) but makes models worse under repeated sampling (pass@k), particularly for boundary prompts where rare correct trajectories may disappear during RL training.

Method: Proposes Per-Problem Base Anchoring (PBA): a simple method that sharpens prompts with sufficient frozen-base correct evidence and anchors risky prompts to the base distribution to preserve rare verifier-positive trajectories that would otherwise be lost in RLVR optimization.

Result: PBA improves both pass@1 and high-budget coverage over matched GRPO across three training seeds on Omni-MATH-Test, using MATH500 as secondary validation. Diagnostic study shows ordinary GRPO loses base-solvable boundary prompts while PBA preserves rare correct trajectories.

Conclusion: RLVR risks pass@k inversion by eliminating rare correct trajectories. PBA demonstrates that post-training optimization should consider which prompts are safe to optimize, not just how strongly to optimize. The issue applies beyond math to vision-language agents with external verifiers.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) can improve one-sample accuracy while making a model worse under repeated sampling. We study this pass@k inversion: after training, the policy may solve fewer distinct problems than its base model at large $k$. The failure concentrates on boundary prompts, where the base model contains rare correct trajectories that are recoverable by sampling but too sparse to reliably appear in finite RLVR rollout groups. We argue that a two-mode account explains this as an absence-of-evidence failure: rare correct trajectories may disappear before RLVR samples and reinforces them often enough. The main contribution is this diagnostic and mechanistic framing. Per-Problem Base Anchoring (PBA) is a deliberately simple proof-of-concept: sharpen prompts with sufficient frozen-base correct evidence, and anchor risky prompts to the base distribution. Across three training seeds on Omni-MATH-Test, with MATH500 as a secondary high-coverage validation benchmark, PBA improves both \PassK{1} and high-budget coverage over matched GRPO. A 3000-prompt regime-controlled diagnostic study is consistent across seeds with the expected signature: ordinary GRPO loses base-solvable boundary prompts, while PBA preserves rare verifier-positive trajectories. We use mathematical verifiers as a controlled testbed for verifier-guided optimization; the same pass@k inversion risk applies to ECCV-relevant vision-language agents when repeated visual, spatial, or chart-reasoning attempts are checked by external tools or verifiers. Reasoning post-training should decide not only how strongly to optimize, but which prompts are safe to optimize.

</details>


### [146] [Conflict Resolution under Degraded Surveillance in Air Corridors Using Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20547)
*Esrat Farhana Dulia,Syed Arbab Mohd Shihab,Caleb Adams,Ruben Del Rosario*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Deep Q-Network-based MARL framework enables decentralized conflict resolution for heterogeneous aircraft in 3D corridors under degraded surveillance conditions, achieving effective trade-offs between safety and capacity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Advanced Air Mobility operations require safe separation of aircraft when surveillance data is noisy, delayed, incomplete, or temporarily unavailable, necessitating robust decentralized conflict resolution methods.

Method: Deep Q-Network-based Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning framework with separate policies for two aircraft categories (small UAVs and eVTOLs), using local observations and 14-action space including course maintenance, turning, vertical maneuvering, landing, and speed control.

Result: Evaluation across 90 traffic scenarios shows agents maintain motion 79% of the time under safe conditions, conflicts resolved within 1s, turning (33%) and speed control (25%) are primary conflict responses, with trade-offs between safety and capacity identified through six Pareto-optimal configurations.

Conclusion: The MARL framework effectively supports simulation-based evaluation of safer conflict-resolution strategies for heterogeneous aircraft under realistic degraded surveillance conditions, demonstrating practical solutions for AAM safety-capacity trade-offs.

Abstract: Safe Advanced Air Mobility operations require aircraft to maintain separation when surveillance information is noisy, delayed, incomplete, or temporarily unavailable. This study develops a Deep Q-Network-based Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning framework for decentralized conflict resolution among heterogeneous small unmanned aerial vehicles and electric vertical takeoff and landing aircraft operating within a structured three-dimensional corridor. Separate policies are trained for the two aircraft categories using local observations and a 14-action space that includes maintaining course, turning, vertical maneuvering, landing, and speed control. The simulation incorporates aircraft-specific dynamics, energy use, corridor constraints, observation noise, communication delay, information dropout, wind disturbance, actuator uncertainty, and model uncertainty. The trained policies are evaluated across 90 combinations of traffic density and minimum separation thresholds. Loss-of-separation frequency and duration generally increase with traffic density and separation requirements, although most events are resolved within 1s. Under safe conditions, agents maintain their motion approximately 79% of the time. During conflicts, turning accounts for 33% of actions, followed by maintaining motion at 29%, speed control at 25%, and vertical maneuvers at 13%. Six Pareto-optimal configurations reveal trade-offs between safety and corridor capacity. The framework supports the simulation-based evaluation of safer AAM conflict-resolution strategies under degraded surveillance conditions.

</details>


### [147] [SOAP, Muon, and Beyond: Pushing LLM Pretraining Scales](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20548)
*Mikail Khona,Aditya Vavre,Boxiang Wang,Deyu Fu,Hao Wu,Mike Chrzanowski,Bryan Catanzaro,Dheevatsa Mudigere,Jeff Pool,Michael Lightstone,Mohammad Shoeybi,Mostofa Patwary,Nima Tajbakhsh,Tijmen Blankevoort*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Enhanced higher-order optimizers (SOAP/Muon) overcome practical challenges for large-scale LLM pretraining, outperforming AdamW while maintaining stability at massive batch sizes with a layer-wise distributed implementation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Higher-order optimizers like Muon and SOAP offer faster convergence than AdamW but face computational cost and numerical stability challenges that limit adoption at scale in large-scale LLM pretraining.

Method: 1. Identify and fix SOAP instabilities at large batch sizes with algorithmic modifications (per-step QR orthogonalization, improved preconditioning strategies). 2. Conduct unified empirical study using update-RMS matching for fair learning rate transfer across optimizers. 3. Introduce layer-wise distributed optimizer compatible with Megatron-LM for efficient large-scale training. 4. Build system-level improvements to accelerate the layer-wise implementation.

Result: SOAP and Muon consistently outperform AdamW on multi-billion-parameter models trained on trillions of tokens. They maintain training stability and quality at batch sizes up to 100M tokens where AdamW degrades. The distributed implementation enables efficient training at scale.

Conclusion: Enhanced higher-order optimizers are practical and superior to AdamW for large-scale LLM pretraining, offering faster convergence while maintaining stability at extreme batch sizes, with open-source implementation provided for community adoption.

Abstract: Higher-order optimizers such as Muon and SOAP offer faster convergence than AdamW, but their computational cost and numerical stability challenges have limited adoption at scale. In this work, we adapt and enhance preconditioned gradient methods to overcome the practical challenges of large-scale LLM pretraining. We first identify instabilities in SOAP at large batch sizes and propose algorithmic modifications including per-step QR orthogonalization and improved preconditioning strategies that eliminate loss spikes and enable stable training in these regimes. We then present a unified empirical study of SOAP, Muon, and AdamW using update-RMS matching to ensure fair learning rate transfer across optimizers. As part of this analysis, we empirically evaluate the orthogonalization quality of Muon. Our experiments on multi-billion-parameter models trained on trillions of tokens reveal that SOAP and Muon consistently outperform AdamW at the scales we tested. Notably, at batch sizes of up to 100M tokens for next-token prediction, these optimizers maintain training stability and quality while AdamW degrades. To enable efficient training at large scale, we introduce a layer-wise distributed optimizer compatible with Megatron-LM. Our implementation balances memory and hides communication while avoiding approximations to the optimizer computations, thus retaining their convergence benefits. Additionally, we identify and build specific system-level improvements to further accelerate our layer-wise implementation. To support the research community, we release a codebase that contains emerging algorithms for optimization: https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/Emerging-Optimizers

</details>


### [148] [SevDiff: Severity-Conditioned Diffusion for Long-Tail Conflict Trajectory Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20549)
*Eni Solomon Laughter*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SevDiff is a severity-conditioned diffusion model that generates vehicle interaction trajectories matching requested Time-to-Collision (TTC) values with high precision, addressing the rarity of conflict events in ADAS evaluation datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ADAS evaluation datasets are heavily biased toward routine driving with rare genuine vehicle-to-vehicle conflict events. The rarer the event, the higher the cost when ADAS fails. Existing generative approaches cannot accept target TTC values as input and be held accountable for producing them within measurable error.

Method: SevDiff is a severity-conditioned denoising diffusion probabilistic model (DDPM) that accepts requested minimum TTC values as scalar conditioning signals and generates paired vehicle interaction trajectories. Trained on 468 interaction windows from the UTE SQM-W-1 expressway weaving-section dataset (1,041 vehicles, 822,691 observations after smoothing).

Result: Achieves 100% hit-rate within +/-0.5s for TTC targets of 0.5-1.5s and 97-99% at 2.0-2.5s, degrading gracefully to 39% at TTC = 5.0s. Generated kinematic features are physically plausible (max 4.7% out-of-range across 12 features, >96.5% samples with no negative speed/gap values).

Conclusion: SevDiff successfully generates severity-conditioned vehicle interactions with high precision, providing a physically interpretable generator characterization through hit-rate degradation patterns that reflect conditioning signal strength relative to training prior.

Abstract: Trajectory datasets used in ADAS evaluation are heavily biased toward routine driving; genuine vehicle-to-vehicle conflict events are rare, and the rarer the event, the higher the cost when an ADAS system fails to handle it. Existing generative approaches address this imbalance by conditioning on scene-level properties - spatial goals, agent structure, or natural-language adversarial objectives - but none can accept a target Time-to-Collision (TTC) value as input and be held to producing it within a measurable error. This paper introduces SevDiff, a severity-conditioned denoising diffusion probabilistic model (DDPM) that accepts a requested minimum TTC value as a scalar conditioning signal and generates paired vehicle interaction trajectories whose realized conflict severity matches the request, evaluated through a hit-rate metric. Trained on 468 interaction windows extracted from the UTE SQM-W-1 expressway weaving-section dataset (1,041 vehicles, 822,691 observations after smoothing), SevDiff achieves 100% hit-rate within +/-0.5 s for TTC targets of 0.5-1.5 s and 97-99% at 2.0-2.5 s, with graceful degradation to 39% at TTC = 5.0 s. Generated kinematic features are physically plausible, with a maximum out-of-range rate of 4.7% across 12 features and no negative speed or gap values in more than 96.5% of samples. The hit-rate degradation pattern is physically interpretable as the strength of the conditioning signal relative to the training prior, making it a precision characterization of the generator rather than a pass/fail result.

</details>


### [149] [Beyond SBDD: Geometric Deep Learning in Polypharmacology and Multi-target Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20550)
*Tianming Han,Zhijie Pan,Wenchi Ge,Qi Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Geometric deep learning enables rational design of multi-target drugs by capturing 3D structural constraints, overcoming limitations of traditional single-target approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The "one drug, one target" paradigm fails for multifactorial diseases due to compensatory pathways and drug resistance. Polypharmacology offers promise but faces computational challenges in designing ligands that satisfy geometric constraints of multiple targets simultaneously.

Method: Systematic survey of geometric deep learning architectures including invariant graph neural networks and SE(3)-equivariant diffusion models. Analysis covers geometric embeddings for binding pockets, multi-target bioactivity prediction via heterogeneous graph fusion, and de novo generation of dual-target ligands. Focus on structure-conditioned generative algorithms combining diffusion models with reinforcement learning.

Result: GDL provides powerful integrative approach to capture 3D structural interdependencies, enabling characterization of shared binding pockets, multi-target prediction, and generation of dual-target ligands that resolve geometric conflicts between competing binding sites.

Conclusion: GDL facilitates paradigm shift from serendipitous exploration to rational, structure-driven polypharmacological molecular engineering, providing clear guide for next-generation therapeutics design.

Abstract: The traditional "one drug, one target" paradigm of structure-based drug design (SBDD) frequently proves inadequate for treating multifactorial diseases such as cancer and neurodegenerative disorders, owing to compensatory signaling pathways and the emergence of drug resistance. While polypharmacology offers a synergistic therapeutic strategy, the rational design of ligands capable of simultaneously satisfying the geometric constraints imposed by multiple targets remains a major computational bottleneck. This review positions geometric deep learning (GDL) as a powerful integrative approach to overcome these limitations. We systematically survey GDL architectures ranging from invariant graph neural networks to SE(3)-equivariant diffusion models that harness non-Euclidean molecular data to capture intrinsic three-dimensional (3D) structural interdependencies. We critically analyze GDL applications across three core dimensions, including the characterization of shared binding pockets via geometric embeddings, multi-target bioactivity prediction through heterogeneous graph fusion, and de novo generation of dual-target ligands. Particular emphasis is placed on emerging structure-conditioned generative algorithms that integrate diffusion models with reinforcement learning to autonomously resolve complex geometric conflicts between competing binding sites. Furthermore, we evaluate the pivotal role of multimodal omics integration and specialized geometric benchmarking infrastructures in validating these models. By synthesizing these methodological advances, this review elucidates the paradigm shift in drug discovery from serendipitous exploration to rational, structure-driven polypharmacological molecular engineering, thereby providing a clear, structured guide for navigating the complexities of next-generation therapeutics.

</details>


### [150] [SenCos-GEM: SENet-Calibrated and Law-of-Cosines-Constrained Geometry-Enhanced Molecular Representation for Property Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20551)
*Tianming Han,Li Zhang,Qi Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SenCos-GEM is a geometry-enhanced molecular representation learning framework that uses physics-guided geometric consistency loss and SENet calibration for robust 3D molecular property prediction with state-of-the-art results on MoleculeNet benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing 3D GNN self-supervised learning methods lack explicit physical constraints, are sensitive to geometric noise from empirical force fields, and overlook dynamic feature modulation during downstream adaptation, leading to catastrophic forgetting and negative transfer.

Method: Introduces a decoupled geometry-enhanced framework with: 1) physics-guided geometric consistency loss based on law of cosines for high-fidelity spatial priors, 2) lightweight SENet modules as task-specific adapters, and 3) dual-modulation prediction head combining FiLM and SENet for dynamic feature recalibration.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art on MoleculeNet benchmarks, with 12.9% RMSE reduction on FreeSolv, 5.3% RMSE reduction on Lipophilicity, and 8.2% MAE reduction on QM9. Shows superior capability in distinguishing stereoisomers and discriminating conformational perturbations.

Conclusion: SenCos-GEM represents a significant breakthrough in molecular property prediction by incorporating explicit physical constraints and dynamic feature modulation, enabling robust 3D spatial modeling with competitive performance across diverse tasks.

Abstract: Effective molecular representation learning is crucial for accurate molecular property prediction. Recently, numerous self-supervised learning (SSL) approaches leveraging 3D GNNs have been developed to capture comprehensive 3D structural information for drug discovery. However, existing methods lack explicit physical constraints and are highly susceptible to geometric noise induced by coarse empirical force fields during large-scale pre-training.Furthermore, they overlook dynamic feature modulation during downstream adaptation, often resulting in catastrophic forgetting and negative transfer. To address these limitations, we introduce SenCos-GEM, a novel explicitly decoupled geometry-enhanced molecular representation learning framework that incorporates SENet-calibrated and law-of-cosines-constrained enhancements. SenCos-GEM employs a physics-guided geometric consistency loss based on the law of cosines to derive high-fidelity and mathematically invariant 3D spatial priors. In addition, lightweight Squeeze-and-Excitation (SE) modules are integrated into the backbone as task-specific adapters, while a dual-modulation prediction head combines Feature-wise Linear Modulation (FiLM) and SENet mechanisms to enable dynamic feature recalibration. SenCos-GEM demonstrates highly competitive performance across diverse classification and regression tasks on MoleculeNet benchmark, establishing new state-of-the-art results specifically on 3D conformation-sensitive regression tasks, such as FreeSolv, Lipophilicity, and QM9, achieving relative error reductions of 12.9% (RMSE), 5.3% (RMSE), and 8.2% (MAE), respectively. Moreover, our model exhibits superior capability in distinguishing stereoisomers and discriminating conformational perturbations, underscoring its robust spatial modeling performance. Collectively, SenCos-GEM represents a significant breakthrough in accurate molecular property prediction.

</details>


### [151] [Thermodynamic Weight Decay: Exploring Grokking Acceleration via Attention Specific Heat](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20552)
*Chitraansh Pandey*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A method called CvAdamW that uses attention variance as a specific heat to predict and accelerate grokking (delayed generalization) in transformers by dynamically adjusting weight decay when phase transitions are detected.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Grokking (delayed generalization after memorization) wastes compute resources and is unpredictable. The paper builds on recent findings that transformer attention is isomorphic to thermodynamic systems, aiming to leverage this insight to make grokking more predictable and efficient.

Method: Treat attention logit variance as specific heat (Cv); monitor Cv online to detect peaks that precede generalization; introduce CvAdamW which dynamically scales weight decay upon detecting phase transitions; address failure modes with memorization gate and exponential-moving-average shock absorber; propose scale-invariant z-score reformulation.

Result: On modular arithmetic (a+b mod 97), CvAdamW enables grokking at epoch 2802 vs. baseline that never groks in 4000 epochs. Cold-start variant reduces mean grokking latency by 257 epochs (6.0%), improves 8 of 10 seeds. Cv peaks before grokking in all 10 seeds.

Conclusion: Neural networks expose detectable precursors of impending generalization transitions; physically motivated proportional interventions can facilitate generalization within fixed compute budgets. The method provides a way to make grokking more predictable and efficient.

Abstract: Grokking -- the delayed generalization of neural networks long after they have memorized their training data -- wastes thousands of training epochs and is notoriously unpredictable. Building on the recent result that Transformer attention is formally isomorphic to a thermodynamic system, we treat the variance of attention logits as a specific heat Cv and show that its peak reliably precedes the generalization transition. We introduce CvAdamW, a drop-in AdamW variant that monitors Cv online and injects thermal energy by dynamically scaling weight decay when a phase transition is detected. Through a strictly iterative development process we identify three failure modes -- initialization noise, mini-batch micro-ripples, and slingshot blinding -- and resolve them with a memorization gate and an exponential-moving-average shock absorber. On modular arithmetic (a+b mod 97), CvAdamW enables grokking at epoch 2802 in a 4000-epoch budget where the baseline never groks. We further propose a scale-invariant z-score reformulation that removes task-specific hyperparameters, and evaluate it across 10 paired seeds. A paired analysis shows the cold-start variant reduces mean grokking latency by 257 epochs (6.0%; median 166 epochs; Wilcoxon p=0.049, Cohen's d=0.68, bootstrap 95% CI [53,489]), improving 8 of 10 seeds; on this single task Cv peaks before grokking in all 10 seeds. Our results indicate that neural networks may expose detectable precursors of impending generalization transitions, and that a physically motivated, proportional intervention can facilitate generalization within a fixed compute budget. Code and data are public.

</details>


### [152] [Double-Scoring: Reliable Extraction of Strong Lottery Tickets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20555)
*Bryce A. Christopherson,Jack Baretz,Darian Colgrove,Salah Dandan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Double-scoring method improves extraction of strong lottery tickets (accurate subnetworks before training) by augmenting score space to avoid layerwise sparsity search, outperforming existing methods with lower hyperparameter sensitivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The strong lottery ticket hypothesis suggests that overparameterized random networks contain subnetworks that are already accurate before training, but reliable extraction of these subnetworks remains difficult. Existing methods like edge-popup face bottlenecks with layerwise sparsity selection.

Method: Introduces double-scoring, an augmented score-space parameterization that replaces layerwise sparsity search with optimization over enlarged score tensors. This method preserves access to all original-coordinate masks and can be interpreted as edge-popup on a zero-augmented network.

Result: Double-scoring substantially improves strong-ticket extraction over fixed-density edge-popup and pruning-at-initialization baselines, outperforms rewound sparse-training topologies, and exhibits markedly lower sensitivity to sparsity hyperparameters. Ablations show gains are tied to augmented score-space competition.

Conclusion: The proposed double-scoring method effectively addresses the layerwise sparsity bottleneck in strong lottery ticket extraction, demonstrating improved performance and robustness through score-space augmentation and competition mechanisms.

Abstract: The lottery ticket hypothesis proposes that large random neural networks contain sparse subnetworks that can match the performance of dense models after comparable training. A stronger version asserts that sufficiently overparameterized random networks contain subnetworks that are already accurate before any weight training. Existing theory establishes that such strong lottery tickets exist, but reliable extraction remains difficult. We revisit edge-popup, a frozen-weight score-training method for extracting strong tickets, and identify layerwise sparsity selection as a central bottleneck. We introduce double-scoring, an augmented score-space parameterization that replaces a layerwise sparsity search with optimization over enlarged score tensors. We prove that fixed-density masking in an augmented score space preserves access to all original-coordinate masks, and we show that the resulting method can be interpreted as edge-popup on a zero-augmented network. In controlled experiments, double-scoring substantially improves strong-ticket extraction over fixed-density edge-popup and pruning-at-initialization baselines, improves on the performance of rewound sparse-training topologies, and exhibits markedly lower sensitivity to sparsity hyperparameters. Ablations show that the gain is not merely due to additional trainable score parameters, but is tied to the augmented score-space competition that induces the effective original sparsity.

</details>


### [153] [Monkey King Bang: A Unified Scientific Multimodal Foundation Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20557)
*Hesen Chen,Xinyu Su,Xiaomeng Yang,Yuetan Lin,Zixiong Yang,Junyi An,Fenglei Cao,Yifeng Jiao,Yunqi Zhang,Yuan Cheng,Zhiyu Tan,Hao Li,Libo Wu,Yuan Qi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MKB is a unified multimodal model for scientific discovery that covers six scientific branches and supports both understanding and generation of native scientific outputs across different domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientific discovery is shifting from isolated disciplines to multi-domain reasoning, but existing AI systems are either too specialized or rely on text tokenization and prompt-based interfaces, limiting their ability to handle diverse scientific inputs, produce modality-native outputs, and support joint understanding, reasoning, and generation across scientific domains.

Method: Built around a shared Transformer backbone with modality-tailored encoders, adapters, and decoders, MKB covers six scientific branches (DNA, RNA, proteins, small molecules, earth science, medical images). Training uses a two-stage modality-then-language curriculum: Stage 1 aligns modality-specific components with frozen backbone; Stage 2 consolidates them with language backbone using mixed scientific and general corpora.

Result: MKB achieves competitive scientific understanding across biological and molecular benchmarks, produces high-fidelity native outputs for weather forecasting, biological generation, and medical-image segmentation, and largely retains the general capabilities of its Qwen3-VL backbone.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate that shared-backbone models with modality-tailored components provide a promising foundation for future cross-domain scientific multimodal exploration, showing the feasibility of this unified paradigm for scientific AI.
