Abstract: Scientific discovery is increasingly shifting from isolated disciplines to multi-domain reasoning, and AI for science faces a similar transition. Existing systems are either specialised for individual domains or unify scientific data mainly through text tokenisation and prompt-based interfaces, limiting their ability to handle diverse scientific inputs, produce modality-native outputs, and support joint understanding, reasoning, and generation across scientific domains. We introduce MKB, a unified scientific multimodal model for both understanding and generation, built around a shared Transformer backbone and modality-tailored encoders, adapters, and decoders. MKB covers six scientific branches, including DNA, RNA, proteins, small molecules, earth science, and medical images, and supports native outputs such as biological sequences, molecular strings, meteorological fields, and segmentation masks. Training follows a two-stage modality-then-language curriculum: Stage 1 aligns modality-specific components with the frozen backbone, and Stage 2 consolidates them with the language backbone using mixed scientific and general corpora. Experiments show that MKB achieves competitive scientific understanding across biological and molecular benchmarks, produces high-fidelity native outputs for weather forecasting, biological generation, and medical-image segmentation, and largely retains the general capabilities of its Qwen3-VL backbone. These results demonstrate the feasibility of the proposed paradigm, suggesting that shared-backbone models with modality-tailored components can provide a promising foundation for future cross-domain scientific multimodal exploration. The model and code are publicly available at https://github.com/Shanghai-Academy-of-AI-For-Science/MKB and https://huggingface.co/sais-org/MKB.

</details>


### [154] [StabilityBench: Benchmarking Instability in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20558)
*Emma Kondrup,Zachary Yang,Anne Imouza,Reihaneh Rabbany*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: StabilityBench is a benchmark operator that transforms single-turn queries into multi-turn conversations with simulated user interactions (demographic proxies or sycophantic baits) to evaluate AI assistants more realistically in conversational settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI assistants are increasingly used in high-stakes settings like healthcare, but current evaluation protocols are inadequate due to strong context dependence. Existing benchmarks are mostly static and single-turn, failing to capture real-world conversational variability and the effects of compounding safeguards in defense-in-depth approaches.

Method: StabilityBench is a principled, general, model-agnostic benchmark operator that transforms single-turn benchmark queries into multi-turn interaction histories by injecting realistic user simulations through demographic proxies or sycophantic baits while preserving original task intent. Also introduces StabilityBench-Mini, a size-preserving variant that samples across diversification axes.

Result: Applied to four benchmarks spanning mathematical reasoning, health QA, and safety, evaluating nine LLMs. Results show model performance is consistently unstable under these injections, with considerable performance degradations on three out of four benchmarks studied.

Conclusion: Current static evaluations have important limitations, motivating the need for more realistic evaluation settings. StabilityBench-Mini enables more realistic evaluation without increasing costs by sampling across diversification axes while preserving benchmark size.

Abstract: AI Assistants are increasingly deployed in high-stakes settings, such as healthcare or government services. Yet their real-world behavior remains poorly understood due to strong context dependence. Current evaluation protocols follow a defense-in-depth paradigm with compounding layers of safeguards, ranging from traditional benchmarks to live or adversarial testing. Such benchmarks remain largely static and single-turn, limiting their ability to capture real-world variability in conversational settings. We propose StabilityBench, a principled, general and model-agnostic benchmark operator that transforms single-turn benchmark queries into multi-turn interaction histories. StabilityBench augments existing benchmarks by injecting realistic user simulations, through demographic proxies or sycophantic baits, while preserving original task intent. We apply StabilityBench to four benchmarks spanning mathematical reasoning, health question-answering and safety, and evaluate nine large language models under these conditions. Our results show that model performance is consistently unstable under these injections, with considerable performance degradations on three out of four benchmarks studied. These highlight important limitations of static evaluations and motivate more realistic evaluation settings. To this end, we propose StabilityBench-Mini: a size-preserving variant of StabilityBench that samples across diversification axes, enabling more realistic evaluation without increasing costs.

</details>


### [155] [Joint Utilization of Geospatial and census proxies for Autoencoder-Assisted Downscaling (JUGAAD) of socioeconomic indicators in India](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20559)
*Aditya Dutt,Paul Gader,Aditya Singh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: JuGAAD: A deep learning framework that combines census and survey data to generate high-resolution poverty and food security predictions in India by addressing scale mismatches between different data sources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a critical need to monitor poverty and food security in developing nations, but existing methods face scale mismatches: census data provide geographic coverage but survey data (with socioeconomic indicators) are infrequently conducted at coarse resolutions, creating methodological challenges.

Method: Three-step deep learning framework: 1) Census and geospatial data are averaged into village-cluster-scale tessellations to reduce noise and handle administrative boundary changes; 2) An autoencoder compresses high-dimensional survey data into low-dimensional latent representation; 3) A regression model maps upscaled census/geospatial data to this latent representation, then applies this to fine-grained census data for predictions.

Result: The methodology successfully predicts socioeconomic indicators at fine scales with strong accuracy when validated against ground-truth district-level survey indicators from India's NSSO (2001 and 2011 data).

Conclusion: JuGAAD provides an effective solution to the scale mismatch problem between census and survey data, enabling high-resolution poverty and food security monitoring in developing countries through deep learning integration of disparate data sources.

Abstract: Monitoring poverty and food security indicators is imperative for addressing socioeconomic challenges in developing nations. A limitation is mismatches in scale between data sources: census data provide geographic coverage, while socioeconomic indicators are derived from infrequently conducted surveys at coarse resolutions, posing a methodological challenge. This study introduces a deep learning framework, JuGAAD, using Indian census and survey data from 2001 and 2011 as a case study. We employ a three-step process: census and geospatial data are averaged into intermediate village-cluster-scale tessellations to reduce noise and regularize administrative boundary changes; an autoencoder compresses high-dimensional National Sample Survey Office (NSSO) data into a low-dimensional latent representation; and a regression model maps upscaled census and geospatial data to this representation. This function is applied to fine-grained census data to generate high-resolution predictions, validated against ground-truth district-level NSSO indicators. Results confirm the methodology predicts socioeconomic indicators at fine scales with strong accuracy.

</details>


### [156] [Chronofy: A Temporal-Logical Decay Architecture for Information Validity in Time-Aware Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20560)
*Muntaser Syed,Marius Silaghi,Sheikh Abujar,Sharun Akter*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Chronofy: A neuro-symbolic framework that embeds temporal validity into RAG systems to prevent temporal hallucination by making fact age irremovable from representation and applying temporal decay functions during retrieval and reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RAG systems treat all retrieved facts as equally valid regardless of temporal provenance, leading to temporal hallucination where plausible but obsolete facts corrupt outputs. In domains like healthcare, temporal relevance is critical (e.g., yesterday's lab reading vs. six-month-old reading).

Method: Three-layer Temporal-Logical Decay Architecture (TLDA): Layer 1 - Dedicated temporal subspace in Matryoshka embeddings to make fact age structurally irremovable; Layer 2 - Learnable exponential decay functions in graph-based retrieval, with decay coefficient grounded in Bayesian decision theory; Layer 3 - Signal Temporal Logic robustness functions to evaluate temporal validity of retrieved knowledge, enforcing weakest-link principle to bound output confidence by most decayed evidence.

Result: Evaluation on temporal knowledge graph forecasting benchmarks, TimE temporal QA benchmark, and domain-specific sensitivity analysis shows that explicit temporal decay modeling improves retrieval precision, reduces temporal hallucination, and enables principled data re-acquisition triggers when temporal context is insufficient.

Conclusion: Chronofy successfully addresses temporal hallucination in RAG systems by embedding temporal validity directly into representation, retrieval, and reasoning layers, providing a principled framework for handling time-sensitive information in retrieval-augmented generation.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems retrieve and integrate external knowledge to ground large language model (LLM) outputs. However, current RAG architectures treat all retrieved facts as equally valid regardless of temporal provenance, leading to temporal hallucination, where plausible but obsolete facts corrupt the output. A clinical lab reading from yesterday is actionable; the same reading from six months ago is noise. We present Chronofy, a three-layer neuro-symbolic framework implementing the Temporal-Logical Decay Architecture (TLDA) that embeds temporal validity directly into the representation, retrieval, and reasoning layers of RAG systems. Layer 1 reserves a dedicated temporal subspace within Matryoshka embeddings to make fact age structurally irremovable from the representation. Layer 2 integrates learnable exponential decay functions into graph-based retrieval, where the decay coefficient $β_j$ is grounded in Bayesian decision theory as an approximation of twice the latent process mean-reversion rate. Layer 3 applies Signal Temporal Logic (STL) robustness functions to evaluate the temporal validity of retrieved knowledge, not LLM output confidence, and enforces the possibilistic weakest-link principle to bound output confidence by the most decayed evidence in the reasoning chain. We evaluate Chronofy on temporal knowledge graph forecasting benchmarks, the TimE temporal QA benchmark, and a domain-specific sensitivity analysis, demonstrating that explicit temporal decay modeling improves retrieval precision, reduces temporal hallucination, and enables principled data re-acquisition triggers when temporal context is insufficient.

</details>


### [157] [CT-Merging: Consensus Directions and Task-Level Scaling for LoRA Adapter Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20561)
*Keumseo Ryum,Joonhyuk Kang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CT-Merging is a novel LoRA adapter merging algorithm that constructs consensus directions from task subspace projectors and assigns task-level RMS coefficient scales, outperforming existing SVD-based merging methods on multi-task adaptation benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LoRA adapters efficiently specialize pretrained models for downstream tasks, deploying one adapter per task requires storage overhead and task selection at inference. Model merging addresses this by combining adapters, but existing SVD-based methods focus on direction construction while using original task SVD coefficients, which creates a mismatch between inherited coefficients and actual task update coefficients.

Method: CT-Merging is a LoRA-aware merging algorithm that: 1) Estimates consensus directions from average task subspace projectors, 2) Uses repeated support across task SVD subspaces to construct a common basis, 3) Assigns task-level RMS coefficient scales in the final update, 4) Reduces reliance on rank-wise SVD magnitudes after direction construction.

Result: On the DC-Merge CLIP adapter benchmark, CT-Merging achieves superior average normalized accuracy compared to state-of-the-art merging methods, improving over DC-Merge by 2.56 points on ViT-B/32 and 1.51 points on ViT-L/14 KnoTS-trained checkpoints.

Conclusion: CT-Merging effectively addresses the coefficient mismatch problem in LoRA adapter merging by constructing consensus directions and assigning appropriate coefficient scales, demonstrating significant improvements over existing merging approaches for multi-task adaptation.

Abstract: LoRA adapters provide an efficient way to specialize a pretrained model for many downstream tasks, but deploying one adapter per task requires adapter storage and task selection at inference time. Model merging addresses this issue by combining independently trained adapters into one multi-task adapter. Recent SVD-based LoRA merging methods mainly focus on constructing shared or task specific directions, while the coefficients assigned to the final directions are often directly from the original task SVD. On a fixed merged basis, inherited coefficients preserve component order with high rank correlation, yet their magnitudes differ substantially from the coefficients induced by the task updates. To address this mismatch, we propose CT-Merging, a LoRA-aware merging algorithm that estimates consensus directions from average task subspace projectors and assigns task-level RMS coefficient scales in the final update. CT-Merging uses repeated support across task SVD subspaces to construct the common basis, while reducing reliance on rank wise SVD magnitudes after direction construction. On the DC-Merge CLIP adapter benchmark, CT-Merging achieves superior average normalized accuracy compared to state-of-the-art merging methods and further improves over DC-Merge by 2.56 points on ViT-B/32 and 1.51 points on ViT-L/14 KnoTS-trained checkpoints.

</details>


### [158] [AI-Driven Surrogate Models for Predicting Electrode-Scale Discharge Behavior in Lithium-Ion Batteries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20577)
*Mengda Xing,Jean-Marie Lagniez,Alejandro Franco*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A deep learning surrogate using Swin3D Transformer with Gaussian Positional Encoding and Temporal Encoding module predicts lithium-ion battery discharge dynamics from volumetric data, achieving higher accuracy and orders-of-magnitude speedup over traditional simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Physics-based simulations for lithium-ion battery electrode-scale discharge behavior are computationally prohibitive, creating a need for faster, more efficient prediction methods to enable high-throughput battery design and optimization.

Method: Proposes a deep learning surrogate pipeline based on Swin3D Transformer that processes volumetric data, incorporating two key innovations: Gaussian Positional Encoding (GPE) to handle complex electrode microstructure geometry, and a specialized Temporal Encoding module to capture non-linear timeseries evolution.

Result: The method significantly outperforms state-of-the-art point cloud baselines in prediction accuracy on an Electrochemical Simulation dataset and reduces computational overhead by orders of magnitude compared to physics-based simulations.

Conclusion: The proposed pipeline provides a scalable and efficient framework for predicting spatiotemporal discharge dynamics, enabling practical high-throughput battery design and optimization that was previously limited by computational constraints.

Abstract: Physics-based simulations are essential for understanding the electrode-scale discharge behavior of lithium-ion batteries (LIBs) but suffer from prohibitive computational costs. To address this, we introduce a novel deep learning surrogate pipeline based on the Swin3D Transformer to predict spatiotemporal discharge dynamics directly from volumetric data. Our approach integrates two key innovations: Gaussian Positional Encoding (GPE), which enhances spatial feature representation by adapting to the complex geometry of electrode microstructures, and a specialized Temporal Encoding module to capture non-linear timeseries evolution. Experimental validation on an Electrochemical Simulation (ES) dataset demonstrates that our pipeline significantly outperforms state-of-the-art point cloud baselines in prediction accuracy. Furthermore, the proposed method reduces the computational overhead by orders of magnitude, providing a scalable and efficient framework for high-throughput battery design and optimization.

</details>


### [159] [Fisher Widths: Local Learning Geometry and Anisotropic Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20578)
*Vu Khac Ky*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces and analyzes two complementary geometric widths on statistical manifolds: Fisher width and inverse-Fisher width, which capture learning complexity and recovery complexity respectively under Fisher information geometry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to understand geometric complexity measures on statistical manifolds through the lens of Fisher information. The authors want to characterize how Fisher geometry affects both learning (parameter estimation) and recovery (sparse reconstruction) problems, where traditional Euclidean approaches may not capture the intrinsic statistical structure.

Method: The method involves defining two geometric functionals: primal Fisher width w_G(T) = w(G^{1/2}T) and inverse-Fisher width w_{G^{-1}}(T) = w(G^{-1/2}T), where G is the Fisher information matrix. These widths are studied for statistical learning (on Fisher balls) and sparse recovery (with anisotropic Gaussian measurements), including support-sensitive analysis.

Result: Key results include: 1) For Fisher-regular losses, Fisher width determines the scale of local parameter fluctuations as w_G(H_r)/√n; 2) For sparse recovery, inverse-Fisher width reveals geometry depends on both sparsity and position of active coordinates in Fisher spectrum; 3) A fundamental inequality w_G(T)w_{G^{-1}}(T) ≥ w(T)^2 showing Fisher anisotropy transfers complexity but cannot reduce both widths relative to Euclidean scale.

Conclusion: The Fisher and inverse-Fisher widths provide complementary perspectives on statistical complexity - the former for learning, the latter for recovery. Fisher anisotropy creates interesting trade-offs between these geometries, but cannot simultaneously reduce complexity in both directions compared to Euclidean geometry. This framework connects geometric complexity measures with statistical performance in estimation and recovery problems.

Abstract: We study Gaussian-width complexity on statistical manifolds through a pair of functionals: the primal Fisher width $w_G(T) = w(G^{1/2}T)$, induced by the Fisher metric, and the inverse-Fisher width $w_{G^{-1}}(T) = w(G^{-1/2}T)$, induced by the inverse Fisher metric. The two widths play complementary statistical roles.
  On the learning side, the Fisher width measures the size of local parameter fluctuations in the geometry induced by the Fisher information. For Fisher-regular losses, we prove that the scale \(w_G(H_r)/\sqrt n\) is attained on sufficiently small Fisher balls.
  On the recovery side, the inverse-Fisher width captures the effect of anisotropic Gaussian measurements whose covariance is determined by the inverse Fisher information. For sparse recovery, the resulting geometry depends not only on sparsity but also on the position of the active coordinates in the Fisher spectrum. We obtain a two-sided estimate for the corresponding statistical dimension, together with support-sensitive recovery estimates and a natural ordering of supports with different curvature profiles.
  Finally, we establish a sharp relation between the primal and inverse-Fisher widths. On any common compact coordinate set $T$, they satisfy \[ w_G(T)w_{G^{-1}}(T)\geq w(T)^2. \] Thus, Fisher anisotropy may transfer complexity from one geometry to the other, but cannot reduce both widths relative to the Euclidean scale.

</details>


### [160] [Exact ReLU realization of affine one-dimensional refinement iterates via residual memory and offset frames](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20586)
*Boldsaikhan Bolorkhuu,Tsogtgerel Gantumur*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proves that vector-valued affine refinement operators can be exactly realized by fixed-width ReLU networks with depth O(n), using a residual memory controller and offset frames for exact backward replay.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish exact fixed-width ReLU realizations for affine refinement operators with finite affine iterates, extending homogeneous realization theory to include affine forcing terms.

Method: Uses a residual memory controller to replace noninvertible residual dynamics with injective skew-product, enabling exact backward replay of residual states. Offset frames align forcing atoms away from residual seams, and complementary loop readouts recover values exactly. Based on Horner-type evaluation of affine forcing sums.

Result: For M ≥ 3, every finite affine iterate Wⁿγ admits an exact fixed-width ReLU realization with depth O(n), applicable to arbitrary compactly supported continuous piecewise linear forcing terms. For M=2, applies to ordinary-frame seam-separated forcing. Also provides stage-dependent extension for finite-dimensional forcing terms.

Conclusion: Affine refinement operators can be exactly realized by shallow ReLU networks, with applications to various recursive constructions including open-curve, finite-state, Hilbert- and Morton-type patterns, demonstrating linear-depth realizations.

Abstract: We study vector-valued affine refinement operators of the form [
(Wγ)(t)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}} A_jγ(Mt-j)+B(t), ]
with finitely supported matrix mask and compactly supported continuous piecewise linear input and forcing data. Building on the homogeneous realization theorem for (B\equiv 0), we prove that, for (M\ge 3), every finite affine iterate (W^nγ) admits an exact fixed-width ReLU realization whose depth is (O(n)).
  The main new ingredient is a residual memory controller. It replaces the noninvertible residual dynamics by an injective skew-product and permits exact backward replay of the residual states required by a Horner-type evaluation of the affine forcing sum. Offset frames align the forcing atoms away from residual seams, allowing complementary loop readouts to recover their values exactly. The remaining branch-selection ambiguity occurs only where the accumulated affine state has already vanished.
  For (M\ge 3), the result applies to arbitrary compactly supported continuous piecewise linear forcing terms. For (M=2), the same construction applies to ordinary-frame seam-separated forcing. We also prove a stage-dependent extension for forcing terms in a fixed finite-dimensional continuous piecewise linear span and record the resulting linear-depth upgrade for open-curve, finite-state, and Hilbert- and Morton-type recursive constructions.

</details>


### [161] [Detecting Neural Network Failures through Spectral Analysis of Internal Activations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20590)
*Arunan J*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Neural networks exhibit spectral instability in internal activations when misclassifying. The paper introduces Self-Detecting Neural Networks (SDNN) that monitor spectral dynamics to detect failures, achieving ~79% AUROC vs ~51% for confidence-based methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural network misclassifications show characteristic spectral instability in internal activations that is invisible at the output layer. This internal spectral signature is masked in final outputs, explaining why confidence-based detection methods struggle to identify failures effectively.

Method: Introduces Self-Detecting Neural Networks (SDNN) that monitor spectral dynamics across network depth using Short-Time Fourier Transform, wavelet decomposition, and statistical moments to capture multi-scale spectral features. A lightweight detector network (5% parameter overhead) learns via curriculum learning on progressively challenging distributions including natural misclassifications, distribution shifts, and adversarial perturbations.

Result: On CIFAR-10, SDNN achieves 79.0 +/- 25.3% AUROC across three seeds, substantially outperforming confidence-based baselines including MaxSoftmax (50.5%) and Energy Score (52.9%) by approximately 25-30 percentage points. Failures exhibit significantly higher spectral drift than correct predictions (1.9% increase, p<0.001).

Conclusion: Spectral analysis of internal activations is a promising direction for neural network reliability, revealing diagnostic information inaccessible to output-based approaches. The work establishes spectral drift as a characteristic signature of misclassifications and demonstrates SDNN's effectiveness in failure detection.

Abstract: Neural network misclassifications exhibit characteristic spectral instability in internal activations that is invisible at the output layer. This phenomenon is identified and formalized as Spectral Drift -- the frequency-domain distance between consecutive layer activations -- with empirical validation showing that failures exhibit significantly higher drift than correct predictions (1.9% increase, p<0.001). This spectral signature emerges during internal processing but becomes masked in final outputs, explaining why confidence-based detection methods struggle.
  This work introduces Self-Detecting Neural Networks (SDNN), a framework that monitors spectral dynamics across network depth using Short-Time Fourier Transform, wavelet decomposition, and statistical moments to capture multi-scale spectral features. A lightweight detector network (5% parameter overhead) learns to identify failure-indicative patterns via curriculum learning on progressively challenging distributions: natural misclassifications, distribution shifts, and adversarial perturbations.
  Experiments on CIFAR-10 demonstrate that SDNN achieves 79.0 +/- 25.3% AUROC across three seeds, substantially outperforming confidence-based baselines including MaxSoftmax (50.5%) and Energy Score (52.9%) by approximately 25-30 percentage points. Ablation studies reveal that wavelet decomposition and statistical features make consistent contributions, while STFT's role remains unclear. This work establishes spectral analysis of internal activations as a promising direction for neural network reliability, revealing diagnostic information inaccessible to output-based approaches.

</details>


### [162] [STeMP: Spatio-Temporal Modelling Protocol](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20592)
*Jan Linnenbrink,Jakub Nowosad,Marvin Ludwig,Anna Frederike Jablotschkin,Fabian Schumacher,Teja Kattenborn,Hanna Meyer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: STeMP is a standardized reporting protocol for spatio-temporal machine learning models that provides both guidance during modeling and ensures transparent reporting of critical decisions and parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a lack of standardized protocols for spatio-temporal machine learning modeling in environmental research, despite the high sensitivity of these models to training data characteristics and methodological choices. Current reporting practices lack transparency, making it difficult to establish trust, transparency, and comparability between models.

Method: Developed the STeMP protocol structured in three sections: Overview (metadata), Model (describing predictors, evaluation, software), and Prediction. Created an R-package with a web application to fill the protocol either manually or semi-automatically from modeling objects. Includes warning system for common pitfalls and encourages community contributions via GitHub.

Result: Successfully created STeMP protocol and supporting tools including an R-package with web application available on GitHub (https://github.com/LOEK-RS/STeMP). The protocol enables standardized reporting and provides guidance through the modeling process with warnings for common pitfalls.

Conclusion: STeMP fills a critical gap in spatio-temporal machine learning modeling by providing a standardized reporting protocol that enhances transparency, trust, and comparability of models. The open-source implementation and community engagement via GitHub ensure ongoing development and adoption.

Abstract: Spatio-temporal machine-learning modelling is an important tool in environmental research. However, machine-learning models are highly sensitive to both the characteristics of the training data, such as its distribution, and methodological choices, including the cross-validation strategy. Each decision has impact and implications on the model itself as well as the estimation of the model quality and applicability for certain purposes. Taking into account the large role of machine-learning based maps of the environment in science and their transfer into practice, transparent reporting of spatio-temporal models, ideally using standardized model protocols, is essential to enable trust, transparency and comparability. However, such protocols are currently lacking for spatio-temporal modelling.
  We propose STeMP (Spatio-Temporal Modelling Protocol) to fill this gap by serving two purposes: standardized reporting to understand the model functioning as well as providing guidance during the modelling process by pointing at critical decisions and parameters. The protocol is structured in three sections: Overview, Model and Prediction. The Overview section contains metadata, while the Model and Prediction sections go into detail, describing predictors, evaluation and software, and further relevant elements of the modelling workflow.
  The protocol definition is hosted on GitHub and accompanied by an R-package (https://github.com/LOEK-RS/STeMP). The R-package contains a web application that can be used to fill the protocol either manually or in a semi-automated way from provided modelling objects. Warnings are returned from the protocol when common pitfalls are encountered, which may help authors as a guide through the modelling process but also support reviewers in the assessment of modelling studies. Via GitHub, incorporation of contributions and feedback from the community is encouraged.

</details>


### [163] [When Does Recurrence Become an Algorithm? Convergence Selection in Weight-Tied Looped Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20594)
*Tong Zhang,Junhao Hu,Yun Peng,Tao Xie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Weight-tied looped transformers learn serial computational frontiers with speed determined by training budget, where architectural prior (not expressivity) selects algorithms, and mechanisms are portable across budget contracts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand when weight-tied looped transformers implement actual algorithms rather than just memorizing patterns, and to identify the factors that determine algorithmic learning in transformers.

Method: Controlled population studies on group word problems using weight-tied looped transformers (one block applied T times). Introduces head instrument convergence-time scaling tau(n,i) and validates via damage cones. Compares standard-depth transformers with weight-tied models on parallel vs serial algorithms.

Result: Four key findings: (1) Budget law showing linear computation frontier with speed v ~ n_train/T_train; (2) Architecture prior selects algorithm (tying flips to serial frontier); (3) Walls determined by group order not circuit complexity; (4) Mechanisms portable across budget contracts with speed re-pricing. Results replicate on easy-to-hard benchmark.

Conclusion: Weight-tied transformers learn genuine serial algorithms whose speed is determined by training budget, with architectural prior (not expressivity) driving algorithmic selection. Standard evaluation metrics are inadequate; convergence-time scaling provides better predictive power for out-of-distribution performance.

Abstract: When does a weight-tied looped transformer -- one block applied T times -- implement an actual algorithm? We answer with four findings from controlled populations on group word problems. (1) The budget law: free training installs a linear computation frontier, a mechanism that solves v positions per loop, whose speed is priced by the training contract: v ~ n_train/T_train (exponent 0.98 +/- 0.04, R^2=0.99), exactly unity under T=n training. SGD selects a frontier matching the minimum the contract demands; granting more test-time loops than ever trained rescues late positions at fixed input length, yielding a principled halting rule T* = ceil(n / v-hat). (2) Architecture prior, not expressivity, picks the algorithm: standard-depth transformers learn parallel scans on this family; weight tying flips the selection to the serial frontier, even when positional addressing for a log-depth scan is supplied. At matched depth and parameters, untied models extrapolate worst and fail to learn A5 at all. (3) The walls are not where circuit complexity says: NC1-completeness costs nothing (A5 generalizes fully), while group order does (S5's 120x120 operator deadlocks joint learning) -- and an operator-first curriculum dissolves the wall in every seed. (4) Mechanisms are portable, not mandatable: warm-starting across budget contracts transfers the algorithm in every seed, re-pricing its speed, while imposing seriality through the input schedule fails where free training succeeds. These results are invisible to standard instruments, which provably saturate at the fixed points trained loops converge to. We introduce a head instrument, the convergence-time scaling tau(n,i), validate it causally via damage cones whose slope reproduces v, and show in-distribution head measurements predict out-of-distribution fate where tail metrics do not. Results replicate on the public easy-to-hard benchmark.

</details>


### [164] [Are Single-Token Sparse Autoencoder Features Causally Necessary? Layer-Depth and SAE-Family Effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20596)
*Seonglae Cho,Zekun Wu,Kleyton Da Costa,Rishi Kalra,Ilham Wicaksono,Adriano Koshiyama*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Single-token sparse autoencoder (SAE) features show stable causal roles within SAE families but cross-family differences exist; training methodology rather than activation function or scale drives interpretability variations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to test whether SAE features maintain consistent causal roles across different SAE families, using single-token features (which activate on one vocabulary item) as a diagnostic case where ground truth comparison is possible.

Method: Analyzed 3.9M features across six models and three SAE families using zero-ablation at full layer depth. Examined single-token feature clustering in decoder space, layer distribution patterns, and conducted controlled ablation experiments with Benjamini-Hochberg correction.

Result: Single-token features cluster 4.7x tighter in decoder space and concentrate in early layers. Ablation causes significant logit reductions in 178 of 208 conditions. Cross-family causal differences exceed within-family scale effects: GemmaScope and BatchTopK features remain causally anchored while LlamaScope features show local redundancy. Target token rank recovers to within 2x baseline 96-98% of time after same ablation.

Conclusion: Cross-family interpretability claims are sensitive to training methodology, not just activation function or scale. The study reveals that SAE feature causal stability varies across families, emphasizing the importance of training recipe in SAE interpretability.

Abstract: Sparse autoencoder (SAE) features are used to interpret and steer large language models, yet whether a feature's causal role is stable across SAE families remains untested. Single-token features that activate on one vocabulary item provide the diagnostic case where ground truth permits direct comparison. We analyze 3.9M features across six models and three SAE families using zero-ablation at full layer depth. Single-token features cluster 4.7x tighter in decoder space and concentrate in early layers (Layer 0 in GPT2-Small; L0-L4 in Gemma). Ablating them yields Benjamini-Hochberg-significant logit reductions in 178 of 208 full-layer conditions, with depth controlling whether damage cascades downstream or shapes the output directly. Cross-family causal differences exceed within-family scale effects: on the same base model, GemmaScope and BatchTopK features remain causally anchored, while LlamaScope features are locally redundant. The target token's rank recovers to within 2x baseline 96-98% of the time after the same ablation, and a controlled activation-function comparison reverses sign within the same model, leaving training recipe as the residual candidate. Cross-family interpretability claims are therefore sensitive to training methodology, not just activation function or scale.

</details>


### [165] [One Round Is All You Need: Analytic Federated Learning for Task-Heterogeneous Multi-Label Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20641)
*Afsaneh Mahanipour,Hana Khamfroush*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Analytic federated learning framework for multi-label medical image classification that handles task heterogeneity (missing class labels across clients) with closed-form operations, requiring only 1-2 communication rounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In clinical federated learning, each institution typically annotates only pathologies within its expertise, creating task heterogeneity where clients hold labels for strict subsets of target disease categories. Existing gradient-based FL methods fail because they need many communication rounds to converge and missing class labels introduce systematic false-negative bias that cannot be corrected.

Method: Proposes analytic federated learning framework with three closed-form operations: 1) balanced label projection that neutralizes class-imbalance bias by normalizing positive/negative contributions, 2) per-class absolute aggregation law that assembles optimal ridge-regression classifier for each disease category from sufficient statistics, and 3) optional analytic pseudo-label refinement round that propagates missing-class knowledge via confidence-filtered teacher classifier.

Result: Experiments on ChestXray14 under four progressively severe missing-class configurations show the method consistently outperforms state-of-the-art FedMLP by up to 18.44 BACC points and 13.24 AUC points while reducing communication costs.

Conclusion: The proposed analytic framework effectively addresses task heterogeneity in federated medical image classification, achieving superior performance with only 1-2 communication rounds regardless of task heterogeneity severity or number of clients, making it practical for real-world clinical applications.

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) enables multiple clinical institutions to collaboratively train a shared disease classifier without centralizing patient data. In practice, however, each institution annotates only the pathologies within its area of expertise, so the federation operates under task heterogeneity: each client holds labels for a strict subset of the target disease categories while the remaining classes are entirely unobserved at that site. Existing gradient-based FL methods fail under this setting because they require hundreds of communication rounds to converge and because missing class labels introduce systematic false-negative bias that the model cannot correct without a principled mechanism. We propose an analytic federated learning framework for multi-label medical image classification under task heterogeneity. The proposed method replaces iterative gradient optimization with three closed-form operations: a balanced label projection that neutralizes class-imbalance bias by normalizing positive and negative contributions to equal total mass; a per-class absolute aggregation law that independently assembles the optimal ridge-regression classifier for each disease category from the sufficient statistics uploaded by its annotating clients; and an optional analytic pseudo-label refinement round that propagates missing-class knowledge from a confidence-filtered teacher classifier to non-annotating clients. The entire procedure requires at most two communication rounds, irrespective of the degree of task heterogeneity or the number of participating clients. Experiments on ChestXray14 under four progressively severe missing-class configurations demonstrate that the proposed method consistently outperforms the state-of-the-art federated multi-label method FedMLP by up to 18.44 BACC points and 13.24 AUC points, while reducing the communication.

</details>


### [166] [Scaling Interpretable Transformers with Parity Bottleneck Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20652)
*Andrew Mack,Kraig Yuheng Tou,Mark Henry,Zhengxun Wu,Lauren Greenspan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The ParityTransformer is a GPT-2-scale architecture with Deep Parity Bottleneck layers that provide interpretable, sparse representations by design, eliminating memory issues of traditional sparse autoencoders while maintaining performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) can recover features from language models post-hoc, but training models with interpretable bottlenecks by construction has been impractical due to prohibitive memory and compute costs of per-layer over-complete bottlenecks.

Method: Introduces ParityTransformer with Deep Parity Bottleneck (DPB) layers that replace learned over-complete bases with parameter-free algebraic dictionaries, providing deterministic incoherence guarantees. Uses a hierarchically structured sparse bottleneck with multi-level mixture-of-experts for hardware-aware sparsity enforcement.

Result: ParityTransformers perform at least as well as post-hoc SAEs on sparse probing tasks, outperform on feature absorption, steering effectiveness, and fine-grained causal interventions. Features are native to the forward pass by construction, addressing whether SAEs probe features the model actually uses.

Conclusion: This work represents a step toward training models whose internal representations are interpretable by design rather than recovered post-hoc, addressing fundamental questions about feature use during computation.

Abstract: Language models are thought to exhibit the phenomenon of superposition, representing many more features than dimensions in their residual streams. Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are designed to recover such features post-hoc, but training models that are interpretable by construction has remained impractical, as a per-layer over-complete bottleneck is prohibitively expensive in both memory and compute. To overcome this issue, we introduce the ParityTransformer, a GPT-2-scale architecture whose intermediate representations are efficient and wide / sparse by design. At each layer, a Deep Parity Bottleneck (DPB) replaces a learned over-complete basis with a parameter-free algebraic dictionary, providing a deterministic incoherence guarantee and eliminating the memory requirements that have prevented per-layer interpretable bottlenecks at scale. A DPB is a hierarchically structured sparse bottleneck which efficiently enforces sparsity using a multi-level mixture-of-experts approach: a hardware-aware implementation that closes the cost gap between activation sparse and dense training to a manageable interpretability tax. Empirically, ParityTransformers perform at least as well as post-hoc SAEs on sparse probing tasks, while out-performing on measures of feature absorption, steering effectiveness, and fine-grained causal interventions. Because subsequent computation acts only on features that survive the sparse bottleneck, the ParityTransformer's features are native to the model's forwards pass by construction, addressing the question of whether SAEs probe features the model actually uses during computation. We see this as a step toward training models whose internal representations are interpretable by design rather than recovered post hoc.

</details>


### [167] [SalesLoop: Reinforcement Learning from Performance Feedback for Sales Lead Ranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20655)
*Chenyu Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SalesLoop is a reinforcement learning framework that addresses offline-online performance gaps in CRM lead ranking by creating a closed feedback loop between model predictions and real-world business outcomes, achieving significant production improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional CRM lead ranking models suffer from a persistent challenge where high offline accuracy doesn't translate to production performance due to three fundamental gaps: offline-online metric mismatch, pointwise-listwise objective misalignment, and temporal distribution drift.

Method: SalesLoop uses reinforcement learning with a closed feedback loop between predictions and business outcomes. It introduces: (1) a performance-aware reward encoding conversion outcomes weighted by ranking position and conversion velocity, and (2) Discriminative GRPO - a listwise optimization objective adapting Group Relative Policy Optimization to discriminative ranking models.

Result: SalesLoop improves NDCG@K by +7.9% and P@K by +15.8% over best static baseline. Production A/B test (160 days, 16.5M leads, 280 sales specialists) shows statistically significant cumulative lift of +4.7% (p=0.047) and +8.7% (p=0.002). In production, achieves 44.1% Top-10% recall and surfaces high-intent leads at 2.3× the conversion rate of specialist baselines.

Conclusion: The SalesLoop framework successfully addresses the offline-online performance gap in CRM lead ranking by establishing a closed feedback loop with business outcomes, demonstrating practical value through significant production improvements and validated real-world performance.

Abstract: Lead ranking in Customer Relationship Management (CRM) systems faces a persistent challenge: models achieving high offline accuracy often underperform in production. We identify three fundamental gaps responsible for this disconnect: offline-online metric mismatch, pointwise-listwise objective misalignment, and temporal distribution drift. To address these gaps, we propose SalesLoop, a reinforcement learning framework that establishes a closed feedback loop between model predictions and real-world business outcomes. Our approach introduces (1) a performance-aware reward that encodes conversion outcomes weighted by ranking position and conversion velocity, and (2) Discriminative GRPO, a listwise optimization objective that adapts Group Relative Policy Optimization to discriminative ranking models.
  SalesLoop improves NDCG@K by +7.9\% and P@K by +15.8\% over the strongest static baseline. A 160-day production A/B test at a New Energy Vehicle manufacturer, spanning 16.5M leads and 280 sales specialists across two provincial markets, validates statistically significant cumulative lift of +4.7\% ($p=0.047$) and +8.7\% ($p=0.002$). In production, the ranking backbone achieves Top-10\% recall of 44.1\% and surfaces high-intent leads at $2.3\times$ the conversion rate of specialist baselines.

</details>


### [168] [Adaptive Multi-Horizon Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20656)
*Manoosh Samiei,Doina Precup,Paul Masset*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes adaptive multi-horizon approach for RL that dynamically selects temporal discounting horizons instead of using fixed discount factor, improving adaptability in changing environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biological agents exhibit flexible temporal discounting while RL typically uses fixed discount factor with single exponential horizon. Need for adaptive temporal horizons to handle complex, changing environments without manual tuning.

Method: Multi-horizon approach that adaptively selects and combines temporal horizons, enabling automatic adaptation to changes in reward structure without manual discount-factor tuning.

Result: Demonstrates effective discount factor identification across MiniGrid environments, including continual settings with three sequentially changing tasks. Shows improved parameter efficiency and adaptability.

Conclusion: Adaptive temporal discounting can enhance both artificial and biologically inspired learning systems, particularly for continual learning scenarios with task switches and varying environmental configurations.

Abstract: Effective decision-making in complex and changing environments requires balancing short-term and long-term consequences. In reinforcement learning (RL), this trade-off is typically controlled through a fixed discount factor, which imposes a single exponentially discounted temporal horizon. However, biological agents exhibit flexible and adaptive temporal discounting, suggesting that effective planning requires multiple timescales. Here, we propose a multi-horizon approach that adaptively selects and combines temporal horizons, enabling robust adaptation to changes in reward structure without manual discount-factor tuning. This flexibility makes the method particularly suitable for continual learning scenarios involving task switches and varying environmental configurations. Empirically, we demonstrate that our approach identifies effective discount factors across a range of MiniGrid environments, including continual settings composed of three sequentially changing tasks. These results suggest that adaptive temporal discounting can improve parameter efficiency and enhance adaptability in both artificial and biologically inspired learning systems.

</details>


### [169] [End-to-End Learning of Safe Optimal Feedback Control in High Dimensions with Control Barrier Function Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20674)
*Xingjian Li,Kelvin Kan,Deepanshu Verma,Krishna Kumar,Stanley Osher,Samy Wu Fung*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Scalable end-to-end training of high-dimensional safety-critical controllers using operator splitting and Jacobian-Free Backpropagation to overcome computational bottlenecks of CBF-based safety filters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous methods for learning controllers with Control Barrier Function safety constraints face computational and differentiation bottlenecks, limiting applications to low-dimensional systems (≤16 dimensions). Need for scalable solutions for high-dimensional safety-critical systems.

Method: Combines operator splitting with Jacobian-Free Backpropagation (JFB) to enable scalable end-to-end training while maintaining hard safety guarantees through CBF safety filters. Uses nonsmooth analysis techniques for theoretical justification.

Result: Successfully demonstrates effectiveness on high-dimensional multi-agent nonlinear control problems with state dimensions up to 1200 and control dimensions up to 400, significantly surpassing previous dimensional limitations.

Conclusion: The proposed methodology enables scalable learning of safety-critical controllers for high-dimensional systems while preserving hard safety guarantees, bridging the gap between theory and practical high-dimensional applications.

Abstract: We consider the problem of learning high-dimensional semi-global feedback controllers under hard safety constraints enforced by control barrier functions (CBFs). Incorporating CBFs into end-to-end policy training requires embedding a quadratic-program-based safety filter as an optimization layer, but computational and differentiation bottlenecks have largely restricted prior approaches to low-dimensional systems, typically with at most 16 state dimensions. We address this limitation by combining operator splitting with the recently developed Jacobian-Free Backpropagation (JFB) method to enable scalable end-to-end training while preserving hard safety guarantees through the CBF safety filter. We justify this training methodology theoretically using nonsmooth analysis techniques and demonstrate its effectiveness on high-dimensional multi-agent nonlinear control problems with state and control dimensions up to 1200 and 400, respectively.

</details>


### [170] [Explanation-Based Runtime Verification for Trustworthy ML-driven Optical Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20675)
*Omran Ayoub,Carlos Natalino,Ali Al Housseini,Felix Foschum,Philipp Morger,Tiziano Leidi,David Hock,Paolo Monti*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Explanation-based runtime verification uses model explanations to assess ML decision soundness before execution in optical networks, intercepting erroneous decisions while maintaining automation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ML models in optical network automation directly impact service quality and network stability through control-plane actions, but incorrect decisions can have immediate negative consequences. As automation increases, ensuring reliability of individual decisions at deployment time becomes critical.

Method: Proposes explanation-based runtime verification that exploits model explanations to assess soundness of individual ML decisions before execution. The approach evaluates explanation coherence and physics grounding consistency at runtime, enabling deferral or rejection of uncertain decisions.

Result: Experimental results on lightpath quality of transmission classification show that explanation-based verification can intercept a significant fraction of erroneous decisions while preserving high automation rate.

Conclusion: Explanation-based runtime verification is an effective approach for improving reliability in ML-driven optical network automation by leveraging explainable AI techniques to assess decision soundness before execution, balancing safety with automation efficiency.

Abstract: Machine learning (ML) models are increasingly integrated into optical network automation frameworks to support tasks such as failure management, performance monitoring and resource allocation. In these environments, ML-driven predictions may be directly coupled with control-plane actions where incorrect decisions can immediately impact service quality, resource efficiency, and network stability. As automation levels increase, ensuring the reliability of individual decisions at deployment time becomes a critical requirement. Explainable artificial intelligence (XAI) techniques have emerged to improve transparency by highlighting the factors influencing ML predictions. In addition to identifying influential features, they provide insights into the underlying reasoning process of the model, revealing how different input variables contribute to the final outcome and how feature interactions shape the decision boundary. In this work, we introduce explanation-based runtime verification, an approach that exploits model explanations to assess the soundness of individual ML decisions before they are executed in the network control loop. The proposed approach evaluates explanation coherence and physics grounding consistency at runtime, enabling the system to defer or reject decisions flagged as uncertain. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach on a representative use case of lightpath quality of transmission classification. Experimental results show that explanation-based verification can intercept a significant fraction of erroneous decisions while preserving high automation rate.

</details>


### [171] [Attribution Markets: A Fisher-Market Formulation for Fractional Credit Assignment Between Planned Tasks and Performed Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20694)
*Salavat Ishbulatov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel quasi-linear Fisher market formulation bridges planning systems with a unified framework that handles genuinely related but unlinked effort using text/structural/temporal signals, with entropy regularization to handle noise sensitivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing personal/organizational planning systems suffer from drifting records between planned tasks and logged actions, using exclusive all-or-nothing links that strand related effort and cause false stall reports on active goals.

Method: Formulate the planning-action bridge as a quasi-linear Fisher market where planned tasks are budget-constrained buyers, performed actions are divisible goods, and a fused text/structural/temporal signal sets buyer valuations. Extended with concave completion utility, satiation-threshold fixed points, and entropy-regularized generalization for noise robustness.

Result: The market framework yields conservation properties, hard budget caps, and provable junk filtering. While initial sharp equilibrium is noise-sensitive, entropy regularization unified with optimal transport provides noise-adaptive robustness, validated empirically on random/adversarial instances.

Conclusion: The approach provides a principled solution to planning-action drift with theoretical guarantees and practical noise robustness, relating to multi-touch attribution, optimal transport, and online Fisher-market algorithms.

Abstract: Personal and organizational planning systems maintain two records that drift apart: what was planned (a task's effort budget) and what was done (a logged action's duration and description). Existing systems bridge them with an exclusive, all-or-nothing link that strands genuinely related but unlinked effort and reports false stalls on active goals. We formulate the bridge as a quasi-linear Fisher market: planned tasks are budget-constrained buyers, performed actions are divisible goods, and a fused text/structural/temporal signal sets each buyer's valuation. Two market instruments - a seller reserve price and a buyer cash option - yield conservation, a hard budget cap, and a provable junk filter as theorems. We extend the market with a concave completion utility discounting progress as a task nears its plan; standard convergence theory for the market's algorithm does not transfer here, resolved by a satiation-threshold fixed point with existence (Brouwer) and local uniqueness under an explicit diagonal-dominance condition, validated empirically on random and adversarial instances. A de-circularized, multi-seed benchmark - observed affinity corrupted independently of the scored ground truth - surfaces a genuine weak spot: the market's sharp, zero-entropy equilibrium is more sensitive to affinity noise than entropy-regularized optimal transport's permanently smoothed one. We resolve this with a one-parameter entropy-regularized generalization unifying the two, plus a noise-adaptive rule for its regularization strength. We report full reproducibility parameters, discuss limitations candidly, and relate the result to multi-touch attribution, optimal transport, and online Fisher-market algorithms.

</details>


### [172] [CEDAR: Causal Edge Discovery for Autoregressive Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20696)
*Mohammad Fesanghary*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CEDAR is a constraint-based method for discovering lagged causal edges in sparse autoregressive time series, using AR(1)-residualized distance correlation for screening, targeted conditional independence tests, and MCI pruning for efficient, interpretable causal discovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the challenge of lagged causal edge discovery in sparse autoregressive time series, particularly when data is scarce and variables exhibit simple lag-1 self-dynamics. Current methods may be inefficient or lose interpretability when dealing with sparse regimes.

Method: CEDAR uses AR(1)-residualized, U-centered distance correlation to screen candidate cross-variable lags, then applies two targeted conditional-independence tests per significant lag candidate (accepting at most one lag per ordered pair). It includes a stable MCI pruning step to remove indirect edges and optional deterministic C-nodes to adjust for specified trend-like nonstationarity.

Result: In sparse regimes where few lags survive screening, CEDAR requires O(d²) conditional independence tests after screening while maintaining edge-level interpretability. It is computationally efficient compared to methods with richer conditioning sets.

Conclusion: CEDAR is most effective for scarce data with lag-1 self-dynamics, but methods with richer conditioning sets become preferable as time series length grows or when higher-order autoregressive or simultaneous multi-lag effects are common.

Abstract: We propose CEDAR (Causal Edge Discovery for Autoregressive Processes), a constraint-based method for lagged causal edge discovery in sparse autoregressive time series. CEDAR screens candidate cross-variable lags using AR(1)-residualized, U-centered distance correlation, then applies two targeted conditional-independence tests per significant cross-variable lag candidate and accepts at most one lag per ordered pair. A stable MCI pruning step removes indirect edges, and optional deterministic C-nodes adjust for specified trend-like nonstationarity. In sparse regimes where few lags survive screening, CEDAR requires $O(d^2)$ CI tests after screening while retaining edge-level interpretability. CEDAR is most effective when data are scarce and variables exhibit lag-1 self-dynamics; methods with richer conditioning sets become preferable as $T$ grows or when higher-order autoregressive or simultaneous multi-lag effects are common.

</details>


### [173] [Perspective Latents as an Architectural Condition for Causal Emergence in Active Inference Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20708)
*Hongju Pae*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Active inference agents show that integrated information (Φₓ) is architecture-dependent and decreases with training, not tracking reward improvement. The learning effect is visible only at the compositional level of information flow patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous work found that integrated information (Φₓ) in reinforcement learning agents grows with training and tracks reward improvement. This raises the question of how reward-free predictive organization in active inference agents relates to these information-theoretic signatures.

Method: Tested within an active inference agent with architecture separating fast perception latent (z) from slow global latent (g), where g is driven by prediction error and structurally decoupled from policy gradients. Used reward-free environmental regime-switching protocol.

Result: Φₓ concentrates in g, but its aggregate magnitude is largely architectural and decreases with training. Learning effects become visible only at atom-compositional level: decoupling flips sign from negative to positive and becomes regime-invariant, while downward causation carries regime-dependent adjustment.

Conclusion: g is the architectural locus of Φₓ-relevant temporal organization in active inference agents. Scalar Φₓ should not be read as a direct index of learned integration; meaningful learning effects are found in compositional information flow patterns rather than aggregate magnitude.

Abstract: A recent line of work measures causal emergence in reinforcement learning agents through Integrated Information Decomposition, reporting that $Φ_r$ grows with training and tracks reward improvement. For active inference, this raises the question of how reward-free predictive organization relates to such information-theoretic signatures. I test this within an active inference agent whose architecture separates a fast perception latent $z$ from a slow global latent $g$, where $g$ is driven by prediction error and structurally decoupled from policy gradients. In a reward-free environmental regime-switching protocol, $Φ_r$ concentrates in $g$; its aggregate magnitude is largely architectural and decreases with training. The substantive effect of learning becomes legible only at the atom-compositional level: decoupling flips sign from negative to positive and becomes regime-invariant under environmental change, while downward causation carries the regime-dependent adjustment. These results identify $g$ as the architectural locus of $Φ_r$-relevant temporal organization in an active inference agent, and argue against reading scalar $Φ_r$ as a direct index of learned integration.

</details>


### [174] [LLMs Get Lost in Evolving User Intent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20734)
*Jihoon Tack,Philippe Laban,Jennifer Neville*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLMs perform well in static, single-turn settings but struggle significantly when tracking and responding to user intent that evolves dynamically across multi-turn conversations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLMs are evaluated and trained primarily in static, single-turn settings where user intent is fully specified upfront. However, real human-AI collaboration involves dynamic conversations where users disclose, revise, and reshape their intent incrementally over multiple turns. There's a need to understand how well LLMs track and act on evolving user intent in authentic interactive settings.

Method: Researchers introduced a framework that transforms existing static, single-turn tasks into dynamic multi-turn conversations where user intent evolves across turns (incrementally revealed, revised, and sometimes redirected mid-conversation). This approach preserves original evaluation protocols, enabling reuse of existing benchmarks without requiring new annotation. The method was applied across multiple tasks to test different model families.

Result: Across multiple tasks and model families, researchers found a consistent phenomenon: strong performance in static settings does not transfer to evolving-intent settings. Models showed substantial performance drops when required to track and respond to dynamically evolving user intent, indicating that current LLMs lack this critical capability.

Conclusion: Today's LLMs have a fundamental gap in their ability to faithfully track and act on user intent as it evolves during conversations. This capability is invisible to static evaluations but is critical for building effective collaborative AI agents. The findings highlight the need for new evaluation frameworks and training approaches that account for dynamic intent evolution in real-world interactive settings.

Abstract: As LLMs become more capable, they are increasingly deployed as collaborative agents, taking on user-delegated tasks through iterative interaction. Yet genuine interaction is inherently dynamic: users rarely specify their intent upfront, instead disclosing, revising, and reshaping it as the conversation unfolds. Despite this, LLMs are still predominantly evaluated or trained in single-turn, fully-specified settings, leaving open a fundamental question: how well do LLMs track and act on user intent as it evolves over the course of a conversation? To study this, we introduce a framework that transforms static, single-turn tasks into dynamic multi-turn conversations in which the user's intent evolves across turns--incrementally revealed, revised, and at times redirected mid-conversation--while preserving each task's original evaluation protocol, enabling existing benchmarks to be reused as controlled testbeds without new annotation. Across multiple tasks, we surface a consistent phenomenon: strong static-setting performance does not transfer to the evolving-intent setting, with substantial drops across model families. Our findings point to a fundamental gap: today's LLMs do not yet faithfully track and act on the user's evolving intent, a capability invisible to static evaluation yet critical for future collaborative agents.

</details>


### [175] [Cardinality-Decomposed Loss: Matching Training Objectives to Relation Structure in Heterogeneous Recommendation Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20737)
*Parul Maheshwari,Amulya Paruchuri,Yiqing Zou,Alireza Sahami Shirazi,Farhad Farahani,Prakhar Mehrotra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes Cardinality-Decomposed Loss (CDL) combining Cross Entropy and BPR to handle heterogeneous bipartite graphs with varying cardinalities in recommendation systems, preventing attribute embedding collapse that standard BPR causes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard BPR loss causes attribute embeddings to collapse to near-random geometry in heterogenous bipartite graphs with varying cardinalities (e.g., user-item preferences vs. user-attributes). This silent failure pollutes user node embeddings and hurts downstream tasks but remains invisible to conventional ranking metrics.

Method: Proposes Cardinality-Decomposed Loss (CDL) that combines Cross Entropy (CE) for one-to-one relations (like attributes) and Bayesian Personalized Ranking (BPR) for one-to-many relations (like user-item preferences). Uses lambda parameter to navigate trade-off between the two losses.

Result: CDL consistently improves discriminability in attribute embeddings across five datasets (MovieLens-1M, Last.fm-360K, PayPal Audience Factory, BookCrossing, Yelp). Ranking (NDCG) improves when attributes carry meaningful preference signal but conflicts when correlation is weak. Lambda-sweep reveals dataset behavior governed by semantic alignment (whether attribute predicts preferences) and topology leakage (whether graph connectivity encodes preferences).

Conclusion: A unified loss function (BPR) for heterogeneous graphs with varying cardinalities causes attribute embedding collapse. The proposed CDL approach with separate losses for different cardinalities better handles these complex recommendation scenarios, with the optimal lambda parameter depending on dataset properties of semantic alignment and topology leakage.

Abstract: Graph Neural Networks trained on heterogenous bipartite graphs form a common basis in recommendation systems. These graphs often express relations that vary in cardinality, for example, user-item preferences are one-to-many and user-attribute features are one-to-one. Traditionally, a unique loss function is applied for all of the network components which is often Bayesian Personalized Ranking (BPR). While BPR works well for the recommendation task, we find that it causes attribute embeddings to collapse to near-random geometry -- a silent failure that leaves standard ranking metrics largely unaffected and therefore invisible to conventional evaluation. This in turn pollutes user node embeddings, which are shaped by both edge types simultaneously, hurting downstream tasks like personalization, segmentation, etc. Here we propose a Cardinality-Decomposed Loss (CDL) that combines both Cross Entropy (CE) and BPR to enable the model to collectively optimize for relations across cardinalities. We confirm this CE-BPR conflict by showing the two losses compete in the shared encoder's parameter space. We evaluate CDL on five datasets spanning two structural configurations -- one-to-one attributes on user nodes (MovieLens-1M, Last.fm-360K, PayPal Audience Factory, BookCrossing) and on item nodes (Yelp) -- and find that CDL consistently improves discriminability in attribute embeddings. We also show that ranking (NDCG) improves when attributes carry meaningful preference signal, but conflicts with it when the correlation is weak. We use a lambda parameter to navigate this trade-off, and a lambda-sweep reveals that dataset behavior is governed by two graph properties -- semantic alignment and topology leakage. Semantic alignment measures whether the attribute predicts preferences, while topology leakage measures whether the graph's connectivity already encodes it.

</details>


### [176] [Adaptive Confidence-weighted Expansion for Trustworthy Multi-Omics Multimodal Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20742)
*Mohammad Raahemi,Ali Sekhavati,Alireza Maleki,Hamid Nasiri*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ACE is a novel multimodal fusion framework that enhances trustworthiness by generating complementary modalities and using dual-level confidence mechanisms to adaptively weigh modalities and estimate global trust scores, outperforming state-of-the-art methods on multi-omics datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multimodal learning approaches lack robust mechanisms for dynamic assessment of data quality and trustable confidence scores, which limits their clinical applicability in safety-critical settings where noisy or uninformative data streams are common.

Method: ACE introduces Adaptive Confidence-weighted Expansion with two key components: (1) enhances multimodal space by generating new complementary modalities from intra-modality correlations, and (2) employs dual-level confidence mechanism that adaptively reweights modalities by reliability before fusion and estimates global trust score over final decision.

Result: ACE significantly outperforms existing state-of-the-art algorithms on four challenging multi-omics datasets (BRCA, KIPAN, LGG, ROSMAP) in both classification performance and confidence calibration.

Conclusion: ACE provides a more stable and robust data fusion method that facilitates the use of multimodal learning in high-stakes problems by enhancing trustworthiness through complementary modality generation and dual-level confidence mechanisms.

Abstract: Multimodal learning is a robust approach to improve predictive performance in applications such as medical prognosis. However, the clinical applicability of models that use multimodal learning is hampered by their poor performance under noisy or uninformative data streams. Present fusion approaches often lack robust mechanisms for the dynamic assessment of data quality and for the provision of a trustable confidence score on the final prediction. This dissuades their deployment in safety-critical settings. To address these limitations, we introduce Adaptive Confidence-weighted Expansion (ACE), a novel framework to enhance the trustworthiness of multimodal fusion models. ACE first enhances the multimodal space by generating new, complementary modalities from intra-modality correlations. It then employs a dual-level confidence mechanism that (1) adaptively reweighs all modalities by their reliability before fusion and (2) estimates a global trust score over the fused, final decision. To evaluate ACE, we used four challenging multi-omics datasets (BRCA, KIPAN, LGG, and ROSMAP). ACE significantly outperforms existing state-of-the-art algorithms in both classification performance and confidence calibration. Our framework provides a more stable and robust data fusion method that facilitates the use of multimodal learning in addressing high-stakes problems.

</details>


### [177] [GaugeQuant: Online Learning of Quantization-Optimal Bases from LLM Symmetries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20757)
*Miguel P. Bento,João Seabra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GaugeQuant: A training method that leverages transformer symmetry to break quantization symmetries, minimizing activation outliers for improved low-bit quantization without calibration data or simulation overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformers have internal continuous symmetries that affect quantization but leave outputs invariant. Current post-training quantization methods require frozen models and calibration datasets. There's a need for in-training quantization methods that can improve quantization quality without these requirements.

Method: Introduces a LogSumExp term to the training loss that breaks the internal symmetries, selecting a basis that minimizes activation outliers. Uses a stop-gradient operator to ensure only rotation matrices are updated while keeping the language modeling objective unaltered. The approach works during regular training without specific calibration data or quantization simulation.

Result: Significant perplexity improvements: With LLaMA-2 7B under W4A4 quantization (group size 128), perplexity drops from 8.22 to 6.73. Under W4A16, perplexity drops from 11.16 to 5.45. The method competes with post-training quantization techniques that require frozen models and calibration datasets, while adding negligible training overhead.

Conclusion: GaugeQuant successfully leverages transformer symmetries to improve quantization quality during training, achieving competitive results without the need for calibration data, quantization simulation, or frozen models. The method demonstrates that in-training quantization optimization can be effective and efficient.

Abstract: Transformers are known to have internal continuous symmetries that leave outputs invariant, while modifying quantization. GaugeQuant leverages this in-training by introducing a LogSumExp term to the loss that breaks the symmetries, thus selecting a basis that minimizes activation outliers. A stop-gradient operator ensures that only rotation matrices are updated, yielding the language modeling objective completely unaltered. Our requires no specific calibration data, no quantization simulation, and adds negligible training overhead. With the LLaMA-2 7B model under W4A4 quantization with group size 128, perplexity drops from 8.22 to 6.73, competing with post-training methods that require frozen models and calibration datasets. Under W4A16, perplexity drops from 11.16 to 5.45. Code is available at https://github.com/MPedraBento/gauge-quant.

</details>


### [178] [Memory-Computation Tradeoffs in Semi Amortized Parametric Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20769)
*Shijie Pan,Agustin Castellano,Zeyu Shen,Enrique Mallada*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the trade-off between offline memory storage and online computation for solving parametric optimization problems, establishing theoretical bounds on how much memory is needed to achieve desired accuracy with limited online computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning-enabled decision systems often use offline data/computation to reduce online compute costs, but there's limited understanding of how much offline information is needed to achieve desired accuracy under fixed online computation budgets.

Method: The authors study amortized parametric optimization: offline phase stores finite memory of solved problem instances, online phase uses nonparametric predictor built from stored solutions to provide warm starts for K steps of projected gradient descent.
