Result: For μ-strongly convex objectives, matching upper/lower bounds on memory required for ε-accuracy under fixed iteration budget K. For convex objectives with β-growth (β>2), near-matching bounds and phase transition in K beyond which additional memory provides no benefit. Developed general proof framework quantifying memory cost of acceleration.

Conclusion: The work provides theoretical understanding of memory-computation-accuracy tradeoffs in parametric optimization, identifying key quantities driving this tradeoff: convergence rate of online optimizer and Lipschitz sensitivity of solution map to problem parameters.

Abstract: Learning-enabled decision systems often use offline data or computation to reduce online compute cost. Despite the empirical success of such approaches, there is limited general understanding of how much offline information is needed to achieve a desired accuracy under a fixed online computation budget. We study this question through the lens of amortized parametric optimization: an offline phase stores a finite memory of solved problem instances, and an online phase produces a solution to a new instance by retrieving a warm start and applying $K$ steps of projected gradient descent. We analyze this setup for smooth convex parametric optimization over a compact domain, using a nonparametric predictor built from the stored offline solutions. For $μ$-strongly convex objectives, we establish matching upper and lower bounds on the memory required to guarantee $\varepsilon$-accuracy under a fixed online iteration budget $K$. For convex objectives satisfying a $β$-growth condition ($β>2$), we obtain near-matching bounds and identify a phase transition in $K$ beyond which additional memory provides no benefit. We further provide a general proof framework that (i) explicitly quantifies the memory cost of acceleration---how much offline memory is required to achieve a prescribed speedup over the unaided online optimizer---and (ii) identifies two key quantities driving this cost: the convergence rate of the online optimizer and the Lipschitz sensitivity of the solution map to the problem parameter. Experiments on parameterized ridge regression confirm the predicted memory--computation--accuracy tradeoffs.

</details>


### [179] [Toward Mechanistic Interpretability of an AI Foundation Model Fine-Tuned for Atmospheric Chemistry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20778)
*Jason Y. Hu,Ivan Higuera-Mendieta,Patrick Obin Sturm,Makoto M. Kelp*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A study examining Microsoft's Aurora weather forecasting foundation model fine-tuned for air quality finds it captures some chemical patterns but lacks true physical/chemical process understanding, demonstrating chemically inconsistent outputs and meteorology-dominated internal representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Weather forecasting foundation models are increasingly used for air quality prediction but operate as black boxes that may exploit statistical patterns rather than learn actual atmospheric chemistry mechanisms. There's a need to understand what these models actually learn when fine-tuned for chemical forecasting.

Method: Examined Microsoft's Aurora model by imposing controlled chemical perturbations on its forecasts and testing against known photochemical relationships. Analyzed internal representations, identified chemically inconsistent combinations, and used sparse autoencoders to find components controlling chemical forecasts.

Result: Aurora captures first-order ozone response to reactive nitrogen but fails to enforce chemical constraints of process-based models. It generates chemically inconsistent species combinations and relaxes localized emissions toward background. Internal representations remain organized around inherited meteorology with little chemistry-specific structure.

Conclusion: Weather forecasting foundation models fine-tuned for atmospheric chemistry don't truly learn physical/chemical mechanisms despite good forecast skill. A framework is needed to test whether AI systems learn actual chemistry, and composition forecasts should be evaluated by internal mechanisms, not just benchmark performance.

Abstract: Weather forecasting foundation models (FMs) are increasingly fine-tuned to predict air quality, offering fast global pollution forecasts at lower computational cost than conventional chemical transport models. These FMs are typically trained on reanalysis data and generate forecasts through autoregressive rollout. They do not explicitly represent governing physical or chemical processes. Therefore, high forecast skill does not reveal whether a model has learned physical mechanisms or exploits statistical regularities in its training data. Here, we present the first study of what a FM fine-tuned for atmospheric chemistry has learned by examining Microsoft's Aurora model. We impose controlled chemical perturbations on its forecasts and test them against known photochemical relationships. We then examine the internal representations that generate these forecasts. We find that Aurora captures a first-order ozone response to reactive nitrogen but does not enforce the chemical constraints that a process-based model encodes. It generates chemically inconsistent combinations of related species and relaxes localized emission features such as wildfire plumes toward background. Internally, its representations remain largely organized around the meteorology inherited during pretraining, with little structure specific to chemistry. Using sparse autoencoders, we identify internal components that causally control the chemical forecast but do not map cleanly onto individual atmospheric processes. This work provides a framework for testing whether AI forecasting systems learn atmospheric chemistry from reanalysis data. As these models are increasingly positioned to inform environmental policy decisions, we argue that composition forecasts should also be judged by their internal mechanisms rather than by benchmark skill alone.

</details>


### [180] [Synthetic minority data is redundant or invalid: a data-dependent validity theory and a de-biased test](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20787)
*Ahmad B. Hassanat,Ahmad S. Tarawneh,Ghada A. Altarawneh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper debunks 20 years of class-imbalanced learning research by showing that standard synthetic data generation methods are fundamentally flawed - they fail both validity and usefulness tests, with most methods performing worse than trivial baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: For two decades, class-imbalanced learning has relied on creating synthetic minority examples, but the standard validation method is biased - it scores synthetic points against the very data that generated them, creating a test that "cannot fail."

Method: The authors propose a de-biased validation approach that treats validity as a population quantity with a consistent estimator scoring synthetic points against withheld real data. They analyze 91 methods across three classifiers on medical and financial datasets, including one generator specifically engineered to pass the classical check.

Result: The classical test underestimates true invalidity in 96-99% of cases, while the de-biased estimator accurately tracks it. All methods fail both validity and usefulness tests: gains over trivial baselines are negligible (median below 0.01 F1), most methods damage calibration, and none clear both bars of validity and information gain.

Conclusion: The paper fundamentally challenges two decades of oversampling research. It shows validity is determined by class overlap in the data, not the generation method, making oversampling redundant for separable classes and invalid for overlapping ones. The authors release an audit tool that flips the burden of proof, requiring synthetic data to demonstrate both validity and information gain on the actual data.

Abstract: For two decades, the standard remedy for class-imbalanced learning has been to fabricate synthetic minority examples, and the standard evidence of their validity has been a check that cannot fail: synthetic points are scored against the very data that generated them. We de-bias the check. Validity becomes a population quantity -- the probability that a synthetic point truly belongs to the minority class -- with a consistent estimator that scores synthetic points against withheld real data. Where held-out ground truth is available, the classical test underestimates true invalidity in 96-99% of method-by-imbalance-ratio cells, while the de-biased estimator tracks it closely. We prove validity is a property of the data, not the method: class overlap sets an invalidity floor no faithful generator escapes, making oversampling redundant where classes separate and invalid where they overlap. Across 91 methods, three classifiers, and datasets spanning medicine and finance -- including a generator engineered to pass the classical check -- none clears both bars: gains over the best trivial baseline are noise-thin (median below 0.01 F1, a decision threshold's reach), and most damage calibration. We release the audit as a pip-installable test and flip the burden of proof: synthetic minority data must now demonstrate, on the data at hand, both validity and information gain.

</details>


### [181] [Memoir: Should a Model Write to Its Memory While It Thinks?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20792)
*Jaber Jaber,Osama Jaber*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Memoir introduces a novel memory architecture with coupled read-write pondering but shows that this coupling causes a learning speed penalty compared to read-only pondering, though both eventually achieve perfect performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test the riskiest coupling in memory systems where each pondering iteration can rewrite the same fast memory it reads from, exploring whether such synchronous read-write operations can work effectively without corrupting learning signals.

Method: Memoir combines per-sample fast memory, shared slow parameters, variable-depth latent recurrence, and future-latent energy objective. It compares coupled (read-write) pondering against read-only pondering with identical parameter counts (81,738), computational budgets, data, optimizer, and experimental setup.

Result: Read-only pondering outperformed coupled pondering in learning speed (0.6557 vs 0.5203 recall at 240 steps), winning on 10/12 seeds. However, both reached perfect recall (1.0000) after 960 steps. The predicted memory corruption failure didn't occur - energy margin grew and held. Kernel optimization reduced forward time from 0.907ms to 0.351ms.

Conclusion: The coupled read-write pondering architecture functions without signal corruption but imposes a learning speed penalty compared to read-only approaches. While both methods eventually achieve equal final performance, the coupling slows convergence, suggesting practical trade-offs in memory system design.

Abstract: Memoir combines per-sample fast memory, shared slow parameters, variable-depth latent recurrence, and a future-latent energy objective. We test its riskiest coupling: each pondering iteration may rewrite the fast tier that the same iteration reads. On procedural associative recall with key interference, we compare a coupled arm against an otherwise identical read-only pondering arm. Both arms contain 81,738 parameters, including 76,362 trainable parameters, and use matched declared forward multiply-accumulate counts, data, optimizer, schedule, and seeds. After 240 training steps across 12 seeds, coupled recall is 0.5203 with a 95 percent interval of [0.4522, 0.5883], while read-only recall is 0.6557 with [0.5953, 0.7160]. The arms are paired per seed, and the read-only lead of 0.1354 gives a paired t of 3.23 on 11 degrees of freedom with a 95 percent interval of [0.0431, 0.2277] on the difference, winning on 10 of 12 seeds. After 960 steps across 8 seeds, both arms reach 1.0000, so the measured effect is a learning-speed penalty at a fixed budget, not a demonstrated capability penalty. That longer control is ceiling limited, leaving convergence on a non-saturating task unmeasured. A predicted failure in which memory rewriting corrupts the energy signal did not occur: the energy margin grew and held. Kernel restructuring also reduced delta-rule forward time from 0.907 ms to 0.351 ms on the stated device. Code and evidence are available at https://github.com/RightNow-AI/Memoir

</details>


### [182] [External Clustering Validation by the Homogeneity-Parsimony Trade-off](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20799)
*Andreas Tiffeau-Mayer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A framework for evaluating clusterings using normalized homogeneity and parsimony scores based on information theory, with set-matching and pair-based variants that unify common evaluation criteria and reveal trade-offs between informativeness and fragmentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scalar metrics for evaluating clusterings against known classes often obscure the fundamental trade-off: clusterings should be informative about class labels while avoiding unnecessary fragmentation. Current metrics fail to capture this intuitive property.

Method: Proposes normalized scores of cluster homogeneity and parsimony based on the information bottleneck principle, modified to not reward lossy compression. Extends the framework beyond Shannon entropies to derive set-matching and pair-based counterparts.

Result: The scores have the intuitive property of varying monotonically under cluster refinement (unlike related proposals). The pair-based homogeneity-parsimony trade-off recovers the receiver operating characteristic of binary classifiers. The framework unifies commonly used evaluation criteria.

Conclusion: The proposed framework is useful for feature selection and algorithm comparison, allowing joint consideration of scores to clarify clustering operating points and identify Pareto-optimal solutions by explicitly quantifying the trade-off between informativeness and fragmentation.

Abstract: Scalar metrics are often used to evaluate clusterings against known classes, but they can obscure a fundamental trade-off: clusterings should be informative about class labels while avoiding unnecessary fragmentation. Here we describe normalized scores of cluster homogeneity and parsimony that quantify this trade-off. These scores build on the information bottleneck principle, modified to not reward lossy compression. We show by example and mathematical proof that our definitions of these scores have the intuitive property of varying monotonically under cluster refinement in contrast to related proposals. Extending the information-theoretic framework beyond Shannon entropies, we furthermore derive set-matching and pair-based counterparts of the homogeneity and parsimony scores. These unify commonly used evaluation criteria and show that, in the pair-based setting, the homogeneity-parsimony trade-off recovers the receiver operating characteristic of binary classifiers. We demonstrate the framework's utility for feature selection and algorithm comparison, illustrating how considering scores jointly can clarify clustering operating points and identify Pareto-optimal solutions.

</details>


### [183] [New Complexity-Theoretic Frontiers of Tractability for Neural Network Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20811)
*Cornelius Brand,Robert Ganian,Mathis Rocton*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents new polynomial-time algorithms for optimally training neural networks with linear and ReLU activations, improving tractability bounds beyond previous state-of-the-art results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite neural networks' fundamental role in machine learning, understanding the computational complexity of optimally training them remains incomplete, especially for simple activation functions like linear and ReLU. While recent results have established tighter lower bounds, less progress has been made in identifying novel polynomial-time tractable network architectures.

Method: The authors develop novel algorithmic upper bounds for training neural networks to optimality. For ReLU networks, they identify architectures where hidden neurons have out-degree 1 as polynomial-time tractable. For linear activation networks, they introduce a novel data throughput condition that enables polynomial-time training.

Result: For ReLU networks: Established polynomial-time tractability for all architectures where hidden neurons have out-degree 1, improving upon the previous algorithm by Arora et al. For linear activation networks: Identified the first non-trivial polynomial-time solvable class of networks via an algorithm that can optimally train network architectures satisfying a novel data throughput condition.

Conclusion: The paper pushes the boundaries of tractability for training neural networks to optimality by providing new polynomial-time algorithms, addressing gaps in understanding computational complexity for training networks with simple activation functions like linear and ReLU.

Abstract: In spite of the fundamental role of neural networks in contemporary machine learning research, our understanding of the computational complexity of optimally training neural networks remains incomplete even when dealing with the simplest kinds of activation functions. Indeed, while there has been a number of very recent results that establish ever-tighter lower bounds for the problem under linear and ReLU activation functions, less progress has been made towards the identification of novel polynomial-time tractable network architectures. In this article we obtain novel algorithmic upper bounds for training linear- and ReLU-activated neural networks to optimality which push the boundaries of tractability for these problems beyond the previous state of the art. In particular, for ReLU networks we establish the polynomial-time tractability of all architectures where hidden neurons have an out-degree of $1$, improving upon the previous algorithm of Arora, Basu, Mianjy and Mukherjee. On the other hand, for networks with linear activation functions we identify the first non-trivial polynomial-time solvable class of networks by obtaining an algorithm that can optimally train network architectures satisfying a novel data throughput condition.

</details>


### [184] [Robust Asynchronous Q-Learning under Reward and State Corruption via Batching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20822)
*Sreejeet Maity,Aritra Mitra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BR-Async-Q is a robust Q-learning algorithm designed to handle adversarially corrupted feedback in reinforcement learning, achieving near-optimal performance guarantees despite corrupted rewards and states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper is motivated by reinforcement learning applications in harsh environments where feedback (rewards and states) can be adversarially corrupted. The authors want to develop robust learning algorithms that can withstand such data corruption while maintaining performance guarantees.

Method: The authors propose BR-Async-Q, a novel epoch-based robust Q-learning algorithm. Key innovations include: (1) partitioning online data streams into batches to reduce variance, and (2) constructing robust estimates of the Bellman optimality operator using batched data to defend against Huber contamination model corruption.

Result: The algorithm achieves a high-probability ℓ∞ error bound that matches vanilla Q-learning, plus a small additive term scaling with the fraction of corrupted samples. When only rewards are corrupted, the algorithm achieves minimax optimal dependence on corruption fraction.

Conclusion: BR-Async-Q provides the first robustness guarantee for asynchronous Q-learning under both reward and state corruption, offering a practical solution for reinforcement learning in adversarial environments with near-optimal theoretical guarantees.

Abstract: Motivated by reinforcement learning in harsh environments, we consider the problem of learning an optimal policy subject to adversarially corrupted feedback. Specifically, at each time-step, an adversary can perturb both the reward and state observations of the learner following the Huber contamination model. To defend against such data corruption, we propose {\texttt{BR-Async-Q}}: a novel, epoch-based, robust \(Q\)-learning algorithm built upon two key ideas: (i) partitioning the online data stream into batches to reduce variance, and (ii) constructing robust estimates of the Bellman optimality operator using such batched data. We prove a high-probability $\ell_\infty$ error bound for {\texttt{BR-Async-Q}} that matches that for vanilla \(Q\)-learning, up to a small additive term that scales with the fraction of corrupted samples. To our knowledge, this provides the first robustness guarantee for asynchronous \(Q\)-learning subject to both reward and state corruption. Furthermore, when only rewards are corrupted, the dependence of our algorithm's bound on the corruption fraction is minimax optimal.

</details>


### [185] [Beyond Heavy Log Curation: Perplexity-Based APT Detection via Unsupervised, Context-Augmented Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20832)
*Shoya Otsu,Kei Suzuki,Toshiaki Koike-Akino,Jing Liu,Ye Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CAPTAIN is a perplexity-based APT detection system that uses pre-trained language models with minimal preprocessing to identify threat activities in logs, reducing engineering costs while maintaining competitive performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: APT detection is challenging due to low signal-to-noise ratio in logs and high costs of investigation. Existing ML approaches require heavily curated training data and sophisticated preprocessing pipelines that demand substantial domain expertise and engineering resources.

Method: CAPTAIN uses pre-trained language models with minimal domain-agnostic preprocessing. It encodes recent history using an encoder model with Q-Former-style bridge, injects compact context tokens into decoder input to make perplexity reflect temporal context, and applies smoothing filters to perplexity time series for stability.

Result: CAPTAIN competes with strong existing baselines across APT-oriented benchmarks while remaining robust under substantially less curated inputs, reducing development and operational costs of advanced log preprocessing.

Conclusion: CAPTAIN demonstrates that using general pre-trained language models with minimal preprocessing can achieve effective APT detection while significantly reducing the engineering burden and domain expertise required for traditional ML-based detection systems.

Abstract: Advanced Persistent Threats (APTs) remain difficult to detect because only a small fraction of events in large-scale logs are attack-related, and investigation is expensive and hard to scale. Prior machine-learning approaches can reduce analyst workload, but they often rely on heavily curated training data and sophisticated preprocessing pipelines. Building and maintaining such pipelines require substantial domain expertise and engineering cost. Motivated by insights from a study of a strong APT detection baseline, we propose CAPTAIN (Context-Augmented Perplexity-based Threat Activity log detectIoN), a perplexity-based detector that leverages general, pre-trained language models with minimal, domain-agnostic preprocessing, enabling robust scoring of long, minimally processed log entries. CAPTAIN encodes recent history with an encoder model and a Q-Former-style bridge, then injects the compact context tokens into the decoder input so that perplexity reflects temporal context. To improve stability, CAPTAIN additionally applies smoothing filters to the perplexity time series. Across APT-oriented benchmarks, CAPTAIN competes with strong existing baselines and remains robust under substantially less curated inputs, that reduces the development and operational cost of advanced log preprocessing.

</details>


### [186] [Offline RL with Hierarchical Action Chunking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20834)
*Ahad Jawaid*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HiQC is an offline goal-conditioned RL algorithm that combines hierarchical planning with action chunking to tackle long-horizon tasks by reducing value estimation error compounding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Offline goal-conditioned RL struggles with long-horizon tasks due to the curse of horizon, where value estimation errors compound through bootstrapped Bellman backups. Existing hierarchical approaches still suffer from myopic low-level controllers and biased value estimates.

Method: Hierarchical Implicit Q-Chunking (HiQC) combines high-level latent planning with low-level action chunking. It conditions the low-level critic on temporally extended action sequences to enable unbiased k-step value backups, compressing horizon at both planning and execution levels.

Result: HiQC achieves the highest aggregate performance on the OGBench suite, with largest gains on long-horizon navigation tasks like humanoid-giant. Theoretically, it provides tighter bounds on value error compared to standard hierarchy or flat chunking alone.

Conclusion: Dual decomposition through hierarchical planning and action chunking effectively addresses the curse of horizon in offline goal-conditioned RL, enabling better performance on long-horizon tasks through reduced value estimation error compounding.

Abstract: Offline goal-conditioned reinforcement learning (RL) holds the promise of learning general-purpose policies from static datasets. However, scaling these methods to long-horizon tasks remains a challenge due to the curse of horizon, where value estimation errors can compound through long chains of bootstrapped Bellman backups. Existing hierarchical approaches mitigate this by decomposing tasks into subgoals, yet they often rely on low-level controllers that suffer from myopic execution and biased value estimates. In this work, we propose Hierarchical Implicit Q-Chunking (HiQC), an offline goal-conditioned RL algorithm that combines high-level latent planning with low-level action chunking. By conditioning the low-level critic on temporally extended action sequences, HiQC enables unbiased k-step value backups, compressing the horizon at both the planning and execution levels. We theoretically demonstrate that this dual decomposition results in a tighter bound on value error under a bounded per-backup error model compared to standard hierarchy or flat chunking alone. Empirically, HiQC achieves the highest aggregate performance among the compared methods on the OGBench suite, with its largest gains on long-horizon navigation tasks such as humanoid-giant.

</details>


### [187] [Multilevel Graph Wavelet Compressed Sensing with Scale-Aware Neural Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20857)
*Amirhossein Nouranizadeh,Sarang Rajendra Patil,Alan John Varghese,Varsha Narayanan,Amit Chakraborty,Mengjia Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A learning-based framework called Graph Wavelet Compressed Sensing (GWCS) for offline compression of graph signals using sparse wavelet representations, achieving high reconstruction fidelity with substantial data compression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current scientific machine learning methods like neural operators and physics-informed neural networks require large volumes of simulated data for training, making data preparation and model training expensive. There's a need for efficient compression methods for graph signals used in engineering applications and inverse problems.

Method: Proposes Graph Wavelet Compressed Sensing (GWCS) framework that combines: 1) a nonparametric multilevel importance sampler that retains high-energy wavelet coefficients within each scale for a given compression ratio, and 2) a scale-aware graph neural network that reconstructs the signal from the sparse wavelet coefficients using spectral graph wavelet transform.

Result: Evaluated on synthetic approximately band-limited graph signals over random graphs and four PDE simulation datasets (Turbulent Radiative Layer, Viscoelastic Instability, Kolmogorov Flow, Dynamic Stall). The framework achieves high reconstruction fidelity and substantial data compression compared to graph signal sampling methods and graph autoencoder baselines.

Conclusion: GWCS provides an effective learning-based approach for compressing graph signals through interpretable sparse wavelet representations, addressing the data preparation and training costs associated with scientific machine learning methods while maintaining high reconstruction quality.

Abstract: Scientific machine learning methods such as neural operators and physics-informed neural networks have advanced engineering applications and inverse problems, but their training typically requires large volumes of simulated data. This makes data preparation and model training expensive. We propose Graph Wavelet Compressed Sensing (GWCS), a learning-based framework for offline compression of graph signals by representing them as sparse, interpretable wavelet-domain representations using the spectral graph wavelet transform. The framework combines a nonparametric multilevel importance sampler, which retains high-energy wavelet coefficients within each scale for a given compression ratio, with a scale-aware graph neural network that reconstructs the signal from the sparse coefficients. We evaluate the proposed framework on synthetic approximately band-limited graph signals over random graphs and four PDE simulation datasets over meshes, which include Turbulent Radiative Layer, Viscoelastic Instability, Kolmogorov Flow, and Dynamic Stall. We compare against graph signal sampling methods and graph autoencoder baselines. Results demonstrate that the framework achieves high reconstruction fidelity and substantial data compression compared to existing benchmarks.

</details>


### [188] [Position Bias is Hidden Behind Ceiling Effects: A Permutation Diagnostic for LLM Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20864)
*Hiroki Tamba*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Position bias in LLM evaluation is only statistically detectable within a specific "Goldilocks zone" of base accuracy (60-95%), with two mechanism types: monotone processing load in low-tier models and non-monotone content ambiguity in narrow capability bands. Frontier models perform above this detection band, making bias unmeasurable rather than absent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current measurements of position bias in multiple-choice LLM evaluation are flawed because they rely on single answer-order shuffles that confound bias signals with content-level noise and sampling stochasticity. The field needs more rigorous, statistically sound methods to properly assess position bias in capability comparisons.

Method: Developed inspect_permute, an open-source extension to inspect_ai framework that runs exhaustive answer-order permutations per question and reports chi-squared/Cramer V signatures with bootstrap confidence intervals. Applied across four vendors (gpt-4o-mini, claude-haiku-4-5, gemini-2.5-flash, grok-3) on five MMLU subjects with 24,000 API calls at temperature-0. Used pre-registered falsifier predictions via public SHA-256 hash before data collection.

Result: Position bias is only statistically detectable within a 60-95% base-accuracy "Goldilocks zone." Below this zone, processing-load dominance swamps subject-specific signals; above it, ceiling effects compress variance below test resolution. Two mechanism types emerge: monotone A-to-D decrease (processing_load in low-tier models) and non-monotone D-drop (content_ambiguity in narrow capability bands). Frontier models in standard MMLU are above detection band.

Conclusion: Position bias measurement is bounded by a detectable region between processing load dominance and ceiling effects. Frontier models' absence of position bias signal should be interpreted as "not measurable" rather than "unbiased." The work enables the field to ask central questions about position bias in a verifiable form, with tools available as open-source under MIT license.

Abstract: Position bias in multiple-choice LLM evaluation is widely cited as a confound in capability comparisons, but published measurements rely on single answer-order shuffles whose results confound the bias signal with content-level noise and sampling stochasticity. I introduce inspect_permute, an open-source extension to the inspect_ai evaluation framework that runs exhaustive answer-order permutations per question and reports the chi-squared / Cramer V signature of position bias with bootstrap confidence intervals. I apply the tool across four vendors (gpt-4o-mini, claude-haiku-4-5, gemini-2.5-flash, grok-3) on five MMLU subjects, 24,000 API calls under temperature-0 generation, with falsifier predictions pre-registered via a public SHA-256 hash before half the data was observed. Position bias turns out to be statistically detectable only within a roughly 60-95% base-accuracy Goldilocks zone. Below it, processing-load dominance swamps subject-specific signal; above it, ceiling effects compress the variance below the chi-squared test resolution. Detectable cells separate into two mechanism types: monotone A-to-D decrease (processing_load, in low-tier models) and non-monotone D-drop (content_ambiguity, in a narrow capability band). Standard MMLU places every frontier-tier model above the detection band, so absence of signal there should be read as not measurable, not unbiased. Together with the ceiling-effect characterisation in arXiv:2606.26185, this work brackets the detectable region of position-bias measurement and makes the field central question askable in a verifiable form. Package, data, preregistration under MIT.

</details>


### [189] [TwistedMerge: Certified Higher-Order Diagnostics and Abstention for Model Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20887)
*Ting Gong,Shitan Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Model merging verification via descent theory: TwistedMerge pipeline certifies or abstains on merges using cohomological consistency tests, separating true obstructions from gauge artifacts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Model merging faces issues where pairwise alignability doesn't guarantee globally consistent alignment, leading to potential degradation when merging multiple checkpoints. Need rigorous certification framework to distinguish true merging obstructions from gauge inconsistencies.

Method: Formulate merging as finite descent problem with checkpoints as local objects, alignment maps as transitions, and cycle products as residuals. TwistedMerge pipeline performs: fixed-chart averaging, removal of gauge inconsistencies, certified central obstruction analysis on comparison complex, and nonabelian holonomy testing. Residual promoted to cohomology class only after passing four tests: inverse-consistency, coefficient-identification, centrality, and closure.

Result: Proved constant-edge no-go result, frozen-complex theorems, and refinement test. Showed cycle-consistent synchronization removes planted neural alignment defects. Controlled systems recover predicted non-coboundary behavior. Found naive factor averaging depends on GLr representative choice, while global synchronization and SVD are stable. Cycle residuals don't predict merge degradation in natural checkpoints, and no natural central/period-index classes certified.

Conclusion: Descent theory provides falsifiable certification and abstention framework for model merging, separating true obstructions from gauge artifacts and enabling safe merging decisions without false positives.

Abstract: Model merging combines independently trained or fine-tuned models, but pairwise alignability does not imply globally consistent alignment. We formulate merging as a finite descent problem in which checkpoints are local objects, alignment maps are transitions, and cycle products are residuals. TwistedMerge is a conservative certification pipeline that separates fixed-chart averaging, synchronization-removable gauge inconsistency, a certified central obstruction on a specified comparison complex, and nonabelian holonomy. A residual is promoted to a cohomology class only after inverse-consistency, coefficient-identification, centrality, and closure tests; otherwise the method abstains and returns an ordinary or synchronized fallback. We prove a constant-edge no-go result, frozen-complex three-way and predeclared-family error-control theorems, and a refinement test for comparison-complex sensitivity. A planted neural alignment defect is removed by cycle-consistent synchronization, showing that a nonzero cycle score alone is not a higher obstruction. Controlled central systems recover the predicted non-coboundary and projective-rank behavior, while noisy estimates move from certification to abstention without false lifts on the tested controls. A trained low-rank-adapter audit shows that naive factor averaging depends on the chosen GLr representative, whereas global factor synchronization and dense-delta SVD are stable. On natural checkpoint collections, cycle residuals do not predict merge degradation and no natural central or period-index class is certified. The results position descent theory as a falsifiable certification and abstention framework.

</details>


### [190] [Information-Theoretically Secure Aggregation for Lightweight Federated Learning: Resilient to Dropouts and Adversaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20890)
*Hyeong-Gun Joo,Songnam Hong,Dong-Joon Shin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A lightweight, information-theoretically secure aggregation framework for sign-based federated learning that protects gradient signs while reducing communication and computation costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sign-based methods like signSGD reduce communication costs in federated learning but expose gradient signs to inference attacks, while existing secure aggregation schemes are incompatible with such methods or incur significant overhead.

Method: Proposes a secure aggregation framework for sign-based FL that computes majority vote polynomial through single-round secure multiplication with two key techniques: inverse-form exponent reduction (halves MV polynomial degree) and single-round secure multiplication (linear offline complexity with single online communication).

Result: Reduces online communication by up to 99.5% and latency by up to 85.7% compared to conventional approaches. Achieves robustness against dropouts and adversarial behaviors with accuracy gains up to 20.65% and 10.74% respectively.

Conclusion: The framework establishes a practical foundation for large-scale, low-latency, and information-theoretically secure aggregation in sign-based FL with end-to-end information-theoretic security under honest-majority assumption.

Abstract: On-device federated learning (FL) enables privacy-preserving and personalized model training on resource-constrained devices such as smartphones and IoT nodes. To reduce communication cost, sign-based methods (e.g., signSGD) transmit one-bit gradients. However, exposing gradient signs makes them vulnerable to inference attacks, while existing secure aggregation schemes are often incompatible with such methods or incur significant computational and communication overhead. We propose a lightweight and information-theoretically secure aggregation framework tailored for sign-based FL. The framework securely computes the majority vote (MV) polynomial through single-round secure multiplication, ensuring end-to-end information-theoretic security under the honest-majority assumption while revealing only the final aggregated sign to the server. To enhance efficiency and scalability, we introduce two key techniques. First, inverse-form exponent reduction halves the effective MV polynomial degree, reducing both communication and computation costs. Second, we propose single-round secure multiplication, achieving linear offline complexity and storage with only a single online communication. Together, these techniques reduce online communication by up to 99.5% and latency by up to 85.7% compared to conventional approaches. Also, by leveraging inherent MDS-code-based decoding, the framework achieves robustness against both dropouts and adversarial behaviors, yielding accuracy gains of up to 20.65% and 10.74%, respectively. Overall, the proposed framework establishes a practical foundation for large-scale, low-latency, and information-theoretically secure aggregation in sign-based FL.

</details>


### [191] [HierarchicalDAEW: Domain-Aware Edge-Weighted Graph Convolution with Evidential Uncertainty for Multi-Section Spatial Gene Expression Prediction from H&E Histology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20896)
*Kritanu Chattopadhyay,Soumya Chatterjee,Ondrej Krejcar,Debotosh Bhattacharjee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HierarchicalDAEW is a dual-graph neural network that predicts spatially resolved gene expression from H&E histology images, using domain-aware edge weighting and hierarchical gene propagation with evidential uncertainty estimation for clinical reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spatial transcriptomics is costly and technically demanding, limiting its clinical use. Current methods that predict gene expression from histology ignore tissue architecture and lack uncertainty quantification, creating barriers for clinical deployment.

Method: Uses a dual-graph architecture: 1) Spot-level graph with Domain-Aware Edge-Weighted convolution that separates edges by tissue domain boundaries using Leiden clustering; 2) Gene-level graph that fuses protein-protein interaction priors with tissue-specific co-expression via learned attention gating to propagate predictions. Incorporates evidential uncertainty estimation for reliable confidence intervals.

Result: Achieves strongest correlation with ground-truth expression across six human Visium sections (breast, colorectal, prostate, cerebellar tissue) compared to 13 published baselines. Gains hold under multi-seed reproducibility checks and negative controls. Ablations confirm both domain-aware edge typing and hierarchical depth are necessary for improvement.

Conclusion: HierarchicalDAEW provides a robust method for predicting spatially resolved gene expression from routine histology, explicitly modeling tissue architecture and providing well-calibrated uncertainty estimates that can identify low-confidence predictions for clinical review, bridging the gap to clinical deployment.

Abstract: Spatial transcriptomics assays remain costly and technically demanding, restricting transcriptome-wide profiling to specialist settings and preventing routine clinical deployment. Predicting spatially resolved gene expression from H&E histology could close this gap, yet current methods largely ignore the underlying tissue architecture and rarely quantify how their predictions can be trusted. We introduce HierarchicalDAEW, a dual-graph architecture that addresses both gaps. On the spot graph, a Domain-Aware Edge-Weighted convolutional operator learns separate projections for inter-domain, intra-domain, and boundary edges derived from Leiden clustering, allowing the model to treat tissue heterogeneity as an explicit structural signal rather than an implicit one. A second gene-level graph then fuses protein-protein interaction priors from STRING-DB with tissue-specific co-expression through learned attention gating, propagating predictions from a landmark gene set to a broader gene panel. Reliability is handled through evidential uncertainty estimation, which produces far better calibrated confidence intervals than Monte Carlo dropout under identical conditions. Across six human Visium sections spanning breast, colorectal, prostate, and cerebellar tissue, and against thirteen published baselines, HierarchicalDAEW achieves the strongest correlation with ground-truth expression, with gains that hold up under multi-seed reproducibility checks and negative controls that rule out positional shortcuts. Ablations further confirm that both the domain-aware edge typing and the hierarchical depth are necessary to this improvement, and calibrated uncertainty estimates identify low-confidence predictions for pathologist review before clinical action.

</details>


### [192] [Multi-turn RL with Structural and Performance Aware Rewards for CUDA Kernel Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20908)
*Quazi Ishtiaque Mahmud,Nesreen K. Ahmed,Ali Jannesari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CudaPerf is a novel RL framework that combines verifiable execution rewards with structural code-aware rewards for optimized CUDA code generation, outperforming baselines by significant margins.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RLVR approaches focus only on outcome-based signals (correctness, speedup) and ignore critical structural properties of programs essential for generating optimized code, especially for CUDA parallel programming.

Method: Two-stage framework: (1) Offline pairwise ranking module learns to distinguish strong/weak programs via contrastive comparisons, (2) Online RL training jointly optimizes correctness, performance, and structural efficiency using unified reward. Uses iterative refinement with execution feedback.

Result: Outperforms Qwen-3-32B (C to CUDA) and CUDA Agent (PyTorch to CUDA): 5X & 3.32X improvements in speedup, 17% & 7% improvements in correctness. Introduces dataset of 2.9k C to CUDA and 1k PyTorch to CUDA programs.

Conclusion: CudaPerf demonstrates the effectiveness of combining verifiable execution rewards with structural code-aware rewards for generating optimized CUDA code, significantly advancing RL-based code optimization techniques.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has emerged as a powerful technique to enhance the reasoning capacity of LLMs for optimized code generation. However, existing RLVR approaches primarily rely on outcome-based signals such as correctness and speedup, overlooking performance-critical structural properties of programs that are essential for generating optimized code. In this work, we propose CudaPerf, a reflective RL framework that incorporates both verifiable execution rewards and structural code-aware rewards derived from parallelization features (e.g., memory coalescing, occupancy, Arithmatic Intensity, and synchronization patterns). CudaPerf operates in two stages: (1) an offline pairwise ranking module that learns to distinguish strong and weak program candidates via contrastive comparisons, and (2) an online RL training phase that jointly optimizes for correctness, performance, and structural efficiency through a unified reward signal. To further enhance learning, CudaPerf utilizes iterative refinement using execution feedback enabling progressive improvement of generated candidates. We also introduce a dataset comprising 2.9k C to CUDA and 1k PyTorch to CUDA programs, each paired with diverse input configurations and multiple CUDA implementations encompassing diverse optimization strategies. CudaPerf is evaluated across multiple benchmarks comprising both C to CUDA and PyTorch to CUDA transformations. Empirical findings suggest that CudaPerf significantly outperforms strong baselines, including Qwen-3-32B (for C to CUDA) and CUDA Agent (for PyTorch to CUDA) by achieving up to 5X & 3.32X improvements in speedup, and 17% & 7% improvements in correctness, respectively.

</details>


### [193] [Three-Pronged Spectral Control for Federated Parameter Efficient Fine Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20914)
*Shiva Raj Pokhrel,Dipsan Bhattarai,Anwar Walid*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TRISHUL is a spectral-control framework for robust federated parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) that addresses non-IID client heterogeneity by using shared frozen multi-head low-rank bases, nuclear norm shrinkage on client-specific updates, and adaptive layer-wise budget allocation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated PEFT enables communication-efficient adaptation of large models on decentralized data, but suffers from fragility under non-IID client heterogeneity. Specifically, in low-rank adaptation (LoRA), different clients learn locally useful but spectrally misaligned update subspaces, causing high-variance aggregation and poor global transfer.

Method: TRISHUL uses: 1) Shared frozen multi-head low-rank bases for algebraically exact aggregation of compact core updates; 2) Nuclear norm proximal shrinkage to suppress client-specific high-rank spectral components before upload; 3) Non-uniform allocation of adaptation heads across layers using a concave water filling budget rule derived from pretrained layer capacity.

Result: TRISHUL improves convergence, stability, and final performance over federated LoRA baselines across vision (CIFAR-100, SVHN) and language (20 Newsgroups, MRQA, GLUE with LLaMA3.2-1B) benchmarks, with greater gains under stronger heterogeneity. It adds negligible computation and no extra per-round communication.

Conclusion: TRISHUL provides an effective spectral-control framework for robust federated PEFT that addresses the challenge of non-IID client heterogeneity while maintaining communication efficiency. The framework operates within the FL no-raw-data-sharing setting but doesn't itself provide formal privacy guarantees.

Abstract: Federated parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) enables communication-efficient adaptation of large pretrained models on decentralized edge data, but it remains fragile under non-IID client heterogeneity. In low-rank adaptation (LoRA), different clients may learn locally useful but spectrally misaligned update subspaces, causing high-variance aggregation and poor global transfer. We propose TRISHUL, a spectral-control framework for robust federated PEFT. TRISHUL follows the FL no-raw-data-sharing setting but does not itself provide formal privacy guarantees. TRISHUL uses shared frozen multi-head low-rank bases to obtain algebraically exact aggregation of compact core updates, applies nuclear norm proximal shrinkage to suppress client-specific high-rank spectral components before upload, and allocates adaptation heads non-uniformly across layers using a concave water filling budget rule derived from pretrained layer capacity. Because shrinkage is performed only on small core matrices, TRISHUL adds negligible computation and no extra per-round communication over the underlying multi-head PEFT protocol. Across vision and language benchmarks, including CIFAR-100, SVHN, 20 Newsgroups, MRQA, and GLUE with LLaMA3.2-1B, TRISHUL improves convergence, stability, and final performance over federated LoRA baselines, with greater gains under stronger heterogeneity.

</details>


### [194] [Best-of-Evidence: Best-of-N Selection under Partial Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20950)
*Cenwei Zhang,Teng Fang,Yuxia Wang,Derek Li,Bryan Dai,Lei You*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Best-of-Evidence (BoE) extends Best-of-N sampling by incorporating partial verification through evidence-based candidate selection when only incomplete or contradictory information is available, showing improvements in medical VQA tasks under specific conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Best-of-N sampling assumes complete candidate evaluation is reliable, but many vision-language tasks only provide partial verification (findings, spans, values, regions, relations). The same claim can appear across candidates with opposing stances, making traditional whole-response verification unreliable.

Method: BoE keeps the BoN candidate pool fixed but introduces a signed candidate-factor graph to represent reusable claims. It allocates a limited budget to evidence actions that can change the final choice, formalizing selection under partial verification with a practical score-based controller. Zero-budget case recovers underlying BoN decision.

Result: Theoretically shows residual evidence capacity limits evidence-driven improvement and shared factor queries achieve O(log K) vs Θ(K) query separation. Experiments on four medical VQA settings demonstrate BoE can improve fixed-pool selection and rescue some BoN failures when evidence is reliable, contrastive, and decision-relevant.

Conclusion: BoE provides a framework for inference-time selection with partial verification, showing practical improvements in medical VQA tasks while revealing limitations in channel quality and candidate generation that prevent universal gains across all scenarios.

Abstract: BoN improves model outputs by sampling several candidates and selecting one with a proxy score, but it assumes that complete candidates can be evaluated reliably. Many vision-language tasks instead provide only partial verification: a finding, span, value, region, or relation may be checkable even when no dependable whole-response verifier exists. Moreover, the same claim may recur across candidates with opposing stances, allowing one observation to support part of the pool and contradict another. We introduce Best-of-Evidence (BoE), an inference-time selection framework that keeps the BoN candidate pool fixed, represents reusable claims with a signed candidate--factor graph, and allocates a limited budget to evidence actions that can change the final choice. BoE formalizes selection under partial verification and provides a practical score-based controller, with the zero-budget case recovering the underlying BoN decision. Theoretically, we show that residual evidence capacity limits any evidence-driven improvement and that shared factor queries can achieve an O(log K) versus Θ(K) query separation in a factor-code model. Common-ledger experiments on four medical VQA settings show that BoE can improve fixed-pool selection and rescue some BoN failures when evidence is reliable, contrastive, and decision-relevant, while also revealing the channel-quality and candidate-generation limits that prevent universal gains.

</details>


### [195] [The Weight of Silence: A Causal Case for Weights Over the Scratchpad in Latent Chess Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20952)
*Ishan S. Kshirsagar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Latent reasoning in language models doesn't function as an actively consulted scratchpad during inference; instead, its main effect is shaping model parameters during training, with RL adding robustness to disruption rather than increasing reliance on thought content.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether the common assumption that latent reasoning functions as an internal scratchpad that models actively consult during inference survives reinforcement learning training. Existing causal analyses are limited to math/logic tasks and single checkpoints, not comparing before vs. after RL stages.

Method: Trained a chess-playing model through a staged latent-reasoning curriculum followed by reinforcement learning. Conducted a six-condition causal intervention suite (substituting/adding noise to latent thought vectors, ablating them, or using exact-zero vectors) on the same model before and after RL.

Result: Legality increased from 48% to 61% after RL while checkmate confabulation was eliminated. Latent thought vectors showed unexpected robustness: substituting/adding noise left performance unchanged, ablation caused only mild degradation, and only exact-zero vectors caused collapse. The key finding was robustness gap - under exact-zero corruption, legality collapsed to 1% pre-RL vs 9% post-RL.

Conclusion: Latent reasoning doesn't function as an actively consulted inference-time scratchpad. Instead, its principal effect is shaping model parameters during training. RL adds robustness to disruption rather than increasing reliance on thought content. The study also demonstrates successful RL gains in chess, unlike previous failures in math/logic settings.

Abstract: Latent, or silent, reasoning lets language models carry out intermediate computation in continuous vector space instead of words, and is widely assumed to function as an internal scratchpad the model actively consults during inference. Whether that assumption survives reinforcement learning has not been tested directly: existing causal analyses of latent reasoning are confined to math and logic tasks, and compare a model's reliance on its thoughts within a single checkpoint, never before and after an RL stage. We train a chess-playing model through a staged latent-reasoning curriculum followed by reinforcement learning, and find legality climbs monotonically to 61% (from a 48% pre-RL baseline) while checkmate confabulation is eliminated entirely. To locate this gain, we run a six-condition causal intervention suite on the same model before and after RL: substituting or adding matched noise to the latent thought vectors leaves performance unchanged, ablating them causes only mild degradation, and only exact-zero vectors cause collapse. This robustness gap is itself the finding: under exact-zero corruption, legality collapses to 1% pre-RL versus 9% post-RL, a gap that survives correction for testing across the full battery; milder conditions trend similarly without independently reaching significance. RL appears to add robustness to disruption, not reliance on thought content. These results push back against the field's default assumption that latent thoughts function as an actively consulted inference-time scratchpad, and instead indicate latent reasoning's principal effect here is shaping the model's parameters during training. We also demonstrate a working RL gain in chess, a domain outside the math and logic settings where multiple groups report the same latent-reasoning-plus-RL recipe failing to improve accuracy over SFT.

</details>


### [196] [ADABORD: a novel AdaBoost approach for ordinal classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21003)
*Rafael Ayllón-Gavilán,Francisco José Martínez-Estudillo,David Guijo-Rubio,César Hervás-Martínez,Pedro A. Gutiérrez*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ADABORD is a new AdaBoost framework for ordinal classification that incorporates ordinal information into base estimators and error functions, outperforming existing methods especially on datasets with 5+ classes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many existing ordinal classification approaches fail to fully leverage ordinal information, treating the problem as nominal classification and losing performance potential. There's a need for methods that properly incorporate the natural order of classes into classification algorithms.

Method: ADABORD adapts the AdaBoost algorithm for ordinal classification by modifying two key components: 1) Using decision trees with an ordinal Gini splitting criterion as base estimators, and 2) Using the absolute ranked probability score as the error function for updating sample weights and classifier weights in the ensemble, which accounts for both ordering and distance between classes.

Result: Extensive comparison against 7 state-of-the-art methods on the TOC-UCO repository (largest OC benchmark) shows ADABORD significantly outperforms competing methods, with particularly strong performance on datasets with five or more classes where ordinal structure is more pronounced. Statistical analysis supports these findings.

Conclusion: ADABORD successfully incorporates ordinal information into the AdaBoost framework, demonstrating superior performance over existing ordinal classification methods, especially for datasets with more classes. The approach makes ordinal classification more effective by properly leveraging class ordering information throughout the boosting process.

Abstract: Ordinal Classification (OC) deals with classification tasks where the classes follow a natural order. Despite the progress in OC, many existing approaches fail to fully leverage the ordinal information, treating the problem as nominal classification and thereby losing performance potential. In this work, ADABORD, an AdaBoost framework specifically designed for ordinal classification problems, is introduced. The ordinal nature of the classes is incorporated into two key components of the well-known AdaBoost algorithm: 1) the base estimator, where decision trees with the ordinal Gini splitting criterion are proposed; 2) the error function used to update sample weights at each stage and the weights of the classifier in the final ensemble model, given by the absolute ranked probability score, a measure that accounts for both the ordering and the distance between classes. ADABORD is extensively compared against seven state-of-the-art methods on the TOC-UCO repository, the largest benchmark collection for OC to date. The experimental results, supported by statistical analysis, show that ADABORD significantly outperforms competing methods, particularly on datasets with five or more classes, where the ordinal structure becomes more pronounced. Source code, along with all experimental protocols, is publicly available to ensure reproducibility and facilitate future research in OC.

</details>


### [197] [Weight-norm Criticality: A Mechanism for Loss Spikes Induced by the Normalization and Weight Decay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21005)
*Xiaolong Li,Zhangchen Zhou,Zhi-Qin John Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Training instability in deep neural networks is not just about learning-rate criticality (Edge of Stability), but also involves weight-norm criticality caused by normalization + weight decay interaction, where excessive decay drives scale-invariant weights toward zero and causes abrupt loss spikes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current explanations of training instability focus primarily on learning-rate criticality, but there's an overlooked factor: weight-norm criticality. The authors argue that practical DNN training involves this additional criticality caused by the interaction between normalization layers (which create scale-invariant components) and weight decay (which shrinks parameter norms).

Method: The paper analyzes the mechanistic interaction between normalization and weight decay in deep neural networks. They propose a theoretical framework showing how weight decay progressively drives scale-invariant weight norms toward zero, while simultaneously increasing loss landscape sharpness. They validate their theory through empirical experiments on networks with scale-invariant components.

Result: The study shows that as weight decay coefficient increases, scale-invariant weight norms approach zero and loss landscape sharpness increases rapidly, destabilizing optimization and causing abrupt loss spikes. This explains why weight penalties improve generalization but cannot be made arbitrarily strong. Empirical validation supports the proposed weight-norm criticality mechanism.

Conclusion: Weight-norm criticality provides a new mechanistic understanding of training instability and loss spikes, complementing the established learning-rate criticality perspective. This explains the practical limitation on weight decay strength and provides testable predictions about training dynamics in networks with normalization layers.

Abstract: Most explanations of training instability focus on \emph{learning-rate criticality}, typically characterized by the Edge of Stability, beyond which optimization becomes unstable. We argue that, in practical deep neural network training, there is an additional and often overlooked \emph{weight-norm criticality}. This criticality is induced by the interaction between normalization (which introduces scale-invariant components) and weight decay (which persistently shrinks parameter norms). As the weight decay coefficient increases, the norms of scale-invariant weights are progressively driven toward zero. Meanwhile, the sharpness of the loss landscape increases rapidly, destabilizing the optimization dynamics and resulting in abrupt loss spikes. This perspective provides a rationale for why weight penalties can improve generalization yet cannot be made arbitrarily strong: excessive decay drives scale-invariant weight norms past a critical boundary and destabilizes training. Our work provides a new mechanistic understanding of loss spikes through the lens of \emph{weight-norm criticality}. Moreover, \emph{weight-norm criticality} yields testable predictions that we validate empirically in networks with scale-invariant components, providing empirical support for the proposed mechanism.

</details>


### [198] [Regularized Optimization on Grassmann Manifold: Theory, Algorithm and Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21039)
*Zhuan Liang,Zheng Zhai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RPMA framework enhances spectral methods by regularizing projection matrix estimation, making it robust to noise and outliers through manifold optimization techniques.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spectral methods for community detection and clustering are sensitive to noise, outliers, and model perturbations, which degrade their performance. Existing spectral projection approaches lack robustness in noisy environments.

Method: Proposes Regularized Projection Matrix Approximation (RPMA) framework that adds regularization to classical spectral projection, formulated as optimization on rank-K projection manifolds (equivalent to Grassmann manifold). Develops Riemannian gradient projection and Cayley-SMW gradient methods for efficient optimization without repeated eigendecompositions.

Result: RPMA improves projection matrix recovery accuracy and consistently outperforms conventional spectral methods for community detection and clustering under noisy conditions, as validated through extensive synthetic and real-world experiments.

Conclusion: RPMA provides a principled framework for robust spectral estimation that yields more stable, sparse, and interpretable projection matrices while maintaining computational efficiency through specialized manifold optimization algorithms.

Abstract: Spectral methods are among the most widely used techniques for community detection, clustering, and graph learning. Their performance, however, critically depends on the accurate estimation of the underlying spectral subspace and can deteriorate substantially in the presence of noise, outliers, or model perturbations. To address this limitation, we propose a Regularized Projection Matrix Approximation (RPMA) framework for robust estimation of rank-$K$ projection matrices. RPMA extends classical spectral projection by incorporating a regularization term, producing projection estimates that are more robust, sparse, and interpretable. We formulate the proposed model as an optimization problem on the manifold of rank-$K$ projection matrices and exploit its geometric equivalence to the Grassmann manifold. Based on this manifold characterization, we derive the first- and second-order optimality conditions, establish the local stability of the regularized leading eigenspace, and characterize the stability of the critical-point landscape under sufficiently small regularization. To efficiently solve the resulting nonconvex optimization problem, we develop a Riemannian gradient projection algorithm with backtracking line search, together with a more efficient Cayley--Sherman--Morrison--Woodbury (Cayley--SMW) gradient method that avoids repeated eigendecompositions. Extensive experiments on both synthetic and real-world datasets demonstrate that RPMA substantially improves the recovery accuracy of projection matrices and consistently outperforms conventional spectral projection methods for community detection and clustering under noisy environments.

</details>


### [199] [Counterfactual Explainability Framework With CycleGAN And Counterfactual-Classifier Alignnment Score for Retinal Disease Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21068)
*Kritanu Chattopadhyay,Sayanjit Singha Roy,Soumya Chatterjee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CounterFundus: A novel CycleGAN-based counterfactual explainability framework that translates pathological fundus images to healthy versions for localizing disease-associated retinal changes, with a new Counterfactual-Classifier Alignment Score to validate spatial consistency with classifier decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing deep learning models for retinal disease detection lack explainability, particularly in linking model decisions to clinically meaningful retinal regions, which hinders clinical adoption. Current post-hoc saliency methods don't provide visually plausible explanations.

Method: 1) CycleGAN-driven counterfactual generation that translates pathological fundus images to estimated healthy versions. 2) EfficientNet-B5-based disease detection integrated with the translation framework. 3) Introduces Counterfactual-Classifier Alignment Score (CCAS) combining Spearman correlation, binary IoU, and pointing accuracy. 4) Uses difference maps between original and counterfactual images to localize disease changes.

Result: 1) Generated counterfactual explanations remain spatially consistent with classifier-relevant retinal evidence across all CCAS dimensions (validated using EigenCAM-aligned evaluation). 2) Ablation studies show CCAS-filtered counterfactual augmentation improves downstream classification performance in fundus images.

Conclusion: CounterFundus establishes a clinically-grounded, explainable AI framework for retinal disease detection that provides visually plausible counterfactual explanations while improving classification performance, addressing the explainability bottleneck for clinical adoption.

Abstract: Automated detection of vision impairing retina-based ocular conditions from fundus images is important for early screening, timely referral and reducing dependency on specialist-only assessment, for which neural network-based deep learning (DL) models have been widely utilized. However, explainability of the DL frameworks remains a major bottleneck for clinical adoption, particularly when model decisions are not linked to retinal regions that are clinically meaningful. To address this issue, this study presents CounterFundus, a novel CycleGAN-driven counterfactual explainability framework, integrating EfficientNet-B5-based retinal disease detection with visually interpretable disease-to-normal fundus image translation. For each pathological image, the counterfactual yielded by the CycleGAN generator represents an estimated healthy counterpart and the resultant difference map is utilized to localize disease-associated retinal changes. Unlike conventional post-hoc saliency methods, CounterFundus provides counterfactual explanations through visually plausible disease-to-normal retinal translation. Thereafter, to quantify the spatial agreement between counterfactual difference maps and classifier saliency, the Counterfactual-Classifier Alignment Score (CCAS) is introduced, embedding Spearman correlation, binary IoU and pointing accuracy into a single assessment protocol. To this end, EigenCAM-aligned evaluation demonstrates that the generated counterfactual explanations remain spatially consistent with classifier-relevant retinal evidence across all CCAS dimensions. Along with that, ablation studies further confirm that CCAS-filtered counterfactual augmentation improves the downstream classification performance in fundus images, establishing CounterFundus as a clinically-grounded, explainable artificially intelligence (XAI) framework for retinal disease detection.

</details>


### [200] [From Evaluation to Optimisation: Hierarchy-Aware Training Signals for CWE Prediction in Python](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21069)
*Muntasir Adnan,Manile Srun,Carlos C. N. Kuhn*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ALPHA benchmark's taxonomy-aware penalty effectively serves as a training signal when delivered via reinforcement learning (GRPO), achieving significant vulnerability prediction improvements in Python code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To validate the theoretical proposition that the ALPHA benchmark's taxonomy-aware penalty could serve as an effective training signal for CWE-level vulnerability prediction in Python, rather than just as an evaluation metric.

Method: Compared three delivery mechanisms: 1) supervised fine-tuning, 2) dual-head classification loss, and 3) reinforcement learning (GRPO) with a dense reward derived from the normalized ALPHA penalty. Evaluated on Qwen2.5-Coder-7B model using Security Hardening and Adversarial Testing (SVEN) dataset.

Result: Supervised approaches regressed below zero-shot baseline under distribution shift, while GRPO succeeded. Best policy reduced cumulative ALPHA penalty by 27.9% under greedy decoding and 25.5% under sampled decoding (statistically significant, p = 0.005), achieving parity with 4.5x larger zero-shot teacher model.

Conclusion: The effectiveness of hierarchical penalty as a training signal depends heavily on the directness of its delivery mechanism, with reinforcement learning (GRPO) proving most effective compared to supervised approaches.

Abstract: The original ALPHA benchmark introduced a taxonomy-aware penalty for evaluating CWE-level vulnerability prediction in Python and proposed that the penalty could theoretically also serve as a training signal. This paper provides that validation. We compare three delivery mechanisms: supervised fine-tuning, a dual-head classification loss, and reinforcement learning with a dense reward derived from the normalised penalty. We find that supervised approaches consistently regress below the zero-shot baseline under distribution shift, while GRPO succeeds. Our best policy reduces the cumulative ALPHA penalty of Qwen2.5-Coder-7B on Security Hardening and Adversarial Testing (SVEN) dataset by 27.9% under greedy decoding, and by 25.5% under sampled decoding(p = 0.005, Welch's t-test), reaching statistical parity with its 4.5x larger zero-shot teacher. We conclude that the value of a hierarchical penalty as a training signal depends largely on the directness of its delivery.

</details>


### [201] [Spectral Transformation for Layer-wise Global Rank Discovery in Federated LoRA for Vision Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21074)
*Hariharan Ramesh,Jyotikrishna Dass*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SpecTraL is a spectral transformation method for federated fine-tuning of Vision Transformers using LoRA that discovers optimal layer-wise global ranks without manual tuning, eliminating aggregation errors while maintaining communication efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LoRA aggregation strategies in federated learning have fundamental limitations: independent averaging introduces cross-term aggregation errors, concatenating heterogeneous client adapters increases download costs and causes reinitialization lag, and other approaches increase server overhead through dense reconstruction or auxiliary model training.

Method: SpecTraL stacks local LoRA modules from clients and performs orthonormal Householder Transformation directly in low-rank latent space, eliminating dense reconstruction. It leverages the Spiked Covariance Model from Random Matrix Theory to analytically separate global consensus signal from non-IID noise, discovering optimal layer-wise global ranks automatically. A padding-aware initialization framework allows clients to incorporate residual LoRA dimensions without re-merging into the base model.

Result: Experiments on federated fine-tuning of ViT-B/16 and ViT-L/16 over DomainNet and NICO++ demonstrate improved accuracy-communication trade-offs, reduced server computation, and elimination of hyperparameter search for rank selection.

Conclusion: SpecTraL resolves fundamental limitations of existing LoRA aggregation strategies in federated learning through a unified design that maintains communication efficiency while improving aggregation accuracy and eliminating manual hyperparameter tuning.

Abstract: Fine-tuning Vision Transformers (ViTs) with low-rank adapters (LoRA) promises better communication efficiency under federated setup, yet existing aggregation strategies face fundamental limitations. Independently averaging these LoRA factors is mathematically inconsistent, introducing cross-term aggregation error. In contrast, approaches that preserve heterogeneous client ranks by concatenating local adapters on the server substantially increase download cost and often require merging global LoRA updates into pretrained weights on the clients, causing reinitialization lag and unstable convergence. Other approaches further increase server-side overhead by reconstructing dense weight updates or training auxiliary models to refine aggregation error. In this work, we propose SpecTraL, spectral transformation for layer-wise global rank discovery, that resolves these challenges within a unified design. SpecTraL stacks local LoRA modules from clients and performs orthonormal Householder Transformation of the stacked adapters directly in the low-rank latent space, eliminating dense reconstruction of the global update and any auxiliary refinement on the server. By leveraging the Spiked Covariance Model from Random Matrix Theory, SpecTraL analytically separates the global consensus signal from non-IID noise, discovering optimal layer-wise global ranks without manual hyperparameter tuning. To match local ranks in subsequent rounds, we introduce a padding-aware initialization framework that lets clients incorporate residual LoRA dimensions without re-merging them into the pre-trained base model. Experiments on federated fine-tuning of ViT-B/16 and ViT-L/16 over DomainNet and NICO++ demonstrate improved accuracy-communication trade-offs, reduced server computation, and elimination of hyperparameter search for rank selection. Our code is available at https://github.com/DASS-Lab-Group/SpecTraL

</details>


### [202] [Nipping the Butterfly Effect in the Bud: Self-Output Fine-Tuning for Autoregressive Weather Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21080)
*Yun-Ye Cai,Hsuan-Tien Lin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Autoregressive DLWP suffers from error growth due to feedback loops between output errors and input distribution shifts; proposed SOFT method uses model's own predictions to calibrate biased input distributions at first step, achieving SOTA long-horizon weather forecasting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon weather forecasting is challenging, and autoregressive DLWP has emerged as the primary paradigm but suffers from rapidly growing prediction errors over long forecasting horizons. The paper aims to understand and mitigate this error growth phenomenon.

Method: The authors analyze error growth theoretically and empirically, revealing it's driven by feedback loops between output errors and input distribution shifts. They propose Self-Output Fine-Tuning (SOFT), a plug-and-play strategy that uses the model's own one-step predictions to calibrate biased input distributions encountered at the first autoregressive step.

Result: Extensive experiments show that SOFT achieves state-of-the-art performance on long-horizon forecasting tasks, substantially reduces both prediction errors and distributional discrepancy.
