The algorithm then truncates the trajectory and branches a new generation, maximally reusing the valid prefix. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that TTEL establishes strictly dominating Pareto frontiers across sequential reasoning domains, measured by pass-at-k vs. generated-token cost. With Qwen3-8B on LiveCodeBench, TTEL attains a pass@64 of 71.0% while generating approximately half as many tokens as independent sampling (360.4k vs. 735.0k). Generalizing to math benchmarks AIME-2025 and HMMT-2025, TTEL cleanly outperforms competing test-time baselines across both Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-4B-Thinking-2507.

</details>


### [228] [Error Certificates for KV-Cache Eviction via Randomized Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21475)
*Peng Xie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Randomized KV-cache eviction with survey-sampling techniques provides reliable error certificates that can distinguish between cache-induced failures and inherent model failures, enabling better attribution and recomputation scheduling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deterministic KV-cache eviction methods that keep top-k tokens based on importance scores have a fundamental flaw: they cannot detect when evicted values are altered in ways that cause arbitrary attention-output errors while leaving retained values unchanged, making serving-time error estimation impossible.

Method: Proposed randomized eviction using Poisson-sampling with known inclusion probabilities, followed by Hájek correction via logit offset inside softmax, and survey-sampling variance estimation over the retained set to create per-step error certificates.

Result: Empirical coverage of 0.97 for error certificates at no accuracy cost; certificate separates cache-induced from inherent failures (AUC 0.73-0.75 vs. 0.47-0.54 for output confidence); better recomputation scheduling than random or confidence gating; but found that output log-probability predicts failure better than certificates, and certificate-gated budget escalation adds nothing.

Conclusion: Randomized eviction enables reliable error attribution (distinguishing cache-induced from inherent failures) rather than prediction, and provides practical benefits for failure attribution and recomputation scheduling despite not improving prediction capabilities.

Abstract: Deterministic KV-cache eviction keeps the top-$k$ tokens under an importance score and deletes the rest. We prove that this design cannot know what it destroyed: evicted values can be altered so that everything the serving system retains is unchanged while the true attention-output error grows arbitrarily, so no serving-time estimator of that error is consistent. Randomized eviction restores identifiability. With a Poisson-sampled tail at known inclusion probabilities, one logit offset performs the Hájek correction inside the softmax, and a survey-sampling variance estimator over the retained set becomes a per-step error certificate with 0.97 empirical coverage at no accuracy cost. On real workloads we pre-registered seven claims and lost three: question-aware eviction at 25--50\% budgets is nearly free; output log-probability predicts failure better than the certificate; certificate-gated budget escalation adds nothing. What survives is attribution: the certificate separates cache-induced from inherent failures (AUC 0.73--0.75, against 0.47--0.54 for output confidence) and schedules recomputation better than random or confidence gating. Randomization buys attribution, not prediction.

</details>


### [229] [Finite-Sample Coverage Audits for High-Recall Candidate Generation: Certification and Learning-Theoretic Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21480)
*Martin Anthony,Kaveh Salehzadeh Nobari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper studies how many audit labels are needed to certify that an empirical pipeline's initial high-recall stage misses few relevant items, showing excluded-pool auditing is minimax optimal and providing exact finite-sample certification tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In empirical pipelines, an initial high-recall stage filters items for later processing, but any relevant items it misses are permanently lost. There's a need to certify with statistical validity that this missed relevant mass is small, which requires determining the label complexity of this certification problem.

Method: The authors use finite-sample validity analysis to characterize the label complexity of missed-mass certification. They prove theoretical bounds showing that auditing must sample from the excluded pool (where unrecovered relevant items lie). They then develop exact finite-sample certification tools using binomial and hypergeometric inversion rather than asymptotic approximations.

Result: Key results: (1) No procedure using only labels from inside the candidate set can certify non-trivial bounds on missed mass; (2) Any valid audit certifying fewer than m missed relevant items with high probability when none are present must inspect ~N₀/m excluded-pool labels; (3) Excluded-pool auditing is minimax rate-optimal; (4) Developed toolkit certifies missed mass, converts to recall via two-pool design, handles nested candidate generators, and produces stress-test certificates.

Conclusion: Excluded-pool auditing is not merely convenient but minimax optimal for missed-mass certification in zero-miss regimes. The finite-sample certification framework provides valid guarantees under the discipline that candidate generators and audit rules are fixed before examining certification labels, enabling selection of least burdensome generators meeting missed-mass targets.

Abstract: An initial high-recall stage in an empirical pipeline decides which items pass to later review, labelling, or modelling, and relevant items it misses are lost to every subsequent stage. We study how many audit labels are needed to certify, with finite-sample validity, that this missed relevant mass is small, and our main results characterise the label complexity of this problem. We first show that no procedure using only labels from inside the candidate set can certify any non-trivial bound on the missed mass: the audit must sample the excluded pool, the only region where unrecovered relevant items can lie. We then prove a matching finite-corpus lower bound. Any valid audit that certifies fewer than $m$ missed relevant items with high probability when none are present, even if adaptive and permitted to label the entire included pool, must inspect on the order of $N_0/m$ excluded-pool labels. Excluded-pool auditing is therefore minimax rate-optimal, not merely convenient, for missed-mass certification in the zero-miss regime. Building on this characterisation, we develop an exact finite-sample toolkit, using binomial and hypergeometric inversion rather than asymptotic approximation, that certifies missed mass, converts it to recall through a two-pool design, certifies pre-specified families of nested candidate generators simultaneously, and produces stress-test certificates against declared perturbation mechanisms. These certificates can be paired with observable review burden to select the least burdensome pre-specified candidate generator meeting a missed-mass target. Every guarantee holds under one discipline: the candidate generator, or the pre-specified family from which it is selected, and the audit rule are fixed before the certification labels are examined.

</details>


### [230] [Windowed-MTP: Removing the Full-Context Draft-KV Tax at Million-Token Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21535)
*Alagappan Valliappan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes Windowed-MTP: a training-free sliding window + attention sink applied only to draft attention in speculative decoding, bounding KV cache reads to constant size for million-token contexts while preserving full-attention verification and output quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: At million-token contexts, current Multi-Token-Prediction (MTP) draft heads in speculative decoding become expensive because they perform full attention over the entire KV cache at each draft step, causing read costs to grow linearly with context. This negates the benefits of speculative decoding precisely where it's most valuable (long contexts), especially with hybrid/linear-attention targets where cheaper verification exposes the draft's full-attention overhead.

Method: Apply a StreamingLLM-style sliding window plus attention sink to the draft's attention only (Windowed-MTP), leaving the target's full-attention verification intact. This training-free approach bounds the draft's KV working set to a constant size by limiting attention to recent tokens plus attention sinks, dropping ~99% of KV entries at 1M tokens. The method uses a compact ring buffer to reclaim unread draft KV (7.7-11% of total KV at 1M).

Result: Windowed-MTP reduces per-decode-step cost by 28-44% at 1M context across three architecture families (Qwen GDN-MoE 35B/122B and Mamba2-hybrid NoPE 120B) on single GPU in SGLang. Per-token latency improves proportionally, with additional improvements where windowing also increases acceptance rates. The approach preserves the target's verified output distribution since full-attention target verification remains intact.

Conclusion: Windowed-MTP provides an effective, training-free solution to the scalability problem of MTP draft heads in speculative decoding at long contexts. By bounding draft KV reads to constant size while maintaining full-attention verification, it preserves output quality while significantly improving decoding efficiency, making speculative decoding practical for million-token contexts where it was previously ineffective.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates autoregressive generation by having a cheap draft propose tokens that a target verifies in parallel. Frontier models increasingly ship a built-in Multi-Token-Prediction (MTP/NEXTN) draft head under the assumption that the draft is negligibly cheap. At million-token context this breaks: an MTP draft head typically runs full attention over the entire KV cache at every draft step, so its read grows linearly with context and comes to dominate the draft cost -- precisely where speculation is most valuable. The effect compounds with draft length (a deep native draft can turn net-negative, slower than no speculation) and sharpens under hybrid/linear-attention targets, where cheaper verification leaves the draft's full-attention read exposed. We apply a StreamingLLM-style sliding window plus attention sink to the draft's attention only (Windowed-MTP), leaving full-attention verification intact. It is training-free, drop-in, and lossless by construction: the full-attention target still decides every accepted token, so windowing changes only which tokens are proposed, never which are accepted. It bounds the draft's KV working set to a constant, dropping ~99% of KV entries at 1M. Across three architecture families (Qwen GDN-MoE 35B/122B and a Mamba2-hybrid NoPE 120B) at 1M context on a single GPU in SGLang, windowing cuts the per-decode-step cost over the shipping native MTP draft by +28% to +44%, an input-invariant margin that widens with context. Since per-token latency is this cost divided by acceptance length, at matched acceptance end-to-end decode latency improves by the same amount, and more where windowing also lifts acceptance, while preserving the target's verified output distribution. Finally, the unread draft KV -- 7.7-11% of total KV at 1M -- is reclaimed via a compact ring buffer at no acceptance or quality cost.

</details>


### [231] [Zero-Flow Two-Sample Tests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21542)
*Yakun Wang,Leyang Wang,Song Liu,Taiji Suzuki*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A new two-sample test called zero-flow two-sample test (ZF2ST) that uses zero-flow discrepancy to detect distributional differences by learning local misalignment patterns between samples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the need for powerful two-sample tests that can detect structured distributional differences while maintaining valid statistical calibration, especially when using flexible models like neural networks.

Method: The method proposes zero-flow discrepancy (ZFD) based on zero-flow criterion, then develops ZF2ST which learns how samples from two distributions are locally misaligned. It uses witness learning separated from hypothesis evaluation, with both regression-based and power-maximized approaches for learning the witness.

Result: The paper proves the validity of ZFD and demonstrates through experiments on synthetic and image datasets that ZF2ST achieves strong testing power for structured distributional changes while maintaining well-calibrated type-I error.

Conclusion: ZF2ST provides a statistically valid and powerful approach to two-sample testing that leverages flexible neural networks while ensuring proper calibration, making it suitable for detecting structured distributional differences in practice.

Abstract: We propose a new approach to two-sample testing for deciding whether two sets of samples are drawn from the same distribution. The test is built on a statistical discrepancy based on the zero-flow criterion, termed zero-flow discrepancy (ZFD). We prove the validity of ZFD and propose a practical testing procedure, termed the zero-flow two-sample test (ZF2ST). The key idea is to learn how samples from the two distributions are locally misaligned and use the resulting directional pattern as evidence of distributional difference. By separating witness learning from hypothesis evaluation, ZF2ST can use flexible neural networks while maintaining valid statistical calibration. We develop both regression-based and power-maximized approaches for learning the witness. Experiments on synthetic and image datasets demonstrate that ZF2ST can achieve strong testing power for structured distributional changes while maintaining well-calibrated type-I error.

</details>


### [232] [X$^3$-OPD: Distilling Reasoning into Large Audio-Language Models via On-Policy Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21550)
*Dongjie Fu,Di Cao,Xize Cheng,Zihan Zhang,Wenxu Jia,Yifu Chen,Shengpeng Ji,Yu Zhang,Tao Jin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: X³-OPD: A cross-modal on-policy distillation framework that transfers reasoning capabilities from text-based LLMs to audio-language models using a three-tier symmetric corpus covering text-to-speech reasoning, audio-event reasoning, and spoken-dialogue reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Audio-language models lag behind text-based LLMs in deep logical reasoning due to scarcity of high-quality audio reasoning data. There's a need to bridge this gap by transferring reasoning capabilities from powerful text models to audio models.

Method: Proposes X³-OPD (cross-modal on-policy distillation) where audio-language student generates reasoning trajectories based on acoustic perception while text teacher provides token-level guidance using matched textual inputs and verified answers. Uses three-tier symmetric corpus: text reasoning in speech, audio-event reasoning in complex acoustic scenes, and spoken-dialogue reasoning with paralinguistic cues.

Result: Experiments on MMSU, MMAU, BIG Bench Audio, and MMAR demonstrate X³-OPD substantially improves audio-grounded reasoning and chain-of-thought quality while largely preserving model's existing capabilities under domain shift.

Conclusion: The framework successfully extends cross-modal distillation beyond textually recoverable content to reasoning grounded in non-linguistic events, prosody, and conversational context, effectively transferring reasoning capabilities from text to audio models.

Abstract: While large audio-language models have achieved remarkable progress in auditory perception, they still lag behind text-based large language models in deep logical reasoning, primarily due to the scarcity of high-quality audio reasoning data. To bridge this gap, we propose X$^3$-OPD, a cross-modal on-policy distillation framework that transfers reasoning capabilities from a powerful text teacher to an audio-language student. During training, the student generates reasoning trajectories conditioned on its own acoustic perception, while the teacher provides token-level guidance using matched textual inputs and verified answers. We further construct a three-tier symmetric corpus covering textual reasoning rendered into speech, audio-event reasoning grounded in complex acoustic scenes, and spoken-dialogue reasoning involving paralinguistic cues. This design extends cross-modal distillation beyond textually recoverable content to reasoning grounded in non-linguistic events, prosody, and conversational context. Experiments on MMSU, MMAU, BIG Bench Audio, and MMAR demonstrate that X$^3$-OPD substantially improves audio-grounded reasoning and chain-of-thought quality while largely preserving the model's existing capabilities under domain shift.

</details>


### [233] [Graph Learning on Ensembles of Cyclic Peptides: An Investigation of Molecular Ensemble Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21561)
*Aaron Feller,Kris Deibler,Maxim Secor*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EnsembleEGNN: a molecular foundation model that encodes conformational ensembles (not just single conformations) for better cyclic peptide property prediction, outperforming sequence-only baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most molecular property prediction methods use a single representative conformation, but molecules often exist as conformational ensembles in solution. This ignores important thermodynamic information that could improve prediction accuracy.

Method: 1. Uses Equivariant Graph Neural Network (EGNN) layers to encode each conformer in an ensemble
2. Pools conformer representations using a Set Attention Block
3. Pretrains on CREMP cyclic peptide ensemble dataset with multi-task self-supervised objectives: masked token recovery, noisy-coordinate reconstruction, and pairwise distance reconstruction

Result: Training from scratch fails (R²=0.005), but pretrained EnsembleEGNN achieves R²=0.477 and Pearson r=0.699, outperforming sequence-only BERT baseline (R²=0.439, r=0.667). End-to-end co-training with BERT encoder improves further to R²=0.538 and r=0.737.

Conclusion: Encoding conformational ensembles into a single thermodynamically informed embedding significantly improves cyclic-peptide property prediction, demonstrating the value of incorporating conformational ensemble information beyond single conformations.

Abstract: Molecular property prediction from structure often uses a single representative conformation, even though many molecules exist as conformational ensembles in solution. We introduce EnsembleEGNN, a molecular ensemble foundation model that encodes an ensemble by first encoding each conformer with shared Equivariant Graph Neural Network (EGNN) layers, then pooling the resulting conformer representations with a Set Attention Block. We pretrain the model on CREMP, a cyclic peptide ensemble dataset, using a multi-task self-supervised objective combining masked token recovery, noisy-coordinate reconstruction, and pairwise distance reconstruction. On the CREMP-CycPeptMPDB dataset, training EnsembleEGNN from scratch fails entirely ($R^2=0.005$). However, the pretrained model reaches $R^2=0.477$ and Pearson $r=0.699$, outperforming the sequence-only BERT baseline ($R^2=0.439$, Pearson $r=0.667$). When EnsembleEGNN is co-trained end-to-end with the BERT sequence encoder, the hybrid model improves further to $R^2=0.538$ and Pearson $r=0.737$. These results demonstrate that encoding conformational ensembles into a single thermodynamically informed embedding improves cyclic-peptide property prediction.

</details>


### [234] [Beyond Sufficiency: Time Series Explanation with Counterfactual Necessity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21573)
*Hongnan Ma,Yiwei Shi,Mengyue Yang,Weiru Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TimePNS is a necessity-aware explanation framework for time-series classifiers that identifies not only sufficient but also necessary subsequences for model predictions using counterfactual interventions on temporal factors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing explanation methods for time-series classifiers focus on sufficiency (identifying subsequences that preserve predictions) but can assign high importance to spurious subsequences that support predictions without being essential to the model's decision. There's a need for methods that also assess necessity of identified subsequences.

Method: Two-stage design: Stage I learns an identifiable causal generative process with a sufficiency-oriented explanation mask. Stage II performs counterfactual interventions on temporal factors to derive necessity signals, which supervise a temporal gate that refines initial explanations by suppressing non-essential components and emphasizing counterfactually necessary ones. Inspired by Pearl's counterfactual notion of necessity.

Result: Experiments on synthetic and real-world time-series benchmarks show that TimePNS more accurately identifies decision-critical subsequences and consistently improves sufficiency-necessity trade-offs over strong baselines.

Conclusion: TimePNS provides a novel framework for necessity-aware time-series explanation that better identifies truly essential subsequences for model predictions, addressing limitations of purely sufficiency-oriented methods.

Abstract: Faithful explanations of time-series classifiers should identify subsequences that are not only sufficient to preserve a black-box model's prediction, but also necessary for maintaining it. However, existing sufficiency-oriented methods can assign high importance to spurious subsequences that support the prediction without being essential to the model's decision. We introduce \textbf{TimePNS}, a necessity-aware framework for time-series explanation. Inspired by Pearl's counterfactual notion of necessity, TimePNS assesses whether a temporal factor is necessary by intervening on it and measuring whether the original prediction is disrupted. The framework adopts a two-stage design. Stage I learns an identifiable causal generative process together with a sufficiency-oriented explanation mask. Stage II performs counterfactual interventions on temporal factors to derive necessity signals, which supervise a temporal gate that refines the initial explanation by suppressing non-essential components and emphasizing counterfactually necessary ones. Experiments on synthetic and real-world time-series benchmarks show that TimePNS more accurately identifies decision-critical subsequences and consistently improves sufficiency-necessity trade-offs over strong baselines.

</details>


### [235] [Expanding Flow Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21585)
*Sophia Tang,Pranam Chatterjee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EFlows introduce flows between distributions of increasing dimensionality using expanding interpolants, enabling variable-size generation across continuous and discrete spaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing flow-based generative models are constrained to fixed dimensions or fixed sequence lengths, limiting their applicability to variable-size generation tasks like variable-length sequences or graphs.

Method: The authors propose Expanding Generative Flows (EFlows) with expanding interpolants that grow state dimensionality by augmenting with conditional noise. They introduce Expanding Flow Maps (EFMs) that factor each step into two learnable operations: expand (adds new coordinates/tokens) and transport (pushes expanded state forward).

Result: The framework enables variable-size generation in both continuous and discrete modalities, with existing fixed-size flows becoming a special case when the expand operator is identity.

Conclusion: EFlows and EFMs provide a principled framework for learning controllable generation where output size is itself a learned, controllable parameter, extending flow-based models to variable-dimensionality problems.

Abstract: Flow-based generative models have enabled remarkable progress in fast and controllable generation across continuous and discrete state spaces, yet existing parameterizations are constrained to fixed dimensions or fixed sequence lengths. Here, we introduce Expanding Generative Flows (EFlows), which define flows between distributions of increasing dimensionality along an expanding interpolant that grows the state by augmenting it with conditional noise. Building on this construction, we propose Expanding Flow Maps (EFMs), a new class of flow maps that distill the expanding interpolant into efficient few-step generative models. Each EFM factors the map between any two timesteps into two learnable operations: an expand operator, which augments the state space with new coordinates or tokens conditioned on the current state, and a transport map, which pushes the expanded state forward along the interpolant. Composing these operators yields a single map that jointly expands and denoises the state, recovering existing fixed-canvas flows and flow maps as the special case in which the expand operator is the identity. We further extend the framework to the discrete simplex, enabling variable-size graph generation and variable-length sequence generation. Across both continuous and discrete modalities, we establish EFlows and EFMs as a principled framework for settings in which output size is itself a learned, controllable degree of freedom.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [236] [pAI-Econ-claude: A Gated Human-in-the-Loop Multi-Agent Architecture for AI-Assisted Economic Theory Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21268)
*Chen Zhu,Xiaolu Wang,Weilong Zhang*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: A gated human-in-the-loop multi-agent architecture called pAI-Econ-claude improves reliability in AI-assisted economic theory development by using specialized gates for failure diagnosis and human checkpoints for irreversible decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based agents in social sciences often produce outputs without cheap, machine-readable correctness signals, creating reliability problems for multi-agent systems when no component can certify final results.

Method: Developed pAI-Econ-claude: a gated human-in-the-loop architecture with shared workspace of inspectable intermediate records, specialized gates that diagnose failure modes and recommend loopbacks, and human checkpoints retaining authority over costly-to-reverse decisions.

Result: Evaluated on five economic theory tasks against ungated baseline: blinded evaluators preferred gated architecture in 4/5 tasks. Mean failure severity fell from 1.58 to 1.16, usefulness rose from 2.60 to 3.10. Largest gains from reality checks and proof reviews.

Conclusion: Gated oversight improves auditability of AI-assisted economic theory without substituting formal verification. Allocation of irreversible human judgment is more informative design variable than pure agent autonomy.

Abstract: In many social-science research tasks, such as economics, LLM-based agents must produce outputs for which no cheap, task-complete, machine-readable correctness signal exists. This creates a distinctive reliability problem for multi-agent systems: how should generation, critique, coordination, and human judgment be organized when no component can certify the final result? We address this problem through pAI-Econ-claude, a gated, human-in-the-loop multi-agent architecture for AI-assisted economic theory development. Agents coordinate through a shared workspace of inspectable intermediate records; specialized gates diagnose targeted failure modes and recommend loopbacks without certifying correctness; and human checkpoints retain authority over decisions that are costly to reverse. We evaluate the architecture on five matched economic-theory tasks against an ungated baseline. Two evaluators blinded to configuration agreed on all five pairwise rankings, preferring the gated architecture in four tasks and the baseline in one. Mean failure severity fell from 1.58 to 1.16, while overall usefulness rose from 2.60 to 3.10. The largest observed gain occurred when a reality check rejected a false market-structure premise and a proof review prompted revision of a false welfare claim. The negative case shows that scaffolding can also compress an economically important mechanism too aggressively. The results support a bounded claim: gated oversight improves the auditability of AI-assisted economic theory without substituting for formal verification, and the allocation of irreversible human judgment is a more informative design variable than pure agent autonomy. The workflow is publicly available at https://github.com/maxwell2732/pAI-Econ-claude.

</details>


### [237] [FedAgentKE: Federated Semantic Knowledge Evolution for Heterogeneous Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21361)
*Weihao Li,Jun Bai,Ziyang Song*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: FedAgentKE is a federated learning framework that enables different LLM-based agent frameworks to collaboratively evolve shared reasoning knowledge without sharing raw data, improving cross-framework and cross-task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-based agent frameworks operate in isolation, with fragmented local experiences and limited cross-framework knowledge transfer. Local memory systems only improve individual agents but don't enable collaborative reasoning evolution across different agent frameworks.

Method: FedAgentKE uses federated semantic knowledge evolution where distributed agent frameworks collaboratively evolve transferable reasoning abstractions through iterative semantic knowledge distillation, aggregation, and adaptation without sharing raw reasoning trajectories.

Result: Experiments show consistent improvements in both cross-framework and cross-task settings, demonstrating the effectiveness of federated semantic knowledge evolution for collaborative agent ecosystems.

Conclusion: FedAgentKE presents a promising approach for building collaborative agent ecosystems where heterogeneous agents can evolve shared reasoning knowledge while preserving privacy and framework independence, showing potential for future collaborative AI systems.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based agents increasingly rely on reasoning, tool use, and iterative execution, yet existing agent frameworks still operate largely in isolation. While recent memory-based agent systems improve individual agents through local retrieval and workflow reuse, local experiences remain fragmented across isolated agent frameworks, limiting cross-framework knowledge transfer and collaborative reasoning evolution. We propose FedAgentKE, a lightweight framework for Federated Semantic Knowledge Evolution across heterogeneous agents. FedAgentKE enables distributed agent frameworks to collaboratively evolve transferable reasoning abstractions through iterative semantic knowledge distillation, aggregation, and adaptation without sharing raw reasoning trajectories. Experiments demonstrate consistent improvements under both cross-framework and cross-task settings, highlighting the potential of federated semantic knowledge evolution for future collaborative agent ecosystems.

</details>
